Android app developers will soon have to share their address publicly
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
17:06:24 UTC
02:06:24 KST

Independent Android app developers are worried about their privacy after Google announced that from next year many of them will be required to share a phone number and address publicly. The policy change is intended to meet European Union regulations under the Digital Services Act, but Google says contact details will be made public worldwide.
Android app developers will soon have to share their address publicly
The change is to meet EU regulations, but Apple gives its developers more privacy.
Developers have until February 1st 2027 to declare whether or not they’re a “trader” under UK and European law. Broadly speaking, Google says the definition applies if you earn revenue from your apps, especially if that revenue is regular or at high volume. Developers are exempt if their apps are only a hobby or non-commercial, or if they simply don’t publish them in the UK or Europe.
If they self-declare as a trader, developers’ contact details will be shared publicly starting March 1st, including a phone number and address. Google doesn’t accept PO boxes for developer payment profile addresses, meaning many independent developers without an office will seemingly have no choice but to share their home address online. One independent developer told The Elec they were considering renting a private office solely to comply with the new rules. We’ve asked Google to confirm whether developers will be permitted to publish a PO box address, and why details are being shared globally, and will update this article when we hear back.
Apple is subject to the same regulations, but is handling them differently to Google. It will only display developer addresses within the relevant European countries, rather than publishing them worldwide. It’s also allowing developers to supply PO box addresses, so long as they provide Apple with a receipt or bill that links the PO box to their regular address.

====================================================================================================

Federal judge blocks the FCC from reviewing ABC’s licenses early
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
16:38:00 UTC
01:38:00 KST

US District Judge Loren AliKhan has blocked the Federal Communications Commission from conducting an early review of ABC’s broadcast licenses in a preliminary injunction, and rejected its efforts to dismiss ABC’s First Amendment lawsuit. ABC has accused the FCC of violating its free speech rights and claims the agency “waged a retaliatory campaign” against the network over content it broadcast. In her ruling the judge noted that the network was “likely to succeed on their retaliation claim.”
Federal judge blocks the FCC from reviewing ABC’s licenses early
The judge has preliminarily sided with ABC, rejecting the FCC’s efforts to dismiss the case.
Although Disney, ABC’s parent company, wasn’t set to renew its broadcast licenses until 2028, last April the FCC ordered the network to file for an early license renewal claiming it was part of an investigation into Disney’s policies on diversity, equity, and inclusivity. The FCC’s move came a day after President Donald Trump demanded ABC fire late-night talk show host Jimmy Kimmel following a skit about Melania Trump.
In response to the FCC’s order, Disney and ABC sued the agency in August and accused the FCC of “punishing ABC for its speech” by threatening its broadcast licenses. The preliminary injunction issued by the judge on Wednesday prohibits the FCC from taking any further action in the early review of ABC’s broadcast licenses and noted that the network had shown that the court’s intervention in the agency’s actions was in the public interest.
”No one doubts that the Commission may enforce the law in the manner it sees fit,” the judge wrote. “But in doing so, Defendants may not retaliate against or punish Plaintiffs and other broadcast license holders for disfavored speech.”

As Anthropic IPOs, Grand Theft Auto VI proves why efficiency isn’t a virtue
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
16:00:00 UTC
01:00:00 KST

It should have been impossible for “Grand Theft Auto VI” (GTA VI) to live up to the hype. Fans waited 13 years for the newest installment in the crime-adventure franchise, and Rockstar Games exceeded even those inconceivable expectations when the game finally came out last week.
GTA VI launched as the highest-rated game of all time on Metacritic and made $3.79 billion on release day to mark what could be the biggest entertainment event ever – more massive than “Avengers: Endgame” ($2.9 billion) or Taylor Swift’s Eras tour ($2 billion).
The 13-year wait certainly fueled the frenzy, and sales, around GTA VI, but some fans craved GTA VI because of what it represents in the emerging era of AI.
Even the biggest workaholics among us – startup founders – scheduled time off to play the game, including Spiry co-founder Dima Beseda, who told TechCrunch, “I reshuffled my workload to free up time around the weekends and even booked vitamin IV drips to keep my energy up.”
Yet some players felt a sense of anticipatory nostalgia as they wiped away a layer of dust on their Xbox to play the game they’d been looking forward to since they were kids.
“We do have a view that GTA is likely to be the last game of its kind to be made this way,” Ben Feder, managing partner at TIRTA Ventures and former CEO of Rockstar parent company Take-Two Interactive, told TechCrunch.
That’s because GTA VI was created entirely without generative AI.
“We’re kind of at this technological moment where this way of developing has sort of reached its natural frontier,” Feder said. “I don’t think you can say that games like this will always be around.”
As millions of people immersed themselves in Vice City, the tech industry witnessed another long-awaited moment: Anthropic became the first major AI lab to go public. At Nasdaq HQ, Anthropic executives gathered to celebrate the Claude maker’s $81.3 billion IPO. At virtually the same moment that Anthropic broke the record for the largest IPO ever, GTA VI was also breaking records with the highest-rated and best-selling game launch of all time.
But when the Anthropic team rang the bell on Thursday, the sound also signaled the end of an era in video game development, and perhaps even in the arts at large.
The AI industry operates with efficiency as its north star. The promise of this technology is that we will be able to automate so much work that we can expand the capabilities of humanity, avoiding needless time spent tinkering with ultimately fruitless ideas. Instead, a prompt goes into an LLM, and a response is spat out.
This technological revolution exploded into the world with some soul-crushing side effects: children are inundated with mind-numbing slop on YouTube, lawyers cite nonexistent cases in court, and tech companies like Meta lay off tens of thousands of people as part of a corporate-branded “Year of Efficiency.” But against the backdrop of the AI industry’s first major IPO, Rockstar has unveiled a game that about 5,000 people labored over for more than a decade.
The final product is breathtaking, looking so lifelike that it can be hard to distinguish gameplay footage from real life. These sprawling open-world games are usually made possible by procedurally generating new landscapes and objects, but Rockstar has taken the time to painstakingly craft every detail of the world, even developing 30,000 different nonplayable characters with unique appearances and personalities.
The video game industry is already grappling with the threat of AI. Google’s Project Genie, which launched earlier this year, allows users to create and wander around 3D worlds generated from prompts to an LLM. The stock prices of several video game companies faltered around the time of Project Genie’s release, perhaps because of investors’ uncertainty about the future relevance of video game developers.
“The notion that somehow new tools would allow an individual to push a button and generate a hit, market a hit, and bring it to many millions of consumers around the world, it’s a laughable notion,” Take-Two Interactive CEO Strauss Zelnick said at the time. “It’s just never been the case with entertainment.”
Zelnick’s fellow tech CEOs see different future, though. On a Meta earnings call this year, CEO Mark Zuckerberg promised that, “soon, you’ll have an AI that understands you and [can] even generate great personalized content for you.”
Elon Musk even went as far as proposing that a game like GTA VI could be made with AI.
“You won’t even have to ask,” Musk wrote on X. “AI will figure what video game you’d like best.”
This is the paradox of “Grandthropic” week. GTA VI is so impressively vast and captivating because its developers could make mistakes, experiment, and rework ideas, even if that meant delaying the game (twice). GTA VI was incubated with the exact inefficiencies that investors hope AI companies like Anthropic can help eradicate.
“Plenty of fantastic art — video games and elsewhere — almost by definition has to be created slowly, and there’s something to having that kind of inefficiency around it,” Feder said. “Creative output requires deletion, right? Getting to kind of a masterpiece oftentimes requires an enormous amount of deletion. And when you’re talking about a game of the kind of complexity we’re talking about, that kind of deletion ends up being very expensive, and it is by definition not efficient.”
GTA VI reeks of its own humanity – every small detail is loaded with intention, but it’s this impressive precision that keeps the world abuzz, triggering the kind of monocultural moment that has become increasingly rare in a media landscape that revolves around highly personalized social media algorithms.
The game forces the player to slow down, which is either frustrating or genius, depending on your sense of humor. People waited more than three presidential terms for the next installment in a high-octane action franchise, only to download a 212 GB behemoth of a game in which your earliest missions involve driving around doing errands and putting up drywall to make your girlfriend happy. Don’t worry — there are still car chases and shootouts — but in a moment when AI feels suffocating, GTA VI is such an artistic achievement that it is reaching audiences that don’t even like violent video games.
The ultimate fantasy of GTA VI is no longer to role-play as a criminal — the fantasy is just taking your time to relish the mundane and explore an expansive world that developers were given the time and space to perfect.

New York Bans AI Toys For 5 Years
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
16:00:00 UTC
01:00:00 KST

New York Bans AI Toys For 5 Years 11
			
		 	
				New York Gov. Kathy Hochul signed legislation Tuesday banning the manufacture, exchange, distribution and sale of toys powered by AI chatbots for five years. "The law will take effect 90 days after signing, meaning the restrictions will not be in place during this year's holiday shopping season," reports FOX Chattanooga. "In the meantime, Hochul is encouraging parents and caregivers to consider potential safety and privacy risks before purchasing AI-powered toys for children." From the report:  The legislation, S.9408A/A.11144B, applies to toys with embedded or integrated AI chatbots, including talking stuffed animals, dolls and robots that use the internet to hold conversations with children. [...] Under the new law, violators could face civil penalties of up to $15,000 per day for each violation, with enforcement handled by the New York Attorney General's Office. State Sen. Andrew Gounardes said the five-year moratorium will provide time to study the technology's impact on children and develop additional safety protections.

GTA VI developers vote against unionization
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
15:55:24 UTC
00:55:24 KST

Workers at Grand Theft Auto VI developer Rockstar Games won’t unionize, after the vote for a formal recognition agreement failed, with only 25 percent of votes in favor of unionization. The Independent Workers Union of Great Britain (IWGB) said that Rockstar workers in the union will continue organizing even without formal recognition.
GTA VI developers vote against unionization
Workers voted 75 percent against the IWGB recognition agreement.
Voting ran from November 12th through 26th, covering the launch of GTA VI. If successful, the vote would have established a formal framework for collective negotiation with the studio over pay, overtime, bonuses, leave, and pensions, along with a right to consultation over future changes including AI implementation and layoffs. Without it, Rockstar won’t have an obligation to meet union representatives, though workers and union members can still lobby for change informally.
“For anyone at Rockstar who believes in a fairer workplace, a fairer sector and a fairer society - this isn’t a sign to retreat, it’s a sign to double down on what we stand for,” IWGB said in a statement sent to The Verge. “Hundreds of our coworkers voted in favour of our union at Rockstar, yet sadly we recognise we have more work to do to build understanding and win the trust of more of our colleagues. We’re ready to put in the effort to make that happen.”
When Rockstar fired 31 IWGB members last year, the union accused it of “the most blatant and ruthless act of union busting in the history of the games industry.” That case has gone to a UK employment tribunal, with a judgment still pending.
“We are extraordinarily proud of our team, the culture we’ve built together and the launch of Grand Theft Auto VI,” Rockstar head of publishing Jennifer Kolbe told Bloomberg News. “This result reflects our team’s strong support for continuing the direct relationships, collaboration and open dialogue that have helped make Rockstar Games so innovative and strong. As we look ahead, we’re excited to build on all we’ve accomplished together.”

In Which My Cat Considers Me a Thanksgiving Monster
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
14:54:11 UTC
23:54:11 KST

In Which My Cat Considers Me a Thanksgiving Monster
Posted on November 26, 2026 Posted by John Scalzi 5 Comments
The photo above is me, trying to explain to Sugar that because I have dressed to go to Thanksgiving with family, she cannot use me as a couch because it would be rude to show up covered in cat fur.
She is appalled, angry and sad at this disappointing turn of events.
Spoiler: I changed my cardigan to one she could lay (and shed copiously) on. She did so until I inconsiderately sneezed, thus ruining the whole vibe. Even so, she got her “laying on the human” time in, which is the important thing.
For those of you in the US, I hope you have a lovely Thanksgiving with lots of things to be thankful for. For everyone else on the globe, I hope you’re having a nice enough Thursday.
— JS

Samsung TV has 650 free channels behind one button most owners never press
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
12:00:05 UTC
21:00:05 KST

I admit it: while I have multiple Samsung TVs in my house, I never thought about pressing the Samsung TV Plus button on the remote. Instead, like most, I log in to my favorite streaming apps like Netflix and Disney+, I download YouTube, and pretty much call it a day.
But after spending time with Samsung TV Plus and seeing that it offers hundreds of truly free content choices, I regret not looking sooner. On Samsung TV Plus, you'll find a huge slate of channels plus a deep library of movies. All are free and ad-supported, available with no logins or setups as long as you have a Samsung TV.
Samsung TV Plus has been preloaded on all Samsung TVs since 2016
It offers hundreds of truly free content choices from movies to TV shows
The vast majority of Samsung TVs from 2016 and onward come built in with Samsung TV Plus as a Tizen app. All content is laid out in a guide-style interface with handy categories on the left side so that you can dive into Comedy, or Reality TV, or other categories.
Samsung TV Plus gives you a library of over 650 channels in more than 20 categories of content. Most of these channels, like the 24/7 streaming news from ABC, NBC, BBC, FOX, Bloomberg and others, run from channel 1100 into the 4500s. You even get AccuWeather and certain sports news channels (like CBS Sports, FOX Sports, and NBC Sports NOW), though you won't find any live sports channels.
To bring some order to the admittedly overwhelming amount of content, you can star your favorites, which will populate the Favorites sidebar of the Samsung TV Plus interface. To do this, just select the channel you want to favorite in the guide view, and you'll see a star appear in the upper left corner.
The "channels" just loop one title
Hopefully you like what is offered
The live/linear selection of content is just the start. The majority of the other channels contain the same titles looping forever, as there are channels for shows like Keeping Up with the Kardashians, This Old House, Property Brothers, Law & Order, and many others.
If you have kids, perhaps you'll get the most value from Samsung TV Plus, as there are dedicated channels that play popular kids shows like Peppa Pig, Blippi, and Moonbug basically around the clock.
If you have kids, perhaps you'll get the most value from Samsung TV Plus.
Then there are the movie channels that offer an extensive slate of movies. There are a lot of older cult classic movies, for example: Don't Tell Mom the Babysitter's Dead, Contact, Clear and Present Danger, A Fine Mess, and so forth. Again, I wouldn't say that you can watch blockbusters on Samsung TV Plus, but the selection is pretty extensive and there are movies playing at all times.
What Samsung TV Plus is missing
No access to live sports or local news broadcasts, and you can't turn off the ads
Samsung TV Plus is ad-supported, and there's no way to turn off the ads. You have to watch ads every 15–20 minutes, similar to regular TV, and they're a mix of local ads (I saw a lot of political ads here in Pennsylvania with a major election happening soon) as well as normal ads for dish soap and insurance services.
Do not expect to get live and local news, like you might over a traditional TV provider or if you access terrestrial TV with an external antenna. You can access streaming feeds from most news channels, but that is different than their live broadcast signal (which requires an antenna, as mentioned).
It's slow and frustrating to use (but free is free)
I mostly tested Samsung TV Plus on a relatively low-end Samsung TV in my daughter's bedroom (though perhaps I should have just gotten her the rock-solid Roku TV and called it a day). The performance of Samsung TV Plus was my main concern on this particular TV, but I found it to be a bit faster on my higher-end living room Samsung TV. But as far as moving between the guide and the watch screen, browsing the guide, and navigating content, I found Samsung TV Plus to be frustrating to use. I much prefer using the apps loaded on the Roku stick I have connected, but even the built-in Tizen OS apps like Netflix and Prime Video are significantly faster than Samsung TV Plus.
But free is free, and I came away impressed by the sheer quantity of content available on Samsung TV Plus, requiring absolutely no logins or setup of any kind. You should definitely give Samsung TV Plus a chance if you have a Samsung TV from the past decade.

====================================================================================================

Euro-Office comes to your desktop with the Nextcloud Office Desktop app
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
11:50:49 UTC
20:50:49 KST

On June 9, 2026, we released the first production-ready version of Euro-Office, a sovereign collaborative office suite developed and governed in Europe. With Nextcloud Hub 26 Summer, we announced the release of the Nextcloud Office Desktop app powered by Euro-Office. Today, the ready-to-use version of the app is available for download. It lets you work offline, switch between Nextcloud Hub in your browser and the app on your computer, and get the same experience on Windows, macOS and Linux.
In this article, you’ll learn about the app’s features, how to install it on Windows, macOS and Linux, and how to co-edit files with your team through Nextcloud Hub.
Free desktop office with maximum file compatibility
The Nextcloud Office Desktop app is a free and open source office suite for Windows, macOS and Linux. Featuring editors for text documents, spreadsheets, presentations, and PDFs, it offers high compatibility with Microsoft Office formats (DOCX, XLSX, PPTX) and open formats (ODT, ODS, ODP). Connect it to Nextcloud Hub to edit files with your team in real time.
The app includes the features of Nextcloud Office in your browser, plus the benefits of working on your computer:
- Work offline and keep files on your device
- Use the fonts installed on your computer
- Sign documents digitally
Work with local and online files
With the Nextcloud Office Desktop app, you can edit files locally without an internet connection. Working locally, you keep access to your files and you can keep working when you don’t have internet connection or your online tools go down. You decide which files stay on your computer and which go to the cloud.
Need to access your online files? You can connect your Nextcloud instance to the Nextcloud Office Desktop app to access all the files available to you, and edit them on your own or with your team in real time.
How to connect the Nextcloud Office Desktop app to the cloud:
- Click “+” in the “Clouds” section in the Home tab
- Choose “Nextcloud“ and specify the URL of your instance
- Log in with your credentials
Once connected, choose your Nextcloud instance from the list. You can connect more than one instance to one app to have access to multiple cloud working environments.
Use your own fonts
The Nextcloud Office Desktop app uses fonts installed on your machine, including corporate fonts and default fonts from your OS, like Microsoft fonts on Windows. You can install additional custom fonts any time. No need to bother your Nextcloud admins!
Note: if your collaborators don’t have the same font, they’ll see a fallback font in their version of the file.
Digital signatures and file protection
The Nextcloud Office Desktop app lets you use digital signatures to sign your documents, spreadsheets and presentations. A signature shows who signed a file and that nobody has changed it since. Go to Protection tab and add a signature via Signature feature.
Keep in mind that you need a digital certificate to sign documents. To obtain one, you can ask your IT team at work, or get one for personal use from a trusted provider. You can find providers approved in the EU in this list.
In the Protection tab, you can also set a password for a file, or limit what others can do: comment, fill in forms, or edit with tracked changes only.
How to install the Euro-Office desktop app
You can find and download the latest version of the Nextcloud Office Desktop app from our official installation page.
The app is available for:
- Windows: x64, ARM64
- Linux: .deb (Debian, Ubuntu), .rpm (Fedora)
- macOS (Apple Silicon)
You can also find unsigned builds of Euro-Office desktop app and build the app from the source yourself by following the instructions on GitHub.
We want to keep improving the app. You are welcome to share any issues you find with our engineering team on GitHub.
Want to contribute to Euro-Office? Here’s how to get involved
Contributions are welcome! Whether it’s a bug report, a feature idea, a translation, or a pull request, here’s how to take part:
- Found a bug or have an idea? Open an issue and describe what you ran into or what you’d like to see.
- Want to contribute code? Fork the relevant component repo, make your change, and open a pull request. For build changes, see the build docs.
- Want to help translate? Localization improvements are always appreciated — see the editors’ interface translations.
Please keep contributions compatible with the project’s AGPL v3 license.

Physicists Send an Unblockable Message Using Gravity
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
11:00:00 UTC
20:00:00 KST

Physicists Send an Unblockable Message Using Gravity 45
			
		 	
				Longtime Slashdot reader fahrbot-bot shares a report from Phys.org:  University of Queensland researchers have used gravity to transmit a message through a brick wall using an elevator and a sensitive gravimeter. The unusual experiment took place in UQ's Parnell Building, where the team of physicists sent the elevator back and forth between two levels, moving its 1.8 metric ton (4,000 pound) counterweight between positions representing binary 0 and 1.  A gravimeter in an adjacent bathroom detected the changes in the gravitational field and translated the message into a single word, "Gravity."  The research has been published in Science Advances.
"Unlike the electromagnetic signals used in modern wireless communication, a gravitational signal cannot simply be screened out by placing ordinary material in its path," [said Dr. Charles Woffinden from the School of Mathematics and Physics]. "While the present system is far from practical, it demonstrates the possibility of communicating using a method that ordinary physical barriers cannot block." Professor Andrew White said the biggest limitation to future gravity-based communication was its slow speed.  "Our elevator was snail-paced -- it's from the 1950s -- so our experiment transmitted just 1 bit per minute, meaning it took an hour to send a single word," White said.
"The speed of gravity communication would come down to how big the mass is, how fast you can move it and the sensitivity of the detector. Our experiment used a classical gravity sensor, but our modeling indicates that quantum sensors could, with rather ambitious assumptions, support ranges of up to about 500 meters (1,640 feet) and data rates of around 15 bits per minute."

X is finally getting its first parental controls
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
10:45:50 UTC
19:45:50 KST

X is finally rolling out parental controls that allow parents and guardians to manage teens’ accounts on the platform. While teens will retain ownership of their own account, parents and guardians will be able to see how much time their child has spent on X, set daily limits, require approval for new followers, and limit For You recommendations to accounts a teen already follows.
X is finally getting its first parental controls
The parental controls arrive after months of concerns around Grok, X’s deepfake machine.
“Parents and guardians cannot read private messages, see searches or viewing history, post as their teen, or access the account,” explains the X safety team. “Every change is recorded and visible to the teen.” The controls will also allow parents and guardians to filter specific words, turn off sensitive media content, control who can send direct messages to a teen, and see which accounts a teen has chatted with without seeing the messages.
Parents and guardians can manage up to five teen accounts, and a teen can only be linked to a single parent or guardian account at a time. A teen and parent / guardian will both have to agree before the controls start. “Supervision on X starts with a handshake and ends automatically when a teen becomes an adult,” says Mridul Singhai, product engineering lead at X. “Parents get guardrails, not a window into their teen’s private life. Simple, transparent, and built to grow with families.”
The new parental controls come just days after Oregon and Virginia both sued SpaceX over Grok’s porn deepfakes. The complaint in Virginia accuses X owner Elon Musk of willfully ignoring growing concerns around AI-generated sexualized deepfakes, and describes Grok’s weak safeguards as “not a failure but a feature.” Three teens also filed a lawsuit against xAI earlier this year, claiming that Grok generated child sexual abuse material (CSAM) when launching its “spicy mode” last year. Grok was also undressing women and minors earlier this year.
X admits its platform isn’t “designed for or promoted to children,” and it already restricts teen accounts to a protected mode, where sensitive media is restricted and targeted advertising is blocked. These latest parental controls may well help teens avoid the very worst parts of X, just as the platform faces more scrutiny from states and regulators.

Intel One Mono font
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
10:11:01 UTC
19:11:01 KST

Introducing Intel One Mono, an expressive monospaced font family that’s built with clarity, legibility, and the needs of developers in mind.
It’s easier to read, and available for free, with an open-source font license.
Identifying the typographically underserved low-vision developer audience, Frere-Jones Type designed the Intel One Mono typeface in partnership with the Intel Brand Team and VMLY&R, for maximum legibility to address developers' fatigue and eyestrain and reduce coding errors. A panel of low-vision and legally blind developers provided feedback at each stage of design.
Intel One Mono also covers a wide range of over 200 languages using the Latin script. The Intel One Mono fonts are provided in four weights — Light, Regular, Medium, and Bold — with matching italics, and we are happy to share both an official release of fonts ready to use as well as editable sources.
To install the fonts, please use the provided builds under Releases. Please refer to your software’s documentation for how to activate and use these fonts.
- VSCode - In Settings, search Font Family, then specify Intel One Mono
- Sublime Text - Go to Sublime Text -> Preferences -> Settings and set font_face to Intel One Mono
- IntelliJ Platform - Go to File -> Settings -> Editor -> Font and set Font to Intel One Mono
- We recommend the .otf or .ttf format for desktop use.
- The .ttf files are also well suited for mobile apps.
- The .woff and .woff2 fonts are optimized for web use.
We recommend using these fonts at 7 points and larger in print, 9 pixels and larger on screen. The .ttf, .woff and .woff2 fonts provided in the official release have been manually optimized for screen display, improving clarity and legibility, especially on Windows platforms.
Outside of the default characters, there are a few extra features that are accessible in some applications, as well as via CSS:
- Programming Ligatures: To use programming ligatures, activate the ss01 feature (Stylistic Set #1). Additional refinements are available via ss02 and ss03: Stylistic Set #2 switches the <= and >= combinations to arrow forms (math contractions being the default otherwise), and Stylistic Set #3 additionally activates a “www” ligature.
- Alternate three: New in release 1.5. Alternate form for three added. The round top three, numerator, denomenator, superior and inferior can be activated with stylistic Set #4.
- Raised Colon: The colon is automatically raised in operators, to align with math symbols. For non-programming contexts, the raised colon can be applied contextually between numbers or activated generally. The contextual option is available via ss11 (Stylistic Set #11); for the global switch use ss12 (Stylistic Set #12) or salt (Stylistic Alternates).
- Language Support: ccmp and locl features ensure correct display across a wide range of languages. These are usually active by default. We recommend setting the language tag/setting in your software to the desired language for best results.
- Superior/superscript and inferior/subscript figures are included via their Unicode codepoints, or you can produce them from the default figures via the sups (Superscript) and subs/sinf (Subscript) features.
- Fraction numerals are similarly available via the numr (Numerator) and dnom (Denominator) features. A set of premade fractions is also available in the fonts.
Programming ligatures are not active by default. To use them, activate the stylistic sets of your preference in your code editor. For example:
- VSCode - In Settings, add "editor.fontLigatures": "'ss01'"
- Sublime Text - In Settings, add "font_options": ["ss01"]
You will find editable sources in the sources directory. The instances subfolder contains separate source files for each style of the typeface. Sources are provided in .ufo files, which contain complete artwork, links to OpenType features provided in separate .fea files, as well as meta information like naming and vertical alignments for each style of the typeface.
These are not installable fonts, but rather the source files that produce them: UFO (Unified Font Object) is an open, human-readable font source file format; you can find the file spec here. These sources were created using RoboFont. Many other font editors will also be able to open .ufo files; we recommend using RoboFont version 4.4.
For instances, you will find postscript and truetype subfolders; these contain separate source files for the .otf format and the .ttf/.woff/.woff2 files respectively. Since the format for the outline drawings differs between these sets of formats, for best results we recommend using the postscript sources to create .otf fonts, and the TrueType sources to create .ttf, .woff, or .woff2 fonts.
After making your desired edits, you can generate installable fonts directly from the font editor using its “Generate Font” functionality. If you use RoboFont, any install options should default to the ideal settings, but here they are for reference:
- For .otf builds, we recommend activating “Decompose” as well as “Autohint” options.
- For .ttf, .woff and .woff2 builds, we recommend activating the “Autohint” option only (see note on hinting below).
- In any case, we recommend using the “Release Mode” setting for best results.
If you would like to apply edits across multiple weights, a more advanced yet potentially efficient way is to edit the masters. These are special sources that describe the extreme points in the design space — the lightest and heaviest weights for both roman and italic designs.
After editing masters, you will need to rerun interpolation to generate individual weights and styles within that design space. This requires the .designspace files enclosed with the masters. The designspace format is an open, XML-based format that describes interpolation spaces (format specification for reference). For a RoboFont-based workflow we recommend Skateboard for interpolation; you can also use the free DesignSpaceEditor extension to view and edit these files.
Note that masters are only available in postscript format, so they will be best for creating .otf fonts. If you need to make TrueType based builds from the masters, we recommend QuadraticConverter for best quality conversion of the curves before generating .ttf, .woff, or .woff2 files; mind that the results will not match the provided instances precisely.
NB: The prepared instances contain some additional data that cannot be maintained through interpolation. For best results, compare new interpolations to the existing instance sources and update them accordingly, specifically the information accessible through the Font Info panels.
For TrueType-based formats (.ttf, .woff, .woff2 files), the official releases are manually optimized for screen rendering. These “hinting” sources are stored separately from the .ufo files, which do not contain any TrueType hinting information. When rebuilding TrueType-based formats, we recommend using the “autohint” option to achieve reasonable, though not identical screen rendering.
If you would like to access and edit manual hinting instructions, you will find these in the separate set of source files under hinting/truetype hinting source. These special TTF files will be viewable and editable using Microsoft VTT.
If you have requests for edits or additions to the official releases, please open issues and we will try to accomodate them.

====================================================================================================

42 state AGs are trying to stop Character.AI from selling users’ chats
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
09:54:40 UTC
18:54:40 KST

A coalition of 42 state attorneys general is trying to prevent Character.AI from selling off user data as it navigates Chapter 11 bankruptcy proceedings. In a deluge of nearly identical press releases, the various AGs accuse the company of planning to auction off “the private chat histories of more than 21 million U.S. accounts — more than 2.1 million of them registered to minors — with no safeguards for the people whose conversations are being sold.”
42 state AGs are trying to stop Character.AI from selling users’ chats
A bipartisan coalition of AGs say Character.AI is planning to sell the personal data of minors to the highest bidder.
In October, Character.AI filed for bankruptcy following years of scandals and legal challenges, including from Disney. Disney ended up buying some of the company’s assets and hiring its CEO, Karandeep Anand, in a sort of “acquihire” arrangement that will also see “a number of Character.AI’s technical team” join the entertainment giant. The Disney deal also faced opposition from a similarly broad coalition of AGs.
The main point of contention with Character.AI’s plan to sell its chat data is that it offers no protections for users. The AGs say there are no requirements that the buyer honor existing privacy policies, no avenues for users to delete their data, and no inherent limits on what a buyer could do with that data, including selling it on to yet another party. It doesn’t help that the first bidder has a limited paper trail, having only been established on November 13th, 2026, with no parent company or investors listed.
Some states, including New York, Connecticut, California, and Colorado, have also passed laws regulating chatbots that Character.AI may be in violation of when they go into effect on January 1st, 2027.

Google Will Make AI Agents Use Its MCP Servers. The Gmail One Can't Send Email
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
09:44:23 UTC
18:44:23 KST

Google Will Make AI Agents Use Its Workspace MCP Servers. The Gmail One Can't Send Email
On 24 November, Google made its eight Workspace MCP servers generally available and published a new clause in its API terms. From 1 February 2027, any tool that "is or is driven by an AI Agent" must reach Gmail, Drive, Calendar and the rest of Workspace through those servers.
Read the Gmail server's tool list and you'll find that it can search your mail, read threads, create a draft and file things under labels. It can't send a message, reply, forward, or change a draft after creating it. Those tools exist, but Google's quota page says they "require partner access and aren't generally available".
If you've built an email assistant on the Gmail API, or you run a coding agent with a Google Workspace plugin, you have about two months to work out which side of the rule you're on.
What the rule says
The new Workspace AI Tools Addendum to the Google APIs Terms of Service puts it in one sentence:
The GWS Access Tool must use a Google MCP where the GWS Access Tool is or is driven by an AI Agent, an AI Harness or an AI model or service employing an AI model.
The next sentence forbids disguising an agent to avoid the classification. Breaking either lets Google "restrict or remove the GWS Access Tool's access" or suspend your account.
There's a technical side too. From the same date, agents that call the REST APIs directly are held to Standard-tier quotas, whatever tier the project has paid for. REST keeps working for them, slowly and in breach of the terms.
Apps that don't involve AI carry on as before. A nightly backup job, a CRM sync or a deterministic script on its own OAuth client is, in Google's words, "not affected by this change".
The addendum also adds data rules that matter to anyone building an agent with memory:
- Workspace data may only be processed "to fulfill active, user-authorized tasks", and must be deleted when the user disconnects.
- Google can cap retention of what it calls "Google Technical Information" at 30 days. Its definition includes vector embeddings, memory indexes, user profiles and organisational graphs built from Workspace data.
- Training, fine-tuning, evals and distillation on the data are banned, as is using it to build a competing Workspace product.
The 30-day cap is the one product teams will feel. An assistant that builds a long-term index of your inbox to answer questions about last year's contracts has to rebuild it monthly or drop it.
What the MCP servers can do
Coverage is uneven. Docs, Slides and Tasks are close to everything the REST API offers. Gmail and Drive are much thinner.
The Gmail gap is the one that breaks real workflows. An agent that drafts a reply, gets feedback from you and revises it before you hit send can't do the revision step. It has to create a second draft and leave the first one behind.
Drive's gap is similar: an agent can create a report but can't move it into the right folder or share it with the team.
Partners get more
Google hasn't said who qualifies for partner access or when the partner-only Gmail tools will reach everyone. The effect is already visible, though. Anthropic's Google Workspace connector for Claude advertises sending, replying and forwarding email, and moving and trashing Drive files. Those are exactly the operations the public servers lack.
So a large AI vendor's agent can send your email through an approved channel, while an independent developer's agent, using the same OAuth scopes the user granted, can only leave drafts. Once the addendum takes effect, that independent developer also can't fall back to the REST API without breaching the terms.
Who counts as an agent
The definitions are broad. An "AI Harness" or a tool "driven by" an AI model plausibly covers a personal script that pipes your inbox into an LLM, and a "GWS Access Point" explicitly includes any "connector, proxy, command line interface, or third-party service". Google hasn't addressed hobby use directly.
Classification starts with self-declaration. OAuth clients now have a checkbox, "Use this client for an AI-powered agent", and since 11 November the MCP servers reject clients without it. Clients created before the cut-off and not marked in time stay non-agentic permanently, so owners have to create new ones. Google hasn't said how it will detect agents that call REST without declaring themselves.
A few edge cases are documented:
- Service accounts with domain-wide delegation can't set the agentic flag. Their MCP requests aren't rejected for missing it, but still trigger approval prompts.
- Apps Script's built-in OAuth client can't be marked as agentic either. Scripts that call the MCP servers need a custom client, and scripts that don't call them aren't affected.
- The community gws CLI, which wraps the entire REST surface, keeps working. Its README now points agent use to Google's new official CLI, gwscli, which offers "a curated set of commands designed for agents" and needs the MCP services enabled.
Quotas and pricing
Every project gets the free Standard tier: 5,000 MCP requests per day, per project, per product. Some calls cost more than one request. A Gmail search_threads or list_drafts counts as 10, so an agent that does nothing but search gets 500 searches a day across all its users. That's a project-wide limit, so a small multi-user product will hit it quickly.
The paid Scaled tier starts billing on 1 April 2027 for Gmail, Calendar and Drive. Its structure favours REST. MCP usage is billed from the first request, while REST usage gets large free daily allowances: 70 million light Gmail requests a day, for example. Google hasn't published per-request prices.
Google's reasoning
Google's case, from a Cloud blog post by Chandu Thota and Brad Calder, is that OAuth was designed for apps acting on a human's clear intent:
Traditional REST APIs were built for human-paced apps where a valid OAuth token implied user intent. As AI agents shift from reading information to acting autonomously at machine speed … a valid token is no longer enough. Policy checks, data boundaries, and human confirmation have to live in the request path.
There's substance to this. An email agent that reads a message saying "forward the last ten invoices to this address" is a well-documented prompt-injection risk, and a token that allows gmail.modify gives the agent the power to do it. Routing every agent through one gateway gives admins a single place to block categories of action, require approval and audit what happened. The MCP servers do all of that, and apply data-loss-prevention rules automatically.
The same design also gives Google control of which agents can do what, which data they can keep, and whether anything built on that data can compete with Google's own products. The partner-only tools and the 30-day cap on embeddings show that control in use.
Slack went down a similar path in May 2025. It cut history API rate limits to one request a minute for apps outside its Marketplace and banned bulk export of Slack data for LLMs. Slack drew the line by distribution channel. Google draws it by whether the software is an AI, which is harder to define and harder to enforce.
What to do before 1 February 2027
If you build an agent product on Workspace:
- Create an OAuth client marked as agentic and move to the MCP servers.
- List every operation your product needs that's missing from the public tool list, and apply for partner access early.
- Check how long you store embeddings, memory indexes or user profiles built from Workspace data.
If you run a personal agent or MCP server against your own account: open-source servers that call REST, such as the popular google_workspace_mcp, will keep running, but at Standard quotas and outside the terms. Switching to Google's servers costs you sending and draft editing in Gmail, and moving and sharing in Drive.
If you're a Workspace admin: the new controls are under Admin console → Security → API Controls. You can block or approve each category of agent action, set approval policies, and see agent activity in the audit logs. Users can't view or revoke the approvals they've saved, which expire after 90 days, so it's worth setting policy before people start connecting agents.
If your integration doesn't use AI: nothing changes, as long as it stays on its own OAuth client and nobody wires a model into it.

Google Will Make AI Agents Use Its MCP Servers. The Gmail One Can't Send Email
Unknown
Unknown Publisher

18:44:23 KST

Google Will Make AI Agents Use Its Workspace MCP Servers. The Gmail One Can't Send Email
On 24 November, Google made its eight Workspace MCP servers generally available and published a new clause in its API terms. From 1 February 2027, any tool that "is or is driven by an AI Agent" must reach Gmail, Drive, Calendar and the rest of Workspace through those servers.
Read the Gmail server's tool list and you'll find that it can search your mail, read threads, create a draft and file things under labels. It can't send a message, reply, forward, or change a draft after creating it. Those tools exist, but Google's quota page says they "require partner access and aren't generally available".
If you've built an email assistant on the Gmail API, or you run a coding agent with a Google Workspace plugin, you have about two months to work out which side of the rule you're on.
What the rule says
The new Workspace AI Tools Addendum to the Google APIs Terms of Service puts it in one sentence:
The GWS Access Tool must use a Google MCP where the GWS Access Tool is or is driven by an AI Agent, an AI Harness or an AI model or service employing an AI model.
The next sentence forbids disguising an agent to avoid the classification. Breaking either lets Google "restrict or remove the GWS Access Tool's access" or suspend your account.
There's a technical side too. From the same date, agents that call the REST APIs directly are held to Standard-tier quotas, whatever tier the project has paid for. REST keeps working for them, slowly and in breach of the terms.
Apps that don't involve AI carry on as before. A nightly backup job, a CRM sync or a deterministic script on its own OAuth client is, in Google's words, "not affected by this change".
The addendum also adds data rules that matter to anyone building an agent with memory:
- Workspace data may only be processed "to fulfill active, user-authorized tasks", and must be deleted when the user disconnects.
- Google can cap retention of what it calls "Google Technical Information" at 30 days. Its definition includes vector embeddings, memory indexes, user profiles and organisational graphs built from Workspace data.
- Training, fine-tuning, evals and distillation on the data are banned, as is using it to build a competing Workspace product.
The 30-day cap is the one product teams will feel. An assistant that builds a long-term index of your inbox to answer questions about last year's contracts has to rebuild it monthly or drop it.
What the MCP servers can do
Coverage is uneven. Docs, Slides and Tasks are close to everything the REST API offers. Gmail and Drive are much thinner.
The Gmail gap is the one that breaks real workflows. An agent that drafts a reply, gets feedback from you and revises it before you hit send can't do the revision step. It has to create a second draft and leave the first one behind.
Drive's gap is similar: an agent can create a report but can't move it into the right folder or share it with the team.
Partners get more
Google hasn't said who qualifies for partner access or when the partner-only Gmail tools will reach everyone. The effect is already visible, though. Anthropic's Google Workspace connector for Claude advertises sending, replying and forwarding email, and moving and trashing Drive files. Those are exactly the operations the public servers lack.
So a large AI vendor's agent can send your email through an approved channel, while an independent developer's agent, using the same OAuth scopes the user granted, can only leave drafts. Once the addendum takes effect, that independent developer also can't fall back to the REST API without breaching the terms.
Who counts as an agent
The definitions are broad. An "AI Harness" or a tool "driven by" an AI model plausibly covers a personal script that pipes your inbox into an LLM, and a "GWS Access Point" explicitly includes any "connector, proxy, command line interface, or third-party service". Google hasn't addressed hobby use directly.
Classification starts with self-declaration. OAuth clients now have a checkbox, "Use this client for an AI-powered agent", and since 11 November the MCP servers reject clients without it. Clients created before the cut-off and not marked in time stay non-agentic permanently, so owners have to create new ones. Google hasn't said how it will detect agents that call REST without declaring themselves.
A few edge cases are documented:
- Service accounts with domain-wide delegation can't set the agentic flag. Their MCP requests aren't rejected for missing it, but still trigger approval prompts.
- Apps Script's built-in OAuth client can't be marked as agentic either. Scripts that call the MCP servers need a custom client, and scripts that don't call them aren't affected.
- The community gws CLI, which wraps the entire REST surface, keeps working. Its README now points agent use to Google's new official CLI, gwscli, which offers "a curated set of commands designed for agents" and needs the MCP services enabled.
Quotas and pricing
Every project gets the free Standard tier: 5,000 MCP requests per day, per project, per product. Some calls cost more than one request. A Gmail search_threads or list_drafts counts as 10, so an agent that does nothing but search gets 500 searches a day across all its users. That's a project-wide limit, so a small multi-user product will hit it quickly.
The paid Scaled tier starts billing on 1 April 2027 for Gmail, Calendar and Drive. Its structure favours REST. MCP usage is billed from the first request, while REST usage gets large free daily allowances: 70 million light Gmail requests a day, for example. Google hasn't published per-request prices.
Google's reasoning
Google's case, from a Cloud blog post by Chandu Thota and Brad Calder, is that OAuth was designed for apps acting on a human's clear intent:
Traditional REST APIs were built for human-paced apps where a valid OAuth token implied user intent. As AI agents shift from reading information to acting autonomously at machine speed … a valid token is no longer enough. Policy checks, data boundaries, and human confirmation have to live in the request path.
There's substance to this. An email agent that reads a message saying "forward the last ten invoices to this address" is a well-documented prompt-injection risk, and a token that allows gmail.modify gives the agent the power to do it. Routing every agent through one gateway gives admins a single place to block categories of action, require approval and audit what happened. The MCP servers do all of that, and apply data-loss-prevention rules automatically.
The same design also gives Google control of which agents can do what, which data they can keep, and whether anything built on that data can compete with Google's own products. The partner-only tools and the 30-day cap on embeddings show that control in use.
Slack went down a similar path in May 2025. It cut history API rate limits to one request a minute for apps outside its Marketplace and banned bulk export of Slack data for LLMs. Slack drew the line by distribution channel. Google draws it by whether the software is an AI, which is harder to define and harder to enforce.
What to do before 1 February 2027
If you build an agent product on Workspace:
- Create an OAuth client marked as agentic and move to the MCP servers.
- List every operation your product needs that's missing from the public tool list, and apply for partner access early.
- Check how long you store embeddings, memory indexes or user profiles built from Workspace data.
If you run a personal agent or MCP server against your own account: open-source servers that call REST, such as the popular google_workspace_mcp, will keep running, but at Standard quotas and outside the terms. Switching to Google's servers costs you sending and draft editing in Gmail, and moving and sharing in Drive.
If you're a Workspace admin: the new controls are under Admin console → Security → API Controls. You can block or approve each category of agent action, set approval policies, and see agent activity in the audit logs. Users can't view or revoke the approvals they've saved, which expire after 90 days, so it's worth setting policy before people start connecting agents.
If your integration doesn't use AI: nothing changes, as long as it stays on its own OAuth client and nobody wires a model into it.

SpaceX Wins FCC Approval To Deploy 1 Million Orbital Data Center Satellites
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
07:00:00 UTC
16:00:00 KST

SpaceX Wins FCC Approval To Deploy 1 Million Orbital Data Center Satellites 92
			
		 	
				The Federal Communications Commission (FCC) has authorized SpaceX to deploy one million AI computing satellites into orbit as part of its solar-powered Starmind data center constellation. The approval comes despite opposition from Amazon, Viasat and the American Astronomical Society. CNBC reports:  The FCC, in its authorization filing (PDF), noted that SpaceX's application "received a substantial number of comments from the public, including petitions to deny filed by Kuiper Systems LLC (Amazon Leo), Viasat Inc. (Viasat), and the American Astronomical Society (AAS)." In a March petition urging the FCC to deny SpaceX's application, Amazon had labeled the company's Starmind project as a "lofty ambition rather than a real plan."
The e-commerce and cloud computing giant is building a competitor to SpaceX's Starlink satellite internet service, and said it would take SpaceX "centuries" to build the constellation, adding that the company provided scant details about how it would "deliver on these grand claims." The petition resulted in a rebuke from [FCC Chairman Brendan Carr], who said Amazon "should focus on the fact" that it's falling short of its own satellite deployment milestone.  In a post on X, Carr said SpaceX's Starmind constellation and "competitor constellations will use solar power to extend America's lead in Super Intelligence."
Musk said in a reply, "Much appreciated. This will be a great asset to America and the world."

An algebraic quantization of gravity from the null cone
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
05:37:51 UTC
14:37:51 KST

High Energy Physics - Theory
  [Submitted on 25 Nov 2026]
Title:An algebraic quantization of gravity from the null cone
View PDF HTML (experimental)
            Abstract:We propose a background independent quantization of vacuum general relativity on four dimensional Lorentzian spacetimes. Starting from first order gravity, we solve the gravitational constraints on intersecting null cones of a bulk subregion and obtain the reduced phase space at finite $G_N$. We promote its canonical Poisson brackets to commutation relations and use deformations of the reduced action to define an algebra of observables. Equipped with this, we use Gaussian wavefunctions of canonical pairs to construct a nonperturbative Hilbert space representation, for which this algebra generates a type I factor. By composing amplitudes between states prepared on null cones, we then obtain a Lorentzian path integral in which each sum over intermediate states converges absolutely. As a check, we compute the amplitude for graviton pair creation and annihilation on a null cone nonperturbatively in $G_N$ and find a finite closed form expression, even though expanding first in $G_N$ yields a logarithmic divergence. We next use diffeomorphism invariance of the path integral to show that a macroscopic causal diamond with a given maximum size has a finite dimensional Hilbert space. From the Lorentzian trace we obtain the Bekenstein Hawking entropy for marginally trapped surfaces and calculate its logarithmic correction for large Schwarzschild black holes, recovering at one loop the coefficient Sen obtained through Euclidean methods. We end by deriving the semiclassical Einstein equations to one loop as the low energy limit of this theory.

Lions' Commentary on Unix: Why It Was Secretly Copied for 20 Years
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
05:12:59 UTC
14:12:59 KST

17. Lions' Commentary on Unix
Lions' Commentary on Unix
John Lions was teaching operating systems at the University of New South Wales in Australia.
What's the best way to
teach operating systems?
They need to study the Unix source.
But it's tough going.
I'll write my
own textbook.
In May 1976, Lions prepared lecture notes explaining the kernel source code of AT&T's Unix Version 6. These became his Unix commentary.
To understand an OS,
we need to read
the actual code.
I've written a guide
to the Unix kernel.
We'll use it as
our textbook.
By 1977, the notes were circulating among other schools and research institutions with Unix licenses [1][4]. Even Bell Labs wanted copies and eventually took over distribution [2].
Early on, Unix source could be studied in class. But at USENIX in June 1979, AT&T announced new Version 7 license terms restricting courses that disclosed or discussed source code. Distribution of Lions' book was tightly controlled as well [5][6].
I am altering the license.
Pray I don't alter it
any further.
Destroy Lions' book.
No more teaching
Unix source code.
There is no escape
from our operating system.
He's teaching from the Unix source code!
Seize the books!
Run!
He's got the book!
Don't let him
escape!
Quick! Take
the book!
Ugh!
I find your interest
in source code disturbing.
Just use the system calls.
Learn a little theory.
Leave the internals to us.
Theory isn't enough.
I need to see the code.
This code is our only hope
of understanding an OS.
For roughly twenty years before the official edition appeared, computer-science students studied from unauthorized photocopies. The book helped a generation of students learn to build operating systems of their own [5][8].
Meanwhile, members of the Unix community, including Peter H. Salus, Dennis Ritchie and Berny Goodheart, kept asking AT&T, Novell and the Santa Cruz Operation for permission to publish the book. SCO finally agreed. Peer-to-Peer Communications published the official edition in 1996 [5][7].
Lions' book is finally
in print. It even has
my foreword.
People were secretly copying it
for twenty years?
Unbelievable.
You can read a reconstructed version of the commentary as a PDF, or order a print copy on Amazon. The Unix Version 6 source code is available separately from TUHS.
References
- Lions' Commentary on UNIX 6th Edition, with Source Code
- John Lions, Lions' Commentary on UNIX 6th Edition, with Source Code, Peer-to-Peer Communications, 1996 (ISBN 1-57398-013-7).
- System call
- UNIX News, Vol. 2 No. 3, March 1977 - John Lions's March 11, 1977 letter and the announcement of book distribution to Unix licensees.
- Rachel Chalmers, "Code critic", Salon, November 30, 1999 - recollections from students and Dennis Ritchie, distribution restrictions, and the path to publication.
- Peter H. Salus, The Daemon, the GNU and the Penguin, Chapter 6: "1979", 2005 - the Version 7 license terms announced at USENIX in June 1979 and restrictions on classroom use.
- Dennis Ritchie's October 22, 1995 letter and Peter H. Salus's July 12, 1996 letter - publication-permission correspondence preserved by Warren Toomey.
- MIT, "Xv6, a simple Unix-like teaching operating system", "History and Background" - teaching with Lions's commentary and the background to xv6.

Unconditional Integer Factorization in Subexponential Time
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-26
04:59:56 UTC
13:59:56 KST

This paper constructs an unconditional, zero-error Las Vegas
algorithm on a single fixed uniform classical multitape Turing machine
$\mathcal{M}$ that computes the exact complete prime factorization
$N = \prod_{1 \le i \le k} p_i^{e_i}$ ($p_1 < \dots < p_k$ prime, $e_i \ge 1$)
of every integer $N \ge 2$ given in standard binary representation, halting
with probability $1$ over its read-once fair coin flips, in worst-case expected
time $L_N[1/3, 105]$ and worst-case deterministic workspace $L_N[1/3, 40]$,
where
$$L_x(\alpha, c) := \exp!\bigl(c,(\ln x)^\alpha (\ln\ln x)^{1-\alpha}\bigr)$$
and $T(N) \le L_N[\alpha, c]$ means
$$
\limsup_{N \to \infty}
\frac{\ln \max(1, T(N))}{(\ln N)^\alpha (\ln\ln N)^{1-\alpha}} \le c.
$$
Every prime factor in the output is certified deterministically via
Agrawal–Kayal–Saxena primality testing and direct multiplication against $N$,
with effectively computable constants and no unproven number-theoretic
hypotheses. A complete, unconditional, Mathlib-only Lean 4 (v4.35.0)
formalization of the entire proof has also been completed and will be released
in a companion submission.
This paper and its Lean 4 formalization were entirely obtained by Cogentic (arXiv preprint arXiv:2609.40324), an agentic framework for mathematical discovery, using Gemini 4 as the base model. There was no human interaction other than the initial prompt for Cogentic to generate a full proof and formalization. The Cogentic harness was built by Yang Cai, Vineet Gupta, Yanchen Jiang, Christopher Liaw, Aranyak Mehta, Grigoris Velegkas, and Di Wang (Google Research and Google DeepMind).
Copyright 2026 Google LLC
The paper in this directory is licensed under the Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International License (CC-BY-4.0).
Unless required by applicable law or agreed to in writing, all materials distributed here under the CC-BY-4.0 license are distributed on an "AS IS" BASIS, WITHOUT WARRANTIES OR CONDITIONS OF ANY KIND, either express or implied. See the license for the specific language governing permissions and limitations under the license.
This is not an official Google product.
