Amazon loses its latest attempt to get out of handling more recall notices
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
23:56:44 UTC
08:56:44 KST

Amazon is on the hook to recall more than 400,000 hazardous products after losing its legal battle against the US Consumer Product Safety Commission (CPSC). The US District Court for the District of Maryland ruled in favor of the CPSC, which argued that Amazon is a distributor of goods, not a logistics provider, making it responsible for carrying out recalls of third-party products.
Amazon loses its latest attempt to get out of handling more recall notices
A court ruled in favor of the US CPSC’s argument that Amazon is responsible for recalled products that other companies sell on its platforms.
Amazon sued the CPSC in March 2025 after the agency ordered Amazon to recall over 400,000 “Fulfilled by Amazon” products that pose a “substantial product hazard,” including faulty carbon monoxide detectors, hair dryers without electrocution protection, and children’s sleepwear that doesn’t meet federal flammability standards.
As a result, Amazon will be required to implement the following measures, initially outlined in the CPSC’s January 2025 order:
Notification:
On the day CPSC publishes the recall releases, Amazon must notify the public by posting the recall releases to Amazon.com; notify original purchasers directly via email and by providing information regarding the recalls on each purchaser’s “Your Orders” page.
Amazon must provide one round of notice of the recalls to Fulfilled by Amazon participants that sold the subject products.
Incentive to Remove Products from Commerce:
Amazon must issue a full refund in the amount of the purchase price to any consumer who submits proof of destruction or disposal of a subject product.
Maintaining Records of Recalls:
Amazon must maintain the recall releases on the “Recalls and Product Safety Alerts” webpage on amazon.com for a minimum of five years.
Amazon must submit monthly progress reports for five years and maintain records of its actions to comply with the Order for the same period.
“We have a longstanding commitment to removing unsafe and recalled products from our store and notifying customers, backed by an industry-leading recalls process,” Amazon spokesperson Sammy Brill says in an emailed statement to The Verge. “We’re reviewing our options following the ruling and look forward to continuing to work with the CPSC to put customer safety first.”
Update, November 25th: Added Amazon’s statement.

Suspected Chinese Agents Are Trying to Dupe Americans with Fake Listings on Job Platforms
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
23:00:00 UTC
08:00:00 KST

Suspected Chinese Agents Are Trying to Dupe Americans with Fake Listings on Job Platforms (cnn.com) 17
			
		 	
				"LinkedIn is probably the greatest intelligence tool that's ever been created," CNN heard from Holden Triplett, director of counterintelligence at the National Security Council in the first Trump administration.
A LinkedIn spokesperson responded that "Creating a fake account or misrepresenting your identity is a clear violation of our terms of service."  (And they also told CNN that LinkedIn remains "focused on detecting state-sponsored abuse and will continue to enforce our policies against fake accounts.")  But it's not just happening on LinkedIn...
Suspected Chinese spies have created an elaborate web of fake firms with job opportunities aimed at tricking current and former US officials into divulging government secrets as part of a scheme that is more pervasive than previously reported, CNN has found.  Interviews with a dozen people caught up in the operation — as well as CNN analysis of suspected bogus websites — reveal how there is a much broader target pool for obtaining sensitive US information after the Trump administration laid off thousands of federal employees. [CNN reported in 2025 that "foreign powers such as China and Russia" had directed their intelligence services "to ramp up recruiting of Americans working in national security by targeting those who had been fired or felt they were at risk of losing their jobs".] These workers left their posts with inadequate guidance and protection from spying plots, ex-officials said, creating a potentially valuable resource for Beijing and weak spot for Washington...  In addition to government workers, the plot has targeted journalists, trade consultants and others, who might have knowledge deemed valuable to US adversaries.
The FBI offered a glimpse into the malicious recruiting scam when it seized 13 websites officials say were tied to fake consulting firms in June. China denied the FBI allegations. CNN analyzed the sites' software code, looked at other suspicious job listings, and interviewed people approached by similar companies. CNN identified several more websites that mimicked the style and substance of those disabled by the FBI.   Two more sites that Max Lesser, a senior analyst at the Foundation for Defense of Democracies think tank, discovered and shared with CNN have some of the same web infrastructure or other connections to the seized websites.  In total, Lesser has uncovered more than 100 other web domains, many of which share similar characteristics with the sites taken down by the FBI....
This sprawling network has the hallmarks of a Chinese intelligence operation, Lesser told CNN.  
After creating fake web sites, the operatives "plant vague, well-paying job ads for consulting positions focused on China-related issues" on jobs sites like LinkedIn, CNN reports, asking applicants to produce "research reports". ("[D]ozens or hundreds of such reports, across various topics of interest to Beijing, can start to form a much clearer picture of US national security policy and its vulnerabilities, according to current and former FBI officials.")
CNN sent a detailed request for a comment to a spokesperson for the Chinese embassy in Washington, DC.  "I am not familiar with what you mentioned," they responded, adding that China "opposes vilifying China by peddling the so-called 'Chinese spy' narrative."

Duran Duran guitarist Andy Taylor dies aged 65
United Kingdom
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
22:57:01 GMT
07:57:01 KST

Tributes for Duran Duran guitarist Andy Taylor after his death aged 65
- Published
Simon Le Bon, Sir Rod Stewart and Gary Kemp are among the pop stars who have paid tribute to Duran Duran guitarist Andy Taylor following his death at the age of 65.
Taylor co-wrote some of the group's biggest hits including Save A Prayer, Rio and Girls On Film.
Le Bon and his bandmates paid tribute, saying "he brought an indelible fire and swagger to our music", while Sir Rod remembered his "dear pal" and Spandau Ballet's Kemp called him "a great player who set the tone for the 80s".
Taylor's wife Tracey called him "the rock of our family", adding: "We will miss him every single day." He died after "a long and courageous battle with prostate cancer", Duran Duran said in a statement on Wednesday.
- Andy Taylor: Duran Duran's livewire guitarist who was a Wild Boy on stage and off 
  - Published19 hours ago
He died "peacefully at home, surrounded by his family, on Monday 23rd November", a statement said.
Paying tribute, his Duran Duran bandmates said: "From the moment he arrived in Birmingham from the North East with a guitar and an unshakeable belief in what we could become, he brought an indelible fire and swagger to our music.
"His playing drove the songs that took us around the world, and with a dry wit that was matched only by his wonderfully unsentimental, no-nonsense outlook on life he kept us grounded and gave the band its edge through the extraordinary years that followed."
They said their thoughts were with his wife, children and grandson.
Le Bon said there was a "a massive Andy Taylor shaped hole in my heart".
"Andy never took the easy musical route. His overdrive overdrove us into being the only funk/rock/punk band in the New Romantic movement, with songs that still survive, decades later."
Sir Rod, who wrote five songs with Taylor for the 1988 album Out of Order, recalled them making "some good music together" and having "some outrageous fun".
Kemp said Taylor "influenced many of us along the way with his sparse staccato style", adding: "He brought shameless rock to the mix."
In a band tribute, Spandau Ballet called him "a hugely talented musician and an important part of the British music scene".
US guitarist Nile Rodgers, the co-founder of Chic, recalled "countless great days and nights" with his "dear friend".
Belinda Carlisle said she was "blessed" to have worked with Taylor, who played the guitar solo and was in the video for her song Mad About You.
Former Led Zeppelin frontman Robert Plant called Taylor "a tough fighter", while Then Jerico's Mark Shaw remembered him as "one of the greatest musicians and songwriters I've ever heard and had the privilege to work with".
Taylor's wife Tracey, a hairstylist he met during the early days of Duran Duran, and whom he married in 1982, said: "For all the incredible experiences music brought him, family always came first for Taylor, adding: "Nothing made him happier than having us all together."
She said he faced the last few years "with so much courage and determination" and said they were "grateful" for the "kindness and support shown to him by fans throughout his illness", saying it brought him "immense comfort and strength".
The son of a fisherman, Taylor taught himself guitar, bass, and drums and began playing professionally at age 13.
He played with several bands around the UK before answering a music paper advertisement for a "live-wire guitarist", which led to his audition for Duran Duran in Birmingham in April 1980.
The group went on to achieve global fame through the New Romantic movement, which combined slick, melodic synth-pop with aspirational music videos and chiselled good looks.
Taylor's guitar lines - full of glassy, powerful riffs - were often a highlight of their songs, from the choppy opening of Girls on Film to the sleazy scuzz of Hungry Like The Wolf.
Duran Duran were one of the biggest pop acts of the 1980s, and its five members became global hearththrobs.
Their indelible hooks and airbrushed music videos found a natural home on the newly launched MTV, giving their career a huge boost in the US.
They became so popular in the US that the press labeled them the leaders of a "second British invasion" of the charts - alongside groups like Spandau Ballet, Human League and Eurythmics.
During a hiatus in 1984, Andy and John Taylor enlisted Robert Palmer for their successful but short-lived side project the Power Station.
The following year, the guitarist decided to quit Duran Duran after a miserable experience performing at Live Aid.
"We'd reached rock bottom and we had absolutely nothing to say to each other," he wrote in his autobiography, Wild Boy.
"We drove to the venue through streets packed with excited rock fans, but inside the bus the atmosphere was as if we were on a way to a funeral."
He went on to play on Palmer's Addicted To Love, and released a solo album, Thunder, in 1987.
After a period writing and producing albums for other artists, he rejoined Duran Duran in 2000, lasting for just one album (2004's Astronaut) before parting ways again.
In 2022, when the band were inducted to the Rock and Roll Hall of Fame, Taylor was notably absent from the ceremony, despite public plans for a reunion.
When the group took the stage, Le Bon read a letter from the guitarist, in which he revealed his diagnosis with stage 4 metastatic prostate cancer.
Nonetheless, he contributed to the following year's Halloween-themed album Danse Macabre, marking his final collaboration with the band.
The following BBC programmes will pay tribute to Taylor:
- Duran Duran at the BBC, on BBC Four on Friday 27 November at 21:30 GMT
- Sounds of the 80s, on Radio 2 on Saturday 28 November at at 18:00 GMT

  - Published12 November
  - Published5 May 2022

S&P considers Nvidia credit downgrade over governance and ‘key man’ risks
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
22:44:32 UTC
07:44:32 KST

S&P Global Ratings, one of the three major credit rating agencies, is considering downgrading Nvidia’s rating, citing concerns about the company’s management and governance practices.
Despite Nvidia’s strong financial performance, the ouster and reinstatement of CEO Jensen Huang has raised concerns about “weak executive oversight and lack of succession planning” and “key man risk,” meaning heavy reliance on Huang for its future success, S&P said in a statement.
The agency expects to finish its review within 90 days and could cut Nvidia’s rating from AA to AA-, the fourth-highest on its scale.
Although Nvidia is certain to keep its investment-grade rating, a downgrade could raise its borrowing costs. Because Nvidia backstops neoclouds and other infrastructure companies that lack strong credit on their own, a lower rating could make their debt costlier or force them to post more collateral.
In August, Nvidia announced plans to arrange $500 billion in financing for AI infrastructure with six financial institutions, including Goldman Sachs, Apollo, and Blackstone. Under the original terms, Nvidia would guarantee 25% of the loans. But even before Huang’s ouster and return, lenders had been demanding that Nvidia guarantee more, Bloomberg reported.
A downgrade would likely prompt lenders to demand even larger guarantees.

====================================================================================================

Why GTA VI’s version of Florida feels so real
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
22:18:23 UTC
07:18:23 KST

There are plenty of reasons why Grand Theft Auto VI is so popular: the 13 years of anticipation, the relatable lead characters and romantic storyline, the sheer level of depth. But another major factor is the sense of place. The GTA series is unmatched when it comes to re-creating the feeling of a bustling, real-world city, and GTA VI’s fictional rendition of Florida is the studio’s most lifelike setting yet. It has functioning buses and trains, plentiful buildings you can go into and explore, residents you can chat with, and huge areas where you can interact with wildlife far away from the bright lights of the Miami-like Vice City. For Emma Fraser, an assistant professor at UC Berkeley who studies urban ruins and virtual cities, the sense of realism has created a unique sensation while playing.
Why GTA VI’s version of Florida feels so real
Virtual city expert Emma Fraser explains what makes Rockstar’s latest open world work.
“There’s something about this particular game that is really discombobulating and distracting in a sense,” she says. “Because you have these moments where you think, ‘I’m in a place, I’m experiencing a location.’ You comprehend intellectually that it’s not real or that it’s at best a representation, but you still have this feeling in your brain and you’ll still dream about being in that place.”
Fraser teaches courses on topics including urban geography and virtual worlds and has written papers exploring topics like “mapping in the digital age” and “the virtual spectacle of the end of the city.” She says she started researching video game cities in part because, despite their influence and reach, they’re an underexplored space in academia. “I’m a geographer, and geographers are obsessed with cities,” she explains. “So much of geography is thinking about: How do we imagine cities? How do we navigate cities? How do we build cities? How do we map cities? And video games have all of that, but they’re not taken that seriously as an academic interest.”
Like much of the world, she’s been looking forward to GTA VI for years, particularly because of Rockstar’s on-the-ground approach to research. Before development on the game began, developers from the studio traveled to Florida to get an accurate picture. As GTA VI producer Rob Nelson told me, the starting point for the game was the setting, rather than the story or characters. “We just went around to feel it out,” he said. “Go to the Everglades, go to the Keys, go to the city, as many places as we can, and start thinking about what aspects of the place we want to incorporate.”
While there are plenty of open-world games, many of the most successful — from Elden Ring to The Witcher — are sent in fantastical spaces. GTA stands apart in how its various settings look and feel true to the real world. And Fraser believes that this is one of the key reasons the series has such universal appeal. “You know immediately how to function in a city,” she explains. “You know there’s going to be law enforcement, there are going to be city blocks, there are going to be pedestrians, cars, and buses. It’s this really interesting environment that gives you a lot of possibilities to get a player more immersed in your world, because they already know a lot about a city as a general concept.”
“You can’t hold a whole city in your mind at one time.”
Fraser describes GTA VI’s world as having “implied expansiveness.” It’s not a one-to-one re-creation of the real location. There are plenty of things missing; you don’t see schools or daycares, and parking spaces are relatively sparse. But the game gets enough details right to be recognizable. Fraser explains that as long as certain parts of the city look correct — the lighting, the coastline, the decoration of street corners, the major arterial roads — that’s enough to convince people that they’re in something resembling Miami.
“You can’t hold a whole city in your mind at one time,” she says. “Instead it’s these highlights. What [Rockstar] do very successfully in the game is those snapshots. They’ve got far more content that is recognizable as Florida or Miami than they’ve been able to fit into previous games. So it’s a much more overwhelming sense of being in that place.” The “implied expansiveness” also comes in the form of dialogue, radio programs, and TV shows that suggest a much bigger world by discussing places and moments that exist outside of the game you can actually explore.
This level of verisimilitude — combined with the fact that players can explore the city from multiple perspectives, everything from walking to flying a plane — has led to an experience that Fraser likens to virtual tourism, which can result in all kinds of different playstyles. “Some tourists are going to blow a lot of money on taxis and get to wherever they’re going as fast as they can,” she explains, “and some people just like sitting on a bus or a train and getting to know a place.” Similarly, there are certain players who will just experience the story and play through the main missions, and others who will get lost in side activities like bird watching.
“Some people just like sitting on a bus or a train and getting to know a place”
What’s also notable about GTA VI is how its relationship-focused premise is motivating some players to experience the world in a much less violent way. And that includes how they move through the city. Some people are driving more carefully to avoid killing civilians, while others are exploring spaces while both characters hang out together. “In the first few games, you’re this empty vessel. You’re a criminal, you’re doing criminal things,” says Fraser. “Whereas with GTA VI your motivation and the way you think about your character is really important.”
Now that the game is out and millions of players are venturing through Vice City, Fraser is hoping to use it to research the ways that real and virtual worlds intersect when it comes to how we navigate cities. GTA VI offers a lot of choice in this regard; you can still steal a Lambo and speed through the streets, but you can also hop on a train, put on some headphones, and listen to a podcast while getting where you need to be. “Because this world is so fully furnished, and you have so many options, you might start to import your own real-world practices into the game,” she says.
But at this point, what has struck her most is the attention to detail that separates the universe of GTA VI from its contemporaries. “You can’t just generate a generic city and stick icons in, which is what a lot of games do,” she explains. “You aren’t going to get a sense of a real place if you do it that way.”

====================================================================================================

Bare Metal Qwen
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
22:15:34 UTC
07:15:34 KST

A standalone bootable x86-64 operating system that runs Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B directly on hardware or KVM with no Linux, no Windows, no C, no Rust, and no libc.
- 24,252 bytes stripped static Multiboot 1 kernel (FAST_Q8=1, default: .text = 17,143 B, .rodata = 2,772 B, .bss = 2.1 MiB NOBITS, zero dynamic dependencies), or 23,612 bytes (FAST_Q8=0, legacy Q8_0xFP32).
- 74–94 ms cold boot to interactive asm-llm> multi-turn chat with persistent KV-cache across turns, all SMP cores online, and the .asmbin v2 (614 MiB FP16 scales) or v1 (649 MiB FP32 scales) model bound zero-copy from physical RAM on 2 MiB huge pages.
- 100% token-exact and byte-for-byte parity with Phase 1 across 1..32 vCPUs.
- 134.9 tok/s decode on QEMU KVM (AMD EPYC 7642, 16 vCPUs; 23.7 tok/s at 1 vCPU, 118.1 tok/s at 8 vCPUs, 136.6 tok/s peak) and 55.4 tok/s live on a 0 GB disk Proxmox VE VM (Intel Xeon E5-2670 v3, 12 cores).
Running live on a diskless (0 GB storage) Proxmox VE VM booted from the hybrid BIOS+UEFI ISO (Intel Xeon E5-2670 v3, 12 cores, 2 GiB RAM):
- x86-64 CPU with AVX2 + FMA + F16C.
- Core build & QEMU KVM:
sudo apt install nasm binutils qemu-system-x86
- Optional, for building a bootable BIOS+UEFI hybrid ISO (make os-iso): sudo apt install grub-pc-bin grub-efi-amd64-bin grub-common mtools xorriso
- An exported models/qwen3-0.6b-q8_0.asmbin file (--scale-dtype fp16 v2 recommended, or legacy v1 --scale-dtype fp32; see Phase 1 setup).
From the repository root (FAST_Q8=1 is enabled by default; pass FAST_Q8=0 for legacy FP32-activation mode):
make os FAST_Q8=1
make os-check
Expected output (FAST_Q8=1):
-rwxr-xr-x 1 user user 24252 build/asm-llm-os.elf
build/asm-llm-os.elf: ELF 32-bit LSB executable, Intel 80386, version 1 (SYSV), statically linked, stripped
There is no dynamic section in this file.
(The kernel is linked as 64-bit ELF and wrapped into ELF32 via objcopy -O elf32-i386 for standard Multiboot 1 loaders like QEMU -kernel and GRUB2; boot.asm transitions immediately into 64-bit Long Mode.)
Boot directly in the terminal over COM1 serial (-nographic):
qemu-system-x86_64 -enable-kvm -cpu host -m 2G -smp 16 \
  -kernel build/asm-llm-os.elf \
  -initrd models/qwen3-0.6b-q8_0.asmbin \
  -nographic
Or use the Makefile shortcut:
make os-run MODEL=models/qwen3-0.6b-q8_0.asmbin SMP=16
Run a one-shot batch prompt with clean ACPI shutdown:
make os-run MODEL=models/qwen3-0.6b-q8_0.asmbin SMP=16 \
  CMDLINE='prompt="Explain how a CPU cache works in 3 concise sentences." max_tokens=64 think=0 shutdown=1'
Open a live 80x25 VGA GUI window with PS/2 keyboard input:
qemu-system-x86_64 -enable-kvm -cpu host -m 2G -smp 16 \
  -kernel build/asm-llm-os.elf \
  -initrd models/qwen3-0.6b-q8_0.asmbin \
  -vga std -serial stdio
Packages the 23 KB kernel, GRUB2 Multiboot 1 config, and the 649 MiB .asmbin model into a single self-contained ~661 MiB bootable ISO (build/asm-llm-os.iso):
make os-iso MODEL=models/qwen3-0.6b-q8_0.asmbin
Boot the ISO in QEMU KVM (SeaBIOS or UEFI OVMF):
# Legacy BIOS (SeaBIOS)
qemu-system-x86_64 -enable-kvm -cpu host -m 2G -smp 16 \
  -cdrom build/asm-llm-os.iso -nographic
# UEFI (OVMF)
qemu-system-x86_64 -enable-kvm -cpu host -m 2G -smp 16 \
  -bios /usr/share/ovmf/OVMF.fd \
  -cdrom build/asm-llm-os.iso -nographic
Because the entire OS and model live inside the ISO and run out of RAM, the VM needs zero hard disks.
- Upload build/asm-llm-os.iso to your Proxmox node's ISO storage (local -> ISO Images -> Upload, or scp into /var/lib/vz/template/iso/asm-llm-os.iso).
- Create and start a diskless VM on the Proxmox host (pick an unused <vmid>, e.g. 250):
qm create 250 \
  --name asm-llm-os \
  --machine pc \
  --bios seabios \
  --cpu host \
  --cores 12 \
  --memory 2048 \
  --balloon 0 \
  --vga std \
  --serial0 socket \
  --ide2 local:iso/asm-llm-os.iso,media=cdrom \
  --boot order=ide2
qm start 250
- Interact either through the Proxmox web UI (noVNC Console, VGA + PS/2 keyboard) or over the virtual serial port:
qm terminal 250
(Note: --cpu host is required so the VM exposes AVX2 + FMA, and --balloon 0 ensures 2 GiB of contiguous guest RAM for the 649 MiB model + 449 MiB KV-cache arena.)
By default, the bare-metal console runs in multi-turn chat mode with persistent KV-cache: each new user turn prefills only the new Qwen3 chat tokens starting at g_chat_pos without recomputing earlier turns, closes every assistant turn in the KV-cache with <|im_end|>\n, and displays live context usage (ctx=X/2048) in the decode stats line. When the KV-cache approaches max_seq (pos + n_prompt + 64 >= max_seq), it prints [chat] context full, resetting KV-cache and resets cleanly. If booted with prompt="..." and shutdown=0, the initial batch turn stays in the KV-cache so you can continue the same conversation in the REPL.
Both PS/2 keyboard (VGA) and COM1 serial (115200 8N1) are polled simultaneously. Type any message and press Enter, or use a built-in command:
Pass via QEMU -append "...", make os-run CMDLINE='...', or make os-iso CMDLINE='...':
Phase 2 compiles the exact same shared/math.asm, shared/matmul.asm, shared/tokenizer.asm, and shared/transformer.asm with -DBARE_METAL -DFAST_Q8=$(FAST_Q8) and pairs them with 4,167 lines of bare-metal OS code:
- Boot & Long Mode (boot.asm, linker.ld): verifies Multiboot 1 magic (0x2BADB002), zeroes .bss, builds 4-level 2 MiB huge-page identity tables covering 0..4 GiB (with 0xFE000000..0xFFFFFFFF marked Strong Uncacheable for Local APIC MMIO), enters 64-bit Long Mode, installs a 32-entry exception IDT, verifies XSAVE + AVX + AVX2 + FMA + F16C via CPUID, and initializes CR0/CR4/XCR0=7/MXCSR.
- Zero-Copy Weights & Physical Arena (runtime.asm): binds the .asmbin (v2 FP16 scales or v1 FP32 scales) Multiboot module directly at its physical address (validating "ASML" magic) and carves the 449 MiB KV-cache + scratch arena from the largest usable Multiboot memory-map region, zeroing it with AVX2.
- Bare-Metal SMP (smp.asm): parses ACPI RSDP -> RSDT/XSDT -> MADT ("APIC") with a Local APIC broadcast fallback, installs a position-independent 16->32->64-bit trampoline at 0x8000, wakes all AP cores via Local APIC INIT-SIPI-SIPI, enables AVX2/FMA/MXCSR identically on every core, and dispatches both matmul_q8_0 and gqa_attn_range (FAST_Q8=1) with a lock-free cache-line-isolated spin-barrier.
- Hardware Drivers (drivers.asm):
  - COM1 Serial (0x3F8, 115200 8N1): raw UTF-8 TX and RX.
  - 80x25 Color VGA (0xB8000): colored boot banner, hardware scrolling, batched CRTC cursor updates, and UTF-8 decoding with Cyrillic-to-ASCII fallback on VGA while streaming raw UTF-8 to COM1.
  - PS/2 Keyboard (0x60/0x64): scancode set 1 to ASCII (Shift, Backspace, Enter).
  - PIT Channel 2 + RDTSC: 20 ms hardware PIT calibration for accurate nanosecond boot/prefill/decode profiling.
  - ACPI Poweroff: QEMU/Bochs ACPI S5 ports (0x604, 0xB004, 0x4004) plus isa-debug-exit fallback.
python3 scripts/verify_os.py --model models/qwen3-0.6b-q8_0.asmbin --run-dir runs/os-verify
All 5 parity prompts (English technical, English concise, Russian UTF-8, arithmetic word problem, --think mode) match Phase 1 (build/asm-llm) 100% exact in prompt tokens, generated tokens, and raw UTF-8 bytes.
KVM SMP Scaling (FAST_Q8=1, .asmbin v2 FP16 Scales, AMD EPYC 7642, 34 prompt tok, 64 gen tok, greedy)
(Because boot.asm identity-maps guest RAM with 2 MiB huge pages, the bare-metal kernel avoids 4 KiB page-table walks across the 614 MiB model and outperforms both Linux mmap() Phase 1 and llama.cpp CPU Q8_0 at every thread count.)

Bare Metal Qwen
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
22:15:34 UTC
07:15:34 KST

A standalone bootable x86-64 operating system that runs Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B directly on hardware or KVM with no Linux, no Windows, no C, no Rust, and no libc.
- 24,252 bytes stripped static Multiboot 1 kernel (FAST_Q8=1, default: .text = 17,143 B, .rodata = 2,772 B, .bss = 2.1 MiB NOBITS, zero dynamic dependencies), or 23,612 bytes (FAST_Q8=0, legacy Q8_0xFP32).
- 74–94 ms cold boot to interactive asm-llm> multi-turn chat with persistent KV-cache across turns, all SMP cores online, and the .asmbin v2 (614 MiB FP16 scales) or v1 (649 MiB FP32 scales) model bound zero-copy from physical RAM on 2 MiB huge pages.
- 100% token-exact and byte-for-byte parity with Phase 1 across 1..32 vCPUs.
- 134.9 tok/s decode on QEMU KVM (AMD EPYC 7642, 16 vCPUs; 23.7 tok/s at 1 vCPU, 118.1 tok/s at 8 vCPUs, 136.6 tok/s peak) and 55.4 tok/s live on a 0 GB disk Proxmox VE VM (Intel Xeon E5-2670 v3, 12 cores).
Running live on a diskless (0 GB storage) Proxmox VE VM booted from the hybrid BIOS+UEFI ISO (Intel Xeon E5-2670 v3, 12 cores, 2 GiB RAM):
- x86-64 CPU with AVX2 + FMA + F16C.
- Core build & QEMU KVM:
sudo apt install nasm binutils qemu-system-x86
- Optional, for building a bootable BIOS+UEFI hybrid ISO (make os-iso): sudo apt install grub-pc-bin grub-efi-amd64-bin grub-common mtools xorriso
- An exported models/qwen3-0.6b-q8_0.asmbin file (--scale-dtype fp16 v2 recommended, or legacy v1 --scale-dtype fp32; see Phase 1 setup).
From the repository root (FAST_Q8=1 is enabled by default; pass FAST_Q8=0 for legacy FP32-activation mode):
make os FAST_Q8=1
make os-check
Expected output (FAST_Q8=1):
-rwxr-xr-x 1 user user 24252 build/asm-llm-os.elf
build/asm-llm-os.elf: ELF 32-bit LSB executable, Intel 80386, version 1 (SYSV), statically linked, stripped
There is no dynamic section in this file.
(The kernel is linked as 64-bit ELF and wrapped into ELF32 via objcopy -O elf32-i386 for standard Multiboot 1 loaders like QEMU -kernel and GRUB2; boot.asm transitions immediately into 64-bit Long Mode.)
Boot directly in the terminal over COM1 serial (-nographic):
qemu-system-x86_64 -enable-kvm -cpu host -m 2G -smp 16 \
  -kernel build/asm-llm-os.elf \
  -initrd models/qwen3-0.6b-q8_0.asmbin \
  -nographic
Or use the Makefile shortcut:
make os-run MODEL=models/qwen3-0.6b-q8_0.asmbin SMP=16
Run a one-shot batch prompt with clean ACPI shutdown:
make os-run MODEL=models/qwen3-0.6b-q8_0.asmbin SMP=16 \
  CMDLINE='prompt="Explain how a CPU cache works in 3 concise sentences." max_tokens=64 think=0 shutdown=1'
Open a live 80x25 VGA GUI window with PS/2 keyboard input:
qemu-system-x86_64 -enable-kvm -cpu host -m 2G -smp 16 \
  -kernel build/asm-llm-os.elf \
  -initrd models/qwen3-0.6b-q8_0.asmbin \
  -vga std -serial stdio
Packages the 23 KB kernel, GRUB2 Multiboot 1 config, and the 649 MiB .asmbin model into a single self-contained ~661 MiB bootable ISO (build/asm-llm-os.iso):
make os-iso MODEL=models/qwen3-0.6b-q8_0.asmbin
Boot the ISO in QEMU KVM (SeaBIOS or UEFI OVMF):
# Legacy BIOS (SeaBIOS)
qemu-system-x86_64 -enable-kvm -cpu host -m 2G -smp 16 \
  -cdrom build/asm-llm-os.iso -nographic
# UEFI (OVMF)
qemu-system-x86_64 -enable-kvm -cpu host -m 2G -smp 16 \
  -bios /usr/share/ovmf/OVMF.fd \
  -cdrom build/asm-llm-os.iso -nographic
Because the entire OS and model live inside the ISO and run out of RAM, the VM needs zero hard disks.
- Upload build/asm-llm-os.iso to your Proxmox node's ISO storage (local -> ISO Images -> Upload, or scp into /var/lib/vz/template/iso/asm-llm-os.iso).
- Create and start a diskless VM on the Proxmox host (pick an unused <vmid>, e.g. 250):
qm create 250 \
  --name asm-llm-os \
  --machine pc \
  --bios seabios \
  --cpu host \
  --cores 12 \
  --memory 2048 \
  --balloon 0 \
  --vga std \
  --serial0 socket \
  --ide2 local:iso/asm-llm-os.iso,media=cdrom \
  --boot order=ide2
qm start 250
- Interact either through the Proxmox web UI (noVNC Console, VGA + PS/2 keyboard) or over the virtual serial port:
qm terminal 250
(Note: --cpu host is required so the VM exposes AVX2 + FMA, and --balloon 0 ensures 2 GiB of contiguous guest RAM for the 649 MiB model + 449 MiB KV-cache arena.)

Deploying a payphone on a tiny Pacific Island
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
22:12:25 UTC
07:12:25 KST

For the past few years Australia’s Telstra has run a campaign of “Free calls to Santa” from the universal service obligation funded payphones across the country – It’s a really fun idea and we wanted to replicate it on our network on Norfolk Island where we run the mobile network, but the’s no payphones.*
* There is now a payphone – You probably already guessed this.
Getting a Payphone to a small island in the South Pacific
Step 1 was getting a payphone.
Those who have seen my office / shed / studio / the HackHouse, know that I may have one or two payphones… But this needed a payphone that could stand the elements and be modern ish.
The answer, is of course, to use the sexiest payphone of all time; the Nortel Millennium. Featuring VFD display, durable keypad, card service, coin service, no fancy metering line required.
Unfortunately Nortel’s bankruptcy in 2008 meant I couldn’t phone up an account manager, but with the hookup of some guy with a U-Haul van full of Millennium’s in the US and a bit of a road trip, Cody from our team managed to pickup some really clean GTE branded payphones for the job.
Then Cody flew to Sydney where we drove to the house of someone who was heading to Norfolk the next day to explain they’d need to leave all their baggage at home as all of their baggage allowance was to be taken up with the weight of a payphone – Thanks Paul.
Programming
Eventually with the Payphone on Norfolk, we needed to get it programmed; for that there’s Howard Harte’s great mm_manager, which, with the aid of a USB modem is able to program the phones.
Sidebar: I also got this working with a softmodem (A fork of D-Modem with some minor tweaks I’ve documented here), so we could do it via straight SIP without the USB modem required – but the USB modem was the easier path.
The Philtel blog post covers how to setup your Millennium if you’re looking to play along at home yourself.
I hit a bit of a stumbling block with mm_manager as the software assumed you were part of the North American Numbering Plan (NANP) – as one of the literally dozens of people who live outside of America, having to put a 1 in front of the number wasn’t going to fly.
After some trial and error I sussed out what bits on the config files that get pushed to the phone controls this and submitted a PR to mm_manager which is now merged.
We’re also able to set the messaging that appears on the screen, which I’m very happy with the branding on.
We set it for free-on-net calls, so anyone can call any Norfone number without charge and the Payphone tag number.
Talking to Santa
Once this would’ve been the hard part, I did some work a long time ago with IBM Watson and built some stuff to annoy voters via phone with automated phone calls and capture their human responses and sort them and play the appropriate response. But tech has come a long way since 2019, and this was the easy part of the project.
We setup the payphone to make HOHOHO (464646) a destination that did not require coins when we did the rating table setup when programming the phone.
The ATA the payphone is connected to just comes through to our IMS like any other fixed line subscriber; the TAS is setup to zero-rate the destination and then it’s dialplan forwards it to “Santa Bridge” listening for SIP on port 5090.
I’ve got 4 pre-recorded “Norfolk Specific” greetings to buy us time to get everything setup in the background, once these greetings play (each ends asking the caller their name) then we feed into the OpenAI API.
One of the members from our team attempted to ask Santa to write a Python script to iterate over a for loop, while another team member asked Santa for firearms. In both cases it did a good job of redirecting.
All the code for this is on Github.
Branding
The phone came GTE branded, so we needed to redo the card with the branding on it that goes around the screen. Two screws and some wiggling later we pulled out the card, and then I badgered my partner into using their day off to design a new one, with a retro style I really like.
Installation
The install was beautifully completed by long-time-reader-of-the-blog Minas from Shield Electrical and Air.
He knocked up a housing to Shield it from the rain and elements and installed it outside the office entrance.
Getting it on Payphone Tag
The last step of the puzzle was making the phone so it could be captured by cult game Payphone Tag – I reached out to Alex and gave him the info, and now it’s there in the map for anyone to capture.
Future payphone adventures
So with this now in place, we’ve still got to give it some tinsel and Christmas cheer, and I’ll get some more pics of it in place.
We’re waiting for someone to claim it on Payphone tag, and we’ve shipped 3 of these to another even more remote Island I’ll hopefully be able to share more info on later.
If you’re ever on Norfolk, give it a call.

====================================================================================================

====================================================================================================

Samsung’s R95H TV shows what RGB LED is capable of
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
22:05:18 UTC
07:05:18 KST

RGB LED TVs finally became affordable in 2026, and there’s always a lot of promise when new TV technologies come out. More color, higher brightness, improved performance — RGB LED TVs have a lot to prove. The Samsung R95H does a lot to deliver some of those promises. It can display more colors than other TVs I’ve seen so far this year, and the combination of light output and its matte screen makes it great for a bright room. RGB LED TVs might still be in their infancy, but the R95H is a solid TV that shows off the benefits of the new backlight tech.
Samsung’s R95H TV shows what RGB LED is capable of
The excellent matte screen and ability to display a vast amount of colors makes the R95H one of the top RGB LED TVs available.
If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.
Because RGB LED TVs use individual red, green, and blue LEDs for their backlight (instead of a field of white or blue LEDs), they can deliver more colors than LED TVs. The Samsung R95H is one of the best examples of this in RGB LED TVs. It can fully display all the colors used in the vast majority of movies (which use the P3 color space) as well as the few that use the wider BT.2020 color space.
Samsung R95H RGB LED TV
The Good
- Matte screen handles reflections incredibly well
- Good color accuracy in Filmmaker mode
The Bad
- Some color crosstalk in white text
- Grayscale leans a bit cyan
- No Dolby Vision support
The accuracy of those colors is also really good in HDR (it’s not quite as good in SDR, because the TV overbrightens most of them). Some earlier reviews called out mediocre color accuracy on the R95H, which I experienced with the first sample Samsung provided me in June. Samsung replaced that sample because I was worried something was wrong with it. But in Filmmaker mode with firmware version 1247 on my current 65-inch TV, I was pleasantly surprised by the R95H.
Samsung R95H specs
Display type: RGB LED
HDR formats: HDR10 Plus, HDR10, HLG
TV OS: Tizen OS
HDMI inputs: 4 x HDMI 2.1 (one with eARC), up to 8 with Wireless One Connect box (sold separately)
Audio support: Dolby Atmos, Dolby Digital, Auracast, Q-Symphony
Gaming features: VRR (up to 165Hz), ALLM, Game Bar, FreeSync Premium Pro
Sizes available (inches): 65, 75, 85
Colors are wonderfully lifelike. The green of the jungles in Planet Earth II, which uses the expanded BT.2020 color gamut, is beautifully vibrant, as are the hummingbirds that populate the trees. With movies that use P3, the foliage in the demo material on the Spears & Munsil Ultra HD Benchmark disc doesn’t have quite the same brilliance, but it still looks accurate and lifelike. Skin tones are natural. White and gray tones, like those in snow scenes, have a slight blue tint. It’s pretty minor, though.
The R95H also has a good amount of light output — a full-field white screen measured 694 nits, while specular highlights hit 2,206 nits in Filmmaker mode (the TV’s most accurate picture mode). That’s similar to the Sony Bravia 7 II but short of the Bravia 9 II by a few hundred nits, and well short of the TCL X11L. Flagship 2026 OLEDs also have brighter highlights but dimmer full white screens, as is expected from OLED panels. Black levels on the R95H are really good for an LED display. There’s a bit of blooming, but not as excessive as I’ve seen on some RGB LED TVs this year. Overall, the Samsung R95H delivers an impactful image.
The matte glare-free finish on the screen limits reflections well. I was never distracted by seeing the ceiling lights reflecting, as I can be with glossy screens. The black-level raise issue — where ambient light gets caught behind the matte coating, causing the screen to look more gray than black — is much better. With a bright room, the black level rises a small amount, but it’s not washed out, and dark images still have depth.
I had a great time gaming on it, too. Some earlier reviews noted issues with smearing, but recent firmware seems to have addressed this for the most part. While racing in F1 24 in 120Hz mode, motion was smooth. The TV struggled a bit more when I set the Xbox to 60Hz, but I still enjoyed playing.
One inherent issue with RGB LED TVs, though, is color crosstalk — I’ve seen it to some extent on every RGB LED TV, though it’s least apparent on the Sony Bravia 9 II. It can happen when there’s a dominant color onscreen near a lighter color, such as white text, lighter skin tones, or white clouds. The dominant color can bleed into those lighter colors, influencing their tint. This happens a bit on the Samsung R95H. It’s most noticeable with white text. I saw it with news tickers on a red background, making the text look subtly pink, and with gaming, particularly while playing No Man’s Sky. Text displayed above a large yellow hologram has a slight yellow coloration that disappears if I move my view away from the hologram. In fleeting moments, it’s not too distracting, but when the color around the text is consistent, the effect is noticeable.
Some TVs avoid this by defaulting the backlight to white and relying on the color filter, as LED TVs have for years. While doing that gets rid of any possibility of color crosstalk, it also diminishes the TV’s color brightness and wider gamut. So with RGB LED TV technology where it currently is in its development cycle, there’s going to be a tradeoff between some color crosstalk or a more limited color gamut.
The R95H still has the same limitations as all Samsung TVs. There’s no Dolby Vision support (Samsung is an original backer of HDR10 Plus and will likely never support competitor Dolby Vision). It also runs on Tizen OS, which is continually one of my least favorite interfaces. Everything is buried a click or two deeper than it should be, including input selection, which makes it cumbersome to use. The remote, rechargeable via both USB-C and the solar panels on the back, is small, and I’m not a fan of the button layout — they’re bunched together, and the volume control feels clunky to me.
The R95H is also still more expensive than most of its competitors. The Sony Bravia 9 II has better processing and is brighter, although it also has more blooming than the R95H. If you can fit a 75-inch in your space, the TCL X11L is significantly cheaper since it launched. I think it’s a better overall TV. Then there are the flagship OLEDs — Samsung’s S95H and the LG G6 — which are both less than or the same price as the R95H, depending on size. And OLED is still a step ahead of RGB LED in overall picture quality because of its better contrast.
There’s still room for RGB LED TVs to improve. With its wide color gamut coverage, good out-of-the-box accuracy, and excellent matte screen, the Samsung R95H is one of the better ones to come out this year. Put up against those RGB competitors, it makes a strong case. But OLEDs are still better overall performers with superior contrast, and even cheaper non-RGB LEDs can get you most of the way there for less.
Photography by John Higgins / The Verge
How I test TVs
I set up each TV in my living room on my home theater credenza. I stream movies and shows through the TV’s apps and from an Apple TV 4K, play discs on my Oppo UDP-203 4K Blu-ray player (including the Spears & Munsil Ultra HD Benchmark disc), and play games on my Xbox Series X and PlayStation 5. This is done at different times of the day and under different lighting conditions, with curtains open, with lamps and overhead lights on, or with blackout curtains up to keep the room dark. While I am a certified ISF Level 3 calibrator, I do not calibrate the TVs before measurement, as the overwhelming majority of TV owners don’t bother. So it’s important to know how well the TVs perform out of the box, with minor tweaks in the menu anyone can do.
For measurement, I use Portrait Displays’ Calman color calibration software, a Murideo 8K Seven pattern generator, an X-rite i1 Pro 3 spectrophotometer, Portrait Displays’ C6 HDR5000 colorimeter, a Konica Minolta LS-100 luminance meter, and Leo Bodnar 4K lag tester.

S&amp;P Warns Nvidia's Board May Be Compromised
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
22:00:00 UTC
07:00:00 KST

S&P Warns Nvidia's Board May Be Compromised (usnews.com)
			
		 	
				S&P Global Ratings has placed Nvidia's AA credit rating on downgrade watch, warning that Jensen Huang's departure and subsequent reinstatement as CEO exposed weaknesses in the company's corporate governance and board oversight. "We believe the long tenure of certain board members may have compromised their independence. The company's corporate governance practices may also not have kept pace with Nvidia's rapid growth and its role as a critical global technology enabler," the ratings agency said. Reuters reports:  Nvidia currently has an 'AA' rating from S&P, which indicates a very strong capacity to meet financial commitments. The highest rating is 'AAA'. The agency said it could downgrade Nvidia within the next 90 days, but expects any potential downgrade to be limited to one notch, unless investigations and other disclosures harm the company's reputation or future business prospects. Meanwhile, Moody's said on Wednesday that bringing back Huang eases near-term uncertainty around Nvidia's business execution and eliminates execution risk associated with appointing a new leader for the chipmaker. The agency said Nvidia's Aa1 rating and stable outlook remain unaffected, but added that governance and regulatory uncertainty persists.

Anthropic, OpenAI Employees Fear Models Are Too Unsafe to Ever Release
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
22:00:00 UTC
07:00:00 KST

Anthropic, OpenAI Employees Fear Models Are Too Unsafe to Ever Release (axios.com) 105
			
		 	
				Axios reports:
Anthropic and OpenAI are facing an increasingly urgent problem: Internal, unreleased models are becoming so powerful that many employees no longer believe they can be safely released, according to more than two dozen sources familiar with the internal debates...
The challenge could significantly slow the AI boom, as the gap between what the best AI systems can do and humans' ability to control them continues to widen, sources told Axios. 
Researchers haven't found technical solutions for controlling the most advanced models. Some insiders believe it's fixable. Others aren't convinced and say companies should stop training new models entirely... Anthropic and OpenAI have unreleased models that are far more powerful than what they've already released publicly, but the AI companies have struggled to ensure that they are safe enough for public release, sources said.  This has led to debate within Anthropic, OpenAI and the AI research community about whether to stop releasing future frontier models publicly, instead reserving them for internal deployment only. 
There has also been debate around whether to train new models at all, sources inside and outside those companies told Axios.

====================================================================================================

Google’s patent for custom AI video feeds paints a bleak picture of entertainment’s future
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
21:51:26 UTC
06:51:26 KST

Last year, Google filed a patent for a system called My Channel that’s designed to create feeds filled with personalized, AI-generated videos and advertisements based on information the company knows about its users. The patent has been rejected, but unfortunately, Google might appeal or resubmit an amended version, and we could see this monstrosity come to market.
Google’s patent for custom AI video feeds paints a bleak picture of entertainment’s future
Imagine an endless feed of AI slop in which you are the main character of every story.
First spotted by Patentlyze, the patent — which was filed last May and published last week — describes how My Channel would use a person’s demographic data and viewing history to create its own prompts that would then be fed into an LLM to help generate AI videos tailored to that person’s specific interests. In addition to factoring in what kinds of videos a user tends to watch, the system would capture contextual data from their device like IP addresses, GPS locations, and specific details about the user’s age. My Channel could also base its prompts on current news like sports scores and things happening on social media, and all of the video generation would take place on Google’s servers.
According to the patent, My Channel would be able to churn out entire movies of various genres. If a user watches a lot of videos about Christopher Nolan’s The Odyssey, the system might serve up something like Ash Koosha’s Odysseus: The Fall. The patent details how My Channel might be able to connect to other streaming services — which would give it even more information about users and, presumably, access to useful training data for video generation. It all seems like a convoluted way to whip up AI knockoffs of professionally produced art, but what’s most unsettling about the patent are its mentions of My Channel creating digital avatars of users and turning them into “the protagonist or lead character” in the content it pumps out.
Setting aside the fact that My Channel would use photos, videos, and audio clips of people to create these avatars, the system sounds like it is designed to generate a form of “entertainment” that is deeply at odds with what makes engaging with art a worthwhile, enjoyable experience. It can be fun to metaphorically project one’s self into fictional narratives to understand how characters feel, but what Google is proposing is giving people access to an endless supply of bespoke content that doesn’t at all encourage them to use their imaginations to empathize with or relate to the characters. The patent makes it seem like Google thinks that viewers would be entertained enough just by seeing themselves onscreen. That’s bleak in and of itself because My Channel sounds very much like an unholy cross between JibJab and the panoptic elements of Google.
My Channel feels like an even more depressing idea when you consider how the service would make it that much easier for its users to avoid broadening their horizons by being exposed to new things that exist outside their comfort zones. If Google were able to strike the right licensing deals with other streaming services, My Channel users could presumably gorge themselves on artless AI regurgitations of preexisting content. That might keep some people glued to the service — a goal that every streaming platform strives for — but it would also discourage them from engaging with entertainment that isn’t expressly crafted to appeal to their assumed tastes.
When people refer to AI-generated videos as “slop,” they’re talking about the fact that generative models are incapable of generating art with the same kind of creative intention that makes human-made stories compelling. Video generation models can mimic visual elements of the data (read: movies and series) they’re trained on, but they cannot truly think or feel in the way that directors, writers, and actors can. This reality is why it has been so baffling to see AI-boosting entertainment companies, streamers, and studios insisting that the technology will fundamentally revolutionize the industry for the better. It feels much more likely that gen AI is going to keep flooding the internet with garbage no one wants to watch.

====================================================================================================

Much shorter version of GRH Lean verification from Anthropic
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
21:48:10 UTC
06:48:10 KST

Research artifact. Not maintained and not accepting contributions.
A Lean 4 proof of the Riemann Hypothesis and of the Generalized Riemann
Hypothesis for Dirichlet L-functions, built on Mathlib and using only the
axioms propext, Classical.choice and Quot.sound.
The proof is based on Levent Alpöge's, with Claude (https://alpo.ge/remark.pdf). This repository is a shorter version, produced in collaboration with my colleagues at Anthropic, and Claude.
- grh/: readable edition, about 300,000 lines. Start at grh/GUIDE.md.
- grh-compact/: the same five theorems, about 76,000 lines.
Each folder is its own Lake project with its own README.
Needs Lean v4.35.0 and Mathlib at commit 56a00585. In either folder:
lake exe cache get
lake build
lake env lean scripts/PrintAxioms.lean
A build takes one to two hours on 12 cores with 24 GB of memory (about 85 minutes more if the Mathlib cache is missing).
The proof has passed machine checks only, listed in each folder's README. No mathematician has read it through, and no paper comes with it.
Apache 2.0, Copyright 2026 Anthropic, PBC. See LICENSE, and NOTICE and
ATTRIBUTION.md in each folder.

Defang Security Incident
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
21:47:16 UTC
06:47:16 KST

On November 12, 2026 an attacker copied the credentials we use to run Defang and downloaded a copy of our main database. Here is what happened, what was exposed, what we have done, and what you should do.
On November 12, 2026 an attacker got into the Defang Deploy build system and copied the credentials we use to run Defang. With those they downloaded a copy of our main database. We confirmed this on November 23, 2026. If you have a Defang Portal account, your account is in that database, and this post explains what was taken and what you should do. We are also writing to every affected user by email.
If you installed the Defang GitHub App, some of those credentials could also be used against your GitHub repositories and your cloud account. We have replaced them, but there are checks only you can do. They are listed under "What you should do" below. Please go through them as soon as you can.
What happened
We used a separate GitHub App for local development and testing. It was installed on two of our own repositories and had more permissions than it needed, including permission to change deployment environments. Acting as that app, the attacker pushed a commit to one of those repositories and tagged it as a release. Our production environment trusted release tags, so their code ran in our CI with access to our production credentials, and the attacker copied them.
The activity we can see ran from about 06:00 to 10:30 UTC on November 12. The credentials stayed valid until we replaced them on November 23. We are still checking whether they were used after November 12. Some of our logs are not detailed enough to rule that out, so we will update this post when we know more.
Timeline
All times are UTC.
- November 12, 06:05: the first workflow that copied build credentials ran, against our development and staging environments.
- November 12, 06:26: our production credentials were copied.
- November 12, 07:57 to 08:10: the database was copied.
- November 15: we were made aware of a security issue with a dependency and installed the update.
- November 23: a routine security scan found suspicious activity in our repositories.
- November 23: we confirmed the database copy from our access logs, replaced our production credentials and emailed every affected user.
- November 24: we published this post.
What the attacker could see
- Your name and email address. This is the address only: the attacker did not get into your email account or see any of your messages.
- Your GitHub or GitLab username, and the names of the organizations that account belongs to
- The answers you gave when you signed up
- If you installed the Defang GitHub App: the names of the repositories it can access, including private ones
- The cloud accounts you connected to Defang (account, project or subscription IDs and regions; no credentials)
- The Compose files you deployed or generated with Defang
- Copies of deployment state from deploys between October 12 and November 19. These can include database passwords and any environment variable built from a defang config value, such as a database URL that contains a password.
If you installed the Defang GitHub App: what the attacker could use
The attacker could use the Defang GitHub App's key, and the GitHub sign-in token we keep when you connect GitHub. The token was stored encrypted, but the encryption key was taken too. Until we replaced them on November 23, anyone holding these could do in your repositories what the app is allowed to do:
- push commits and branches
- change workflow files and start workflow runs
- overwrite Actions secrets and variables
- change deployment environments and their protection rules
- open and merge pull requests
- change repository settings, including branch protection, deploy keys and collaborators
A workflow run started this way can receive your cloud credentials, because the role Defang set up for GitHub deploys trusts workflows in your repository.
If you have billing details with us
The copied credentials included a key for our account at Stripe, our payment provider. Until we replaced it on November 23, someone holding that key could read the record Stripe keeps for you:
- your name and email address
- your billing address, or the parts of it you gave us, and your tax ID if you entered one
- the brand, last four digits and expiry date of your card
- your subscription and invoice history
Your full card number and security code were not exposed. Stripe holds those and does not make them available to Defang or to anyone using our key.
What was not exposed
- Passwords. Defang has none for you: you sign in with GitHub, GitLab or an emailed code.
- Your cloud credentials. We never hold them. Your BYOC deployments run in your own account with your own credentials, and Defang's servers have no direct access to that account or to anything running in it. The one indirect path is the GitHub Actions deploy role described above.
- The contents of your repositories. They are not stored in our database.
- Config values you set with defang config. They stay in your cloud account, except for the copies in deployment state described above.
What we have done
- Replaced the Defang GitHub App key and secrets on November 23, and revoked the GitHub sign-in tokens we held.
- Replaced our other production credentials, including database, payment and email credentials, on November 23.
- Signed out every session and invalidated every Defang access token issued before November 23.
- Stopped keeping copies of deployment state on November 19 and deleted the copies we had.
- Cancelled every pending cloud invite link. If you were inviting someone, send a new invite.
- Notified GitHub, who are helping us audit for token misuse.
- Installed the security update on November 15.
- Revoked the development GitHub App's credentials and suspended the app on November 23.
- Reverted the production and staging deployment configurations that were altered by the attacker.
- Notified users and relevant authorities of the data breach.
What you should do
Everyone
- Be careful with email that claims to come from Defang, GitHub or GitLab, especially anything that asks you to sign in, approve an app or share a code. Someone holding this data can write a convincing message that uses your real name and username. We will never ask you for a password or a token by email.
- You will need to sign in to Defang again.
If you installed the Defang GitHub App: in GitHub
- Check your connected repositories for activity you don't recognize since November 11:
  - new commits, branches or tags
  - changes under .github/workflows
  - workflow runs you didn't start
  - changed Actions secrets or variables
  - new deploy keys or collaborators, and changed branch protection rules
- Look in particular at anything from defang-app[bot]. Your security log is at github.com/settings/security-log; organizations have an audit log under Settings.
- Lock down the deployment environments Defang created. In each connected repository, open Settings, then Environments. For every environment whose name starts with defang-, set "Deployment branches and tags" to your deploy branch only. You can also add required reviewers. This stops a workflow on any other branch from receiving your cloud credentials.
- Revoke and reconnect Defang's access to your GitHub account. In GitHub, go to Settings, then Applications, then Authorized GitHub Apps, and revoke Defang. Sign in to Defang again afterwards.
- If you no longer deploy from GitHub with Defang, uninstall the Defang app from your account or organization.
If you deploy from GitHub Actions: in your cloud account
- Check who has used your deploy role since November 11. The role or identity Defang set up for GitHub deploys has administrator access to your account. A token from a branch or workflow you don't deploy from is a sign of misuse.
  - On AWS this is usually defang-cd-CIRole. Search CloudTrail for AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity calls on it.
  - On Google Cloud, check the workload identity pool's token exchanges.
  - On Azure, check the sign-in logs for the managed identity.
- Narrow that role's trust to the branch or environment you deploy from, if it currently allows any branch.
If you deployed with Defang between October 12 and November 19
- Change your database passwords and any secret that was part of another environment variable, then redeploy. Use defang config set <NAME> for each one.
- If your Compose file has secrets written directly in it, change those too.
If you have billing details with us
You do not need to replace your card. The exposed details are not enough to charge it. Watch your statements as you normally would, and be wary of email or calls that quote your last four digits or billing address to appear legitimate.
If any of these checks turns up something you don't recognize
Rotate the secrets in that repository's Actions secrets and environments and the cloud credentials its workflows can reach, then tell us.
Frequently asked questions
Was my GitHub password exposed? Do I need to change it right away?
No. Defang never had your GitHub password. You sign in with GitHub, GitLab or an emailed code, so there is no password for us to lose.
What the attacker could use was the Defang GitHub App's key and the GitHub sign-in token we keep when you connect GitHub. We replaced the key and revoked those tokens on November 23. Changing your GitHub password is not required, but it does no harm, and turning on two-factor authentication is a good idea in any case. The checks that matter are the ones under "If you installed the Defang GitHub App: in GitHub" above: look for activity you don't recognize, lock down deployment environments, and revoke the Defang GitHub App.
Did they take only my information from GitHub, or did they go further? Did they get my email?
They got your email address, but not your email account. The address we have on file for you is in our database, so it was copied. Defang has no access to your mailbox, so the attacker could not read your email or sign in to your email account through us.
From GitHub, what we hold is your username, the names of the organizations your account belongs to and, if you installed the Defang GitHub App, the names of the repositories it can access. The contents of your repositories are not in our database.
If you installed the Defang GitHub App, the attacker also held the credentials that let them act in your repositories until we replaced them on November 23. We are still checking whether they were used after November 12, and we will update this post when we know more. That is why we ask you to review your repositories and your cloud account. "What the attacker could see" and "What the attacker could use" above list everything.
Did this happen only to me, or to all users?
Anyone with a Defang Portal account is in the database that was copied, so your email address, and potentially your name, was exposed along with everyone else's. Beyond that, what was exposed depends on how you use Defang. See above section for details.
How can I protect my data? How do I check that an email really comes from you?
- Don't sign in, approve an app or share a code because an email asked you to. Open the Defang Portal, GitHub or GitLab yourself by typing the address in your browser, rather than following a link in a message.
- We will never ask you for a password or a token by email.
- Someone holding this data can quote your real name, username or the organizations you belong to. A message that knows those details is not proof that it comes from us.
- If you are unsure about a message, forward it to sec@defang.io and we will tell you whether we sent it.
- Work through the checklist in "What you should do" above. In short: review your repositories, review your cloud account, change database passwords and secrets if you deployed between October 12 and November 19, and turn on two-factor authentication on your GitHub and cloud accounts.
How do I delete my account and data?
Open Settings in the Defang Portal and delete your account there, or email sec@defang.io and we will do it for you.
Contact us
If you find anything suspicious, or want an engineer to go through your account, repositories or cloud account with you, write to sec@defang.io. We will update this post as we learn more.
The Defang Team

If any of these checks turns up something you don't recognize
Rotate the secrets in that repository's Actions secrets and environments and the cloud credentials its workflows can reach, then tell us.
Frequently asked questions
Was my GitHub password exposed? Do I need to change it right away?
No. Defang never had your GitHub password. You sign in with GitHub, GitLab or an emailed code, so there is no password for us to lose.
What the attacker could use was the Defang GitHub App's key and the GitHub sign-in token we keep when you connect GitHub. We replaced the key and revoked those tokens on November 23. Changing your GitHub password is not required, but it does no harm, and turning on two-factor authentication is a good idea in any case. The checks that matter are the ones under "If you installed the Defang GitHub App: in GitHub" above: look for activity you don't recognize, lock down deployment environments, and revoke the Defang GitHub App.
Did they take only my information from GitHub, or did they go further? Did they get my email?
They got your email address, but not your email account. The address we have on file for you is in our database, so it was copied. Defang has no access to your mailbox, so the attacker could not read your email or sign in to your email account through us.
From GitHub, what we hold is your username, the names of the organizations your account belongs to and, if you installed the Defang GitHub App, the names of the repositories it can access. The contents of your repositories are not in our database.
If you installed the Defang GitHub App, the attacker also held the credentials that let them act in your repositories until we replaced them on November 23. We are still checking whether they were used after November 12, and we will update this post when we know more. That is why we ask you to review your repositories and your cloud account. "What the attacker could see" and "What the attacker could use" above list everything.
Did this happen only to me, or to all users?
Anyone with a Defang Portal account is in the database that was copied, so your email address, and potentially your name, was exposed along with everyone else's. Beyond that, what was exposed depends on how you use Defang. See above section for details.
How can I protect my data? How do I check that an email really comes from you?
- Don't sign in, approve an app or share a code because an email asked you to. Open the Defang Portal, GitHub or GitLab yourself by typing the address in your browser, rather than following a link in a message.
- We will never ask you for a password or a token by email.
- Someone holding this data can quote your real name, username or the organizations you belong to. A message that knows those details is not proof that it comes from us.
- If you are unsure about a message, forward it to sec@defang.io and we will tell you whether we sent it.
- Work through the checklist in "What you should do" above. In short: review your repositories, review your cloud account, change database passwords and secrets if you deployed between October 12 and November 19, and turn on two-factor authentication on your GitHub and cloud accounts.
How do I delete my account and data?
Open Settings in the Defang Portal and delete your account there, or email sec@defang.io and we will do it for you.
Contact us
If you find anything suspicious, or want an engineer to go through your account, repositories or cloud account with you, write to sec@defang.io. We will update this post as we learn more.
The Defang Team

Saudi National Charged In OpenAI Bugging To Be Released On $1 Million Bond
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
21:00:00 UTC
06:00:00 KST

Saudi National Charged In OpenAI Bugging To Be Released On $1 Million Bond (cnbc.com) 15
			
		 	
				CNBC reports:
Abdulrahman Alfozan, the Saudi national accused of bugging an OpenAI executive conference room, will be allowed to be released from jail on a $1 million bond, a San Francisco federal court judge ruled Tuesday.   Alfozan, 35, is expected to be released on Wednesday and be fitted with an ankle monitor, according to his attorney, David Rizk...  [U.S. District Court Magistrate Judge Alex Tse] ruled in favor of a release motion by Rizk filed last week that said prosecutors had admitted they had "found no new evidence to support the theory that Mr. Alfozan was stealing trade secrets, or to support the speculation that he was acting as an unregistered foreign agent for Saudi Arabia."
The San Francisco U.S. Attorney's Office opposed the former OpenAI engineer's release, saying in its response that he remains a flight risk due to his "considerable resources and ties to Saudi Arabia."  Alfozan's release request "blithely dismisses the seriousness" of the charge he faces, and "appears to presume, without support, that the government's investigation is complete," prosecutors wrote... Prosecutors said they have found hundreds of audio files on Alfozan's personal computer, "including
dozens of recordings of meetings or conversations that occurred at OpenAI throughout September 2026, many of which involve discussions of what may be trade secrets."

FCC approves SpaceX plan to put AI data centers in space
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
20:56:44 UTC
05:56:44 KST

SpaceX was given conditional approval by the Federal Communications Commission on Wednesday to deploy and operate a constellation of up to 1 million satellites for its orbital data center business, a regulatory victory for Elon Musk and his bid to move AI data centers into space.
The FCC authorization was granted despite objections from Amazon, Viasat, and the American Astronomical Society, which flagged concerns over orbital debris and congestion, radiofrequency interference, and environmental consequences. Amazon, which is building a competitor to SpaceX’s Starlink service, argued in its petition that the application lacked basic details and described it as a “lofty ambition rather than a real plan.” Despite that lack of detail, Amazon also said the proposal “risks real harm.”
The FCC dismissed some of the key concerns raised by opponents, including potential threats to scientific research, because the agency determined they fell outside its regulatory authority.
FCC Chair Brendan Carr said in a statement that the action “opens a bold new frontier for the computing infrastructure needed to power America’s leadership and growth.”
Musk thanked Carr in a post on X, writing “Much appreciate. This will be a great asset to America and the world.”
This initial regulatory approval doesn’t mean orbital data centers are imminent.
SpaceX faces a number of technical and economic challenges, including how to dramatically reduce the cost of manufacturing and launching satellites, cool processors in space, and even how to secure financing for chips that are destined for orbit.
The company must also clear other regulatory hurdles. The FCC has not yet approved SpaceX’s plans to handle orbital debris for satellites that would operate above 600 kilometers, for instance. SpaceX requested a waiver of existing FCC rules, which requires satellites ending missions below 2,000 kilometers reenter Earth’s atmosphere within five years of completing those missions. The company is interested in moving some of these retired satellites into other orbits around Earth or even around the sun, according to the FCC authorization document.
SpaceX has said it plans to deploy the satellites, known as Starmind, at altitudes ranging from 500 to 1,002 kilometers. The FCC’s approval gives SpaceX the authority to use radiofrequency communications to maintain contact with and control its orbital data centers from Earth if their primary communications systems fail. The Starmind satellites would primarily communicate using lasers to connect with other satellites, including SpaceX’s Starlink.
The FCC has given SpaceX time to sort out these issues. Under the approval, SpaceX has seven years to get its first operational satellite into orbit and 14 years to deploy the entire constellation.

Here’s where you can save (a little) on the Ocarina of Time remake
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
20:47:55 UTC

A lot of people have The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time for the Nintendo Switch 2 on their holiday wishlist. In case you haven’t bought it yet, I wanted to share some small but welcome deals happening on the physical and digital versions of the game. Costco members can currently grab the cartridge edition for $62.99, down from its regular price of $69.99 (it’s the only retailer currently discounting the physical version). Both Amazon and Costco are also selling the digital version for $54.99, a small $5 discount.
Here’s where you can save (a little) on the Ocarina of Time remake
In addition to GTA VI, Nintendo’s stellar Zelda remake is sure to be one of the most popular gaming gifts this holiday.
Ocarina of Time is one of Nintendo’s most groundbreaking titles, having served as its first 3D Zelda game when it launched in 1998. Parts of the original game haven’t aged particularly well — namely some dungeon design and the game’s tricky 3D navigation, since there was only one analog stick at the time — which makes it tough for some to enjoy going back to play the N64 version. Thankfully, the Switch 2 remake introduces several fixes, a host of quality-of-life improvements, and, of course, some lovely visuals that make it a graphics powerhouse by today’s standards.
If you go the digital route, a recent Switch 2 update lets you gift games to players in your family group. It doesn’t have to be your actual family, either, just up to eight people you designate as part of your group. The digital game will then appear gift-wrapped on their homescreen.

Slow Ubuntu security response times
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
20:38:00 UTC
05:38:00 KST

Message-ID: <20261125172638.73c0a05b@hboeck.de>
Date: Wed, 25 Nov 2026 17:26:38 +0100
From: Hanno Böck <hanno@...eck.de>
To: oss-security@...ts.openwall.com
Subject: Slow Ubuntu security response times
Hello,
Lately, I'm observing that Ubuntu's response to severe security flaws
is concerning.
On November 16th, details about a remote memory corruption called
NoisyTCP (CVE-2026-99288) in the Linux kernel's TCP stack were
published: https://www.openwall.com/lists/oss-security/2026/11/15/2
Today, nine days later, Ubuntu still has not published a fix yet:
https://ubuntu.com/security/CVE-2026-99288
Other distros had a fix already available when the details were
published.
Likewise, a KVM host escape (CVE-2026-99312) affecting AMD
virtualization published on Nov 18th has no fix yet either [3]:
https://ubuntu.com/security/CVE-2026-99312
Ubuntu has been slow in patching another remote TCP corruption
(CVE-2026-98432) lately, where it took multiple weeks until a fix was
available. See also: https://chalmovsky.com/posts/wtf-ubuntu
Recent news indicates that this is might be exploited in the wild:
https://www.greynoise.io/blog/check-point-security-management-servers-intrusion-campaign
I think everyone understands that these times aren't easy for security
teams. Nevertheless, remote corruption vulnerabilities in the Linux
kernel are the kind of thing where I expect updates to be available
within hours, not days or weeks.
On a recent Ubuntu LTS system, the kernel version is 7.0.0-44. This
seems to indicate that Ubuntu is not relying on longterm supported
kernels from kernel.org, but is instead backporting patches to an old
kernel version. On a Debian stable system, the kernel version is
6.12.118+deb13, indicating that it's based on a very recent longterm
kernel from kernel.org. I would consider the backporting strategy for
the kernel to be hardly viable and probably everyone should base their
Linux kernels on longterm releases supported by the Linux kernel
developers.
-- 
Hanno Böck - Independent security researcher
https://itsec.hboeck.de/
https://badkeys.info/
Powered by blists - more mailing lists
Please check out the Open Source Software Security Wiki, which is counterpart to this mailing list.
Confused about mailing lists and their use? Read about mailing lists on Wikipedia and check out these guidelines on proper formatting of your messages.
