Nfs2root: A 12-year-old pre-auth remote root in the Linux kernel's NFS server
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
20:35:50 UTC
05:35:50 KST

Port 2049 sits open on file servers, NAS appliances, build farms and Kubernetes storage nodes. Since 2014, the Linux kernel listening on it has let anyone who can reach it write into kernel memory before logging in. We pointed OpenSec's Antara at it. It came back with a root shell.
Pre-auth heap overflow in the Linux NFS server (nfsd)
Fixed in Linux 6.12.117, 6.18.61, 7.3.2 and 7.4-rc1. Update the kernel and reboot.
What you need to know
- What: a pre-auth heap overflow in the kernel's NFS server, nfsd.
- Who: any Linux host serving NFSv4.1 or 4.2 (the default) on a reachable tcp/2049.
- Impact: one TCP connection writes 61 bytes past a kernel heap object; chained, that gives a root shell.
- Now: patch and reboot. Until then, firewall tcp/2049.
Key facts
- Severity 9.8 Critical (AV:N/AC:L/PR:N/UI:N)
- Attack Remote, unauthenticated, tcp/2049
- Impact Kernel heap overflow → remote root shell
- Bug age 12 years, since Linux 3.16 (2014)
- Affected In-kernel nfsd with NFSv4.1 or 4.2
- Fixed in 6.12.117 · 6.18.61 · 7.3.2 · 7.4-rc1
One connection, no login
The NFS server runs inside the kernel and parses network input before it knows who you are. CVE-2026-99362 lives in that gap: a client can shrink its reply cache to zero bytes, then make the server copy a full reply into it. The copy lands on the kernel heap, and the client picks most of the bytes.
Twelve years in a few lines of C
Timeline
- Commit 47ee52986472 narrows the reply buffer while handling SEQUENCE. It ships in Linux 3.16.
- 12 yearsEvery kernel since 3.16 carries it: LTS, distribution and appliance kernels.
- Jérémy Jean of ANSSI sends a fix upstream with a KASAN report.
- OpenSec's Antara flags the bug, scores it 9.8 and turns it into a root shell on its own.
- CVE-2026-99362 is published with fixes.
Watch it run
A real run, end to end. Left: the victim's console. Right: our client on another machine, talking to the victim only over tcp/2049.
Root cause
Each NFSv4.1 session slot caches its last reply, so a retried request is answered exactly once. The client decides how big that cache is.
Set it to the minimum and every slot's buffer is zero bytes. nfsd4_sequence() then shrinks the reply buffer to that limit before it accepts the slot. Pad the request's tag so the buffer fills right after the SEQUENCE opcode, and the encoder quits without flagging an error. The server believes SEQUENCE succeeded and caches the whole reply, headers and tag included, into a buffer with room for nothing.
That is 80 bytes copied, 61 of them past the end of the heap object, 40 of them chosen by the client.
The fix checks that the reply fits before taking the slot, and makes the encoder report what it can't fit.
From overflow to root
The exploit stays private. Each step is an ordinary NFS request:
- Overflow The write corrupts the neighbouring slot's bookkeeping.
- Leak A replay request returns 3.8 KB of kernel heap, and with it the KASLR slide.
- Free A forged pointer makes the kernel free an object we choose.
- Reclaim We refill that memory with a fake callback built from NFS client names.
- Trigger We hang up. nfsd calls the fake callback, and the kernel runs /bin/sh as root.
In the recording, that command is whoami; id; poweroff -f: the victim prints root, then uid=0(root), and switches itself off.
Found and proven by a machine
Antara, OpenSec's white-box harness, flagged the nfsd reply-cache path, scored it 9.8, and built the leak, the arbitrary free and the root shell shown in the video by itself: end to end, with no human in the loop.
Kernel hardening does not remove the overflow. Patch every host.
Am I exposed?
Tick what's true for your host. All four means exposed, today.
Am I exposed?
What to do
Patch and reboot
On a distribution kernel, install the update that lists CVE-2026-99362.
Can't patch today? Close 2049
Or turn off NFSv4.1 and 4.2
Then restart nfs-server. This turns off pNFS and some 4.2 features.
Hunting for it
The attack is valid NFS, so there is no network signature. Look for:
- Kernel warnings from nfsd4_check_resp_size, on every attempt, successful or not.
- Oopses with nfsd4_*, svc_* or call_usermodehelper* frames.
- Shells spawned by kernel workers on a file server.
- Bursts of short NFSv4.1 sessions from one source.
Disclosure
We reported the bug to the NFSD maintainers and security@kernel.org on 22 September 2026 with the full remote-root exploit, and sent a patch the same day. CVE-2026-99362 was published with fixes on 20 November.
Credits
- OpenSec Intelligence: found the bug with Antara, which escalated it to pre-auth root on its own, and reported it with the full exploit and a patch.
- Jérémy Jean (ANSSI): found it independently and wrote the upstream fix, 5d5a72e965c8 and 241848cc774e.
- Chuck Lever and Jeff Layton: the NFSD maintainers.
References
nfsd: preflight SEQUENCE replies before accepting a slotThe upstream fix for CVE-2026-99362, by Jérémy Jean.github.com nfsd: set op->status when an operation's header cannot be encodedThe follow-up guard in the NFSv4 encoder.github.com
- Introduced by 47ee52986472 (Linux 3.16)
- Patch thread
- OpenSec's patch submission

====================================================================================================

WhatsApp’s new scheduled messages let you choose the right time to send
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
20:31:48 UTC
05:31:48 KST

WhatsApp’s 3 billion users are getting an option that Meta says is one of the most requested features, with the ability to schedule messages in advance for both private conversations and group chats. Most messaging platforms have had this for a very long time, but now it’s on WhatsApp too.
WhatsApp’s new scheduled messages let you choose the right time to send
You can schedule WhatsApp texts you might forget to send up to two weeks ahead of time.
If you think of a text at an inconvenient time or don’t want to forget to fire off a birthday message at 4AM, you can draft it and set it to send anywhere from 10 minutes to two weeks later. The feature works with photos and videos, too.
Meta:
To schedule a message, press and hold the send button and pick the date and time you want it to arrive. It will look like any other message to the person receiving it. You can see all future messages you have lined up, and delete them beforehand if you change your mind.
Even if your phone is off or out of battery, your scheduled message will still show up at the time you chose, and it remains end-to-end encrypted like all your personal messages, so no one else can read it, not even us.
Scheduled messages work with text, photos and videos, and are rolling out globally over the coming weeks. It’s one more way to show up for the people who matter to you, now at exactly the right time.

The Big Idea: Alma Alexander
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
20:12:36 UTC
05:12:36 KST

The Big Idea: Alma Alexander
Posted on November 25, 2026 Posted by Athena Scalzi 2 Comments
Real life mythos and legends often play a role in creating a fantasy world 0f one’s own, and author Alma Alexander has an especially unique myth0logy (one that is personal to her) that she is drawing from for the first book in her new trilogy, The Shadow Before the Dawn.
ALMA ALEXANDER:
Back in the 12th century, a quondam poet wrote an epic that contained the line, “There are only three subject matters for any discerning man: that of France, that of Britain, and that of Great Rome.” Possibly the most immediately familiar one of these would be the Matter of Britain – you will know it as the body of medieval literature associated with Great and with Less Britain (Brittany, for those who recognize it better by that name), and concerned with the legendary kings and heroes associated with it, King Arthur, Lancelot, the Knights of the Round Table, the Holy Grail, all that (mostly, blame Geoffrey of Monmouth). The Matter of France deals with Charlemagne and his paladins, and their wars with Moors and Saracens. The Matter of Rome, or Res Romana, is a more rarefied elixir of Greek and Roman mythology, which includes the Matter of Troy (hence the Odyssey and the Iliad), and which focused on military heroes like Alexander the Great and Julius Caesar.
But although those three were “anointed”, as it were, there are very much other “Matters” out there, for various countries and cultures. We might argue about whether Wagner muddied the waters with the Ring of the Nibelungen and twisted Matters of Norse origin and of German origin together in a Gordian knot; the Matter of Spain could be – well – maybe El Cid (Spanish people, feel free to correct me); we might definitely argue about the Matter of America, and about ideas it might contain, such as Manifest Destiny. The Matters of Countries are the invisible backbones upon which the shape of their existence hangs.
There is a Matter hidden deep in Eastern Europe, my own Matter, which talks of a line of Kings who walked into being a line of Saints, who created a kingdom out of a smattering of feeble little fiefdoms, and then forged it into an empire, and then fought a Last Battle in which everything they wrought in the Earthly realm was destroyed but which wrote their lineage into the Heavenly ledgers forever and gave their people the light they needed in order to survive the 500 years of slavery that followed their loss in that Last Battle. I am talking about the Nemanjic Dynasty in the 12th/13th centuries Balkans.
For a long time I have wanted to go back to that ancient tree and pick its fruit. This is my own Matter, the one that belongs to my own background, to my own people. It is a period stuffed with history, mystery, miracles, myths, legends, and larger-than-life characters, ripe for the plucking, and one which is rather less well known to the English-language reading public than the more easily accessible Matters out there. I started on a version of this story years ago, and wrote about 20,000 words of it, and then Life fell on me like a landslide and I lost in quick succession my husband, my mother, and a 19-year-old cat (don’t just dismiss this last; I had raised her from a 9-week-old kitten who weighed less than what it would take to make a decent hamburger when she came into my hands, and her loss HURT).
For six years I was a caregiver and then (in increasingly dense layers) someone who mourned and grieved, and all of that leaves little room for telling stories… so I kind of stopped writing. At all. And then, somehow, I picked up those twenty thousand words and looked at them again. And realized that I needed to tell another story before the story that I set out to tell in the first place. And then I realized that I needed to tell another story before THAT, to give weight and stability to the Matter I was addressing. And just like that, I had a trilogy on my hands.
The Shadow Before the Dawn, book 1 in what became that trilogy, was finally fully formed in 2026. The other books are in process. But that first book… this story matters to me, right from that beginning, deeply, organically. Sometimes I think everything I have ever written was training for this story, these books, to which I feel a true responsibility and which I am dedicated to giving the respect and dignity that they deserve.
Because I am writing a Matter. A story that shaped a nation.
You might not know this story.
Come with me, and let me open the doors into a world full of a magic which may be rich and strange and unfamiliar to you. It isn’t to me. This is my Matter, and it runs in my blood. I hope, when you are done reading this, that you will judge that I have done justice to it.
The Shadow Before the Dawn: Amazon

OpenAI says heavy ChatGPT users may hit limits sooner as it merges Chat and Work
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
20:03:27 UTC
05:03:27 KST

OpenAI has started rolling out a unified Chat and Work experience in ChatGPT to a few of its users, amid criticism from some subscribers that the merger could restrict their usage.
The ChatGPT maker began introducing the update to a small group of Pro subscribers, product lead Thibault “Tibo” Sottiaux said in a post on X on Tuesday. The rollout follows OpenAI’s earlier announcement of the merger in an email to users. An OpenAI spokesperson told TechCrunch that while most users are not expected to face more frequent usage resets, a small fraction of its heaviest users may hit their limits sooner.
That’s among the concerns raised by some ChatGPT subscribers on social media and OpenAI’s community forum recently. The users fear that merging the usage limits for Chat and Work could leave them with less access to advanced reasoning models than they currently have, even as they continue paying the same subscription fees.
Under the new system, Chat and Work will share a single usage meter across ChatGPT, the OpenAI spokesperson told TechCrunch. Conversations using the Instant model will, however, remain unlimited and will not count toward the shared allowance. Users can also continue using Instant after exhausting their usage limits. The spokesperson added that OpenAI would continue working to improve efficiency so subscribers could get more out of their plans.
While the rollout has started for a small group of users, OpenAI plans to bring shared usage limits more broadly in December, per its support page.
Similar to OpenAI, rival Anthropic also moved to bring its conversational chat service Claude and work-focused Cowork under one interface in September.
The changes come as AI companies face mounting infrastructure costs and growing demand for increasingly capable models, putting pressure on their computing resources. Bringing conversational and work-related tools into a single interface could also make AI chatbots simpler to use, allowing users to move between chats and more complex tasks without switching tabs.

Tuesday Jensen Huang Said Nvidia Doesn't Need a Succession Plan, and that He's in Therapy

Tuesday Jensen Huang Said Nvidia Doesn't Need a Succession Plan, and that He's in Therapy 87
			
		 	
				CNBC reports:
[Jensen Huang] told staff that Nvidia has built a team that doesn't require succession planning the way a normal organization does, because the company operates a unique business model where there are multiple capable leaders at Nvidia that are leading different parts of the organization.  A succession plan would create unnecessary hierarchy, Huang said, according to one of the people present...
Huang told staff that Tuesday's company meeting was the second hardest meeting of his life, the first one being with his wife. He said he is in therapy, according to one of the people present.   
The Independent notes that Jensen Huang told Nvidia's board Sunday "that he had more affairs beyond the two that were already in the public domain, The Wall Street Journal reported, citing people familiar with the matter."
Huang was reportedly "contrite" about his behavior during the crunch talks, and the board discussed "the importance of candor and honesty" with him, according to the newspaper.  
Elsewhere CNBC reports even President Trump's "staunchest allies" in the U.S. Congress "recoiled after the reinstatement and vowed to continue their investigations into Huang..."
"The risk remains that there could be information that a Chinese spy knows about a key individual in the supply chain, and that's not worth the risk," Sen. Kevin Cramer, R-N.D., said Tuesday on CNBC's "Squawk Box." "We might even have to put a babysitter on Jensen Huang for crying out loud ... just to make sure that he doesn't do something that's going to risk the United States...."

Spending 35B Opus 5.5 Tokens to Write a Symphony
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
19:57:08 UTC
04:57:08 KST

Back to blog
Opus 5.5 writes its Magnum Opus (2500 times)
A four-movement symphony for full orchestra. Claude wrote it, recorded it and made the video, over about 2,500 rounds of review.
  "I fear not the man the man who one shots 2500 symphonies with Claude, rather I fear the man who 2500 shots a single symphony with Claude"
So, I got bored and wanted to push Opus to its limit with respect to music composition. Basically, I did the meme:
You can watch a full virtual instrument rendering of the composition on youtube here, which is likely higher quality than the version you'd get streamed from x.com:
YouTube picks the picture quality from your connection and the size of the player. For a sharper score, choose 1080p or higher under the gear icon, or go full screen.
- 4movements
- 929bars
- 22,979notes
- 25 minof music
- 35BOpus tokens, roughly
The full scores, as PDF:
The source of the scores, the critics' reviews and the scripts are on GitHub: github.com/halfprice06/claude-symphony
These are virtual instruments, so the audio quality is not as good as if an orchestra recorded this live. But its pretty good, and considering Claude did all of the mockup and rendering in a DAW with pro-level VSTs, I'd say the result is still pretty impressive.
The whole thing used around 35 billion Opus tokens.
We used Claude Code Projects (beta) for this, and I find it really useful for project-based coding. The orchestrator / thread model worked really well for us in practice. Generally, each movement was owned by a thread.
I thought the process I used to achieve this was pretty interesting, so I decided to write it up.
tl;dr - For things that can't be 'verified' as good or bad like art/music, just make the agent do more iterations, way more iterations than you think.
There were three jobs: writing the symphony, turning the score into a recording, and making the video. I'll go through them in that order.
Part 1: Writing the symphony
Claude Opus 5.5 composed a four-movement symphony for full orchestra, about 25 minutes of music, in one month. This is how it was done.
The music is written as text
  Claude cannot easily click notes onto a staff, but it can write text. So the whole score was written in LilyPond, a free program that turns typed text into printed sheet music. A note is a letter and a number: c4 is a C that lasts a quarter note. From the same text, LilyPond also produces a basic computer playback.
Because the score is text, Claude could edit it, compare two versions, and check it with small programs, the way programmers work on code.
The first theme of Movement I, as Claude typed it
These eight bars for the first violins are copied from the score's source text. Point at a note in the text or on the staff to find it in the other. Click a note to hear it.
Swipe the staff sideways to see all of it.
"Only these notes" is a simple synthesizer in your browser playing the violin line alone. The recording is the same eight bars from the final mockup, with the whole orchestra.
One Claude writes, another reviews
The method is called an actor-critic loop. One Claude, the composer, writes the music. Another Claude, the critic, reviews it.
- The composer writes a version of a movement.
- A new critic, which has never seen the piece before, studies it and suggests specific fixes.
- The composer decides which fixes to accept.
- The result becomes the next version, and a new critic starts again.
Each critic looked at one subject only, such as melody, harmony, the use of the instruments, or whether the parts are playable. A written log recorded every decision, so that a later critic would not undo an earlier one.
Each movement went through this loop about 600 times. With four movements, that's about 2,400 rounds.
Originally I only planned to do this looping process around 50 times. But once I hit 50, it seemed like the agent was still improving. So, I just kept going. This entire process was mostly autonomous. I just prompted Claude to keep going.
Every round, from the written log
Each square is one round. Time runs from left to right, ten rounds per column, and each of the ten rows is one critic's subject. Pick a square to read that round's line in the log.
Swipe the grid sideways to see later rounds.
Loading the log…
    the log records a change
    the log says nothing was changed
These are the real lines from the log, cut to about 200 characters. They are written in the working shorthand of the loop, with bar numbers and instrument names.
  
Critics had to read the textbooks
A critic's opinion was not enough. I supplied standard textbooks on orchestration, harmony and music notation. From then on, each critic was assigned a chapter and had to show where the score followed it and where it did not.
One example: an orchestration textbook says that in loud passages two horns are needed to balance one trumpet. A critic checked every loud brass chord against that rule, found three chords where an important note was too weak, and proposed fixes. The composer accepted all three.
Great symphonies as models
Claude also collected scores by composers such as Beethoven, Dvořák and Tchaikovsky. Every tenth critic compared a whole movement with one movement by a master and answered one question: would a conductor believe a master wrote this?
What I did
I set the goal: a symphony that could pass for the work of a master, with melodies people remember. I chose the method, supplied the books and decided how long the loops ran. I left the musical choices to Claude.
I was also the only one who could listen. Claude cannot hear audio, so it judged the music from the written score. I listened to computer playbacks and asked for a small number of changes.
Version 1 next to the final version
I thought it would be neat to compare version 1 of each movement against the final version. There are no high quality recordings of version 1, so both are played here with the same basic MIDI sounds, which is the closest thing to compare.
Version 1 against the final version
The strip is the final version of the movement, one segment per bar. The brighter a bar, the more of its notes are new or changed since version 1. Click anywhere on the strip to play from that bar, and switch between the two versions while it plays.
A note counts as kept when version 1 has the same pitch at the same place in the matching bar. A pitch played by several instruments at once counts once. A program matched the bars of the two versions, so the shading is approximate. Both versions are set to the same volume.
Version 1 of Movement I was a sketch for strings only, and its first theme was different. In Movements II, III and IV the main themes begin with the same notes in version 1 and in the final version, and the bright bars on the strips show where the changes are. By the count described above, about 14% of the notes in the final Movement I were already in version 1, 35% in Movement II, 61% in Movement III and 55% in Movement IV.
What to keep in mind
Claude wrote 99.9% of every single element of the composition. I maybe influenced a measure or two of note selection over the whole process.
Part 2: Turning the score into a recording
A written score doesn't make any sound, so the next job was a recording.
The recordings of the symphony are not a live orchestra. They are mockups: every note was recorded in advance by real players, and software plays those recordings back in the order the score asks for. This is how the four mockups, about 25 minutes of music, were made.
The tools
Two programs did the work, both on my Mac. REAPER is a music production program. It holds one track per instrument, mixes them together and saves the result as an audio file. EastWest Hollywood Orchestra is the library of recorded notes. (These are the DAW and the pro-level VSTs I mentioned at the top.)
Claude did not click around in either program. It wrote scripts that read the score and built each REAPER project from it. The score is a text file, written in LilyPond, so a script can read every note and every marking directly.
Claude cannot hear
A person making a mockup listens, adjusts, and listens again. Claude cannot listen. It can only measure the audio file. So after every recording it checked things that can be measured:
- Is the melody louder than the accompaniment?
- Does each note start on the beat?
- When a loud chord stops, does the sound die away as fast as it does on a real orchestra recording?
The loop
Each movement was improved in rounds. Every round had the same four steps:
- Record the movement.
- Three critics measure the recording. Each critic is a separate Claude with one job. One checks how the notes are played. One checks the balance between instruments. One checks something different each round, such as the timing or the sound of the room.
- Each critic writes a short list of the worst problems, with evidence and a suggested fix.
- Another Claude decides which fixes to make, changes the scripts and records again.
The four movements took 184 rounds in total.
The opening of the finale at six stages
The same 18 bars, from the basic computer playback to the final recording. Pick a stage to hear it. You can switch while it plays.
All six clips are set to the same volume here, so the differences you hear are in the playing and the mix. As first made, rounds 0 to 11 were 5 to 6 dB quieter than rounds 20 and 30.
My listening
The measurements missed things, and I caught them by listening. I said the long timpani rolls were drowning out other instruments. I said the accents were too harsh, the sound was "too wet" and "a bit mushy", repeated notes were hard to tell apart, and the recordings were too quiet on AirPods.
Each of my notes became a rule, and a new thing for the critics to measure. Two examples:
- Too wet. The reverb was cut in half and shortened.
- A volume change on almost every note. I opened a project and saw that the loop had been fixing problems by moving the volume fader note by note. No person works that way. All of those moves were removed, and the problems were fixed the way a person would fix them: by changing how the instrument plays the note.
Learning from people who do this by hand
About a month in, we studied how professionals make mockups. The sources were YouTube tutorials by film-score mixers, the sample library maker's own template project, and project files that composers have shared for others to study. Three lessons changed the recordings most: use less reverb, shape each phrase so it rises and falls, and leave a small gap between repeated notes.
Part 3: Making the video
The last job was the video at the YouTube link above.
Each video shows the orchestra's sheet music as a dark page. Every note lights up at the moment it sounds, in a color for its instrument, and a small light hops from note to note along each instrument's line. A program worked all of this out from the written score and the recording.
Finding the notes
Like I said above, the score is written as text in LilyPond. For the videos, LilyPond printed each movement as one long line and also wrote down where on the page it put every note. That gave the video program the exact place of all 22,979 notes.
Staying in time
The recording speeds up and slows down, as any performance does. REAPER, the software that made the recording, keeps a list of every change of speed. The video program uses that list to work out the second at which each note sounds. The lights land within two hundredths of a second of the sound.
Pointing the camera
An orchestra's score is too tall to fit on the screen, so the camera shows only part of the page. It goes to the instruments that are loudest in the recording at that moment. The program plans the camera's whole route before it draws anything, so the camera moves seldom and arrives before the passage it is moving to begins. When two instruments far apart on the page matter at the same time, the screen splits in two.
Words on the page
Each instrument's name is printed on the page where it starts to play. Short labels, 124 in all, point out what a music theory class would: a theme coming back, an unusual chord, a quotation from an earlier movement. One Claude wrote the labels from the score, and a second Claude checked every one against the notes.
Watching and fixing
I watched each version and said what looked wrong. The camera jerked around. The instrument names shifted about. The opening took 19 seconds to reach the first sound. Each comment led to a fix, and the videos were made five times in two days. The four movements were then joined into one 25-minute video for YouTube, and its music now starts in the first second.
Adding it up
So that's the whole process. Add it up and it's about 2,400 rounds on the score, 184 on the recordings and 5 on the video. That's roughly the 2500 in the title.
None of those rounds had a pass/fail test, because there isn't one for whether music is good. What I could do was give each critic one narrow thing to check, make it back up its opinion with a textbook or a measurement, and run the loop way more times than seemed necessary. Then I listened to the result myself, because Claude can't.
Credits
- Composer: Opus 5.5
- Assistant for the final mockup recordings and the video: Fable
- Human overseer: Dan Price (me)
GitHub repo
The scores, the reviews and the scripts from this project are on GitHub: github.com/halfprice06/claude-symphony
For orchestras
If you are with a professional symphony orchestra and might be interested in performing this live, please reach out to me. I'm on X at x.com/gooby_esq, or you can email me at [email protected].

EastWest Hollywood Orchestra is the library of recorded notes. (These are the DAW and the pro-level VSTs I mentioned at the top.)
Claude did not click around in either program. It wrote scripts that read the score and built each REAPER project from it. The score is a text file, written in LilyPond, so a script can read every note and every marking directly.
Claude cannot hear
A person making a mockup listens, adjusts, and listens again. Claude cannot listen. It can only measure the audio file. So after every recording it checked things that can be measured:
- Is the melody louder than the accompaniment?
- Does each note start on the beat?
- When a loud chord stops, does the sound die away as fast as it does on a real orchestra recording?
The loop
Each movement was improved in rounds. Every round had the same four steps:
- Record the movement.
- Three critics measure the recording. Each critic is a separate Claude with one job. One checks how the notes are played. One checks the balance between instruments. One checks something different each round, such as the timing or the sound of the room.
- Each critic writes a short list of the worst problems, with evidence and a suggested fix.
- Another Claude decides which fixes to make, changes the scripts and records again.
The four movements took 184 rounds in total.
The opening of the finale at six stages
The same 18 bars, from the basic computer playback to the final recording. Pick a stage to hear it. You can switch while it plays.
All six clips are set to the same volume here, so the differences you hear are in the playing and the mix. As first made, rounds 0 to 11 were 5 to 6 dB quieter than rounds 20 and 30.
My listening
The measurements missed things, and I caught them by listening. I said the long timpani rolls were drowning out other instruments. I said the accents were too harsh, the sound was "too wet" and "a bit mushy", repeated notes were hard to tell apart, and the recordings were too quiet on AirPods.
Each of my notes became a rule, and a new thing for the critics to measure. Two examples:
- Too wet. The reverb was cut in half and shortened.
- A volume change on almost every note. I opened a project and saw that the loop had been fixing problems by moving the volume fader note by note. No person works that way. All of those moves were removed, and the problems were fixed the way a person would fix them: by changing how the instrument plays the note.
Learning from people who do this by hand
About a month in, we studied how professionals make mockups. The sources were YouTube tutorials by film-score mixers, the sample library maker's own template project, and project files that composers have shared for others to study. Three lessons changed the recordings most: use less reverb, shape each phrase so it rises and falls, and leave a small gap between repeated notes.
Part 3: Making the video
The last job was the video at the YouTube link above.
Each video shows the orchestra's sheet music as a dark page. Every note lights up at the moment it sounds, in a color for its instrument, and a small light hops from note to note along each instrument's line. A program worked all of this out from the written score and the recording.
Finding the notes
Like I said above, the score is written as text in LilyPond. For the videos, LilyPond printed each movement as one long line and also wrote down where on the page it put every note. That gave the video program the exact place of all 22,979 notes.
Staying in time
The recording speeds up and slows down, as any performance does. REAPER, the software that made the recording, keeps a list of every change of speed. The video program uses that list to work out the second at which each note sounds. The lights land within two hundredths of a second of the sound.
Pointing the camera
An orchestra's score is too tall to fit on the screen, so the camera shows only part of the page. It goes to the instruments that are loudest in the recording at that moment. The program plans the camera's whole route before it draws anything, so the camera moves seldom and arrives before the passage it is moving to begins. When two instruments far apart on the page matter at the same time, the screen splits in two.
Words on the page
Each instrument's name is printed on the page where it starts to play. Short labels, 124 in all, point out what a music theory class would: a theme coming back, an unusual chord, a quotation from an earlier movement. One Claude wrote the labels from the score, and a second Claude checked every one against the notes.
Watching and fixing
I watched each version and said what looked wrong. The camera jerked around. The instrument names shifted about. The opening took 19 seconds to reach the first sound. Each comment led to a fix, and the videos were made five times in two days. The four movements were then joined into one 25-minute video for YouTube, and its music now starts in the first second.
Adding it up
So that's the whole process. Add it up and it's about 2,400 rounds on the score, 184 on the recordings and 5 on the video. That's roughly the 2500 in the title.
None of those rounds had a pass/fail test, because there isn't one for whether music is good. What I could do was give each critic one narrow thing to check, make it back up its opinion with a textbook or a measurement, and run the loop way more times than seemed necessary. Then I listened to the result myself, because Claude can't.
Credits
- Composer: Opus 5.5
- Assistant for the final mockup recordings and the video: Fable
- Human overseer: Dan Price (me)
GitHub repo
The scores, the reviews and the scripts from this project are on GitHub: github.com/halfprice06/claude-symphony
For orchestras
If you are with a professional symphony orchestra and might be interested in performing this live, please reach out to me. I'm on X at x.com/gooby_esq, or you can email me at [email protected].

Jensen Huang broke Nvidia’s code of conduct, and his employees may not forget it
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
19:54:57 UTC
04:54:57 KST

When Jensen Huang gave his first all-hands address since returning as CEO, there was an odd emphasis on Nvidia’s code of conduct. He admitted to violating the code, then emphasized that no one was exempt from it. But that “does not mean, the moment you violate a code of conduct, we walk you out the door,” Huang said, according to Wired. “Facts, circumstances, a lot of assessments” still matter. In other words, the code applies to everyone, but breaking it doesn’t automatically cost anyone their job, including, apparently, the CEO.
It’s an awkward stance for a company whose CEO has admitted to breaking its rules. A central part of modern human resources, a code of conduct can seem like a quasi-legal rulebook that lays out unacceptable behavior. But experts say most codes are better understood as a reflection of corporate culture. When an infraction goes unpunished, the main risk isn’t legal but cultural — that employees will feel embarrassed or even angry about the company they work for. That’s the risk Huang now faces at Nvidia.
Matt Plass, chief market strategy officer at LRN, an ethics and compliance training outfit, recently surveyed more than 2,000 workers across different industries for a study on workplace codes of conduct. As Plass sees it, the biggest risk is that employees will become ashamed of their workplace.
“People coming into the workforce now, ethics is incredibly important to them,” he told TechCrunch. “They’re really mindful of the ethical standards of their company. They’re really embarrassed and angry when senior leadership lets them down.”
The consequences of violating the rules aren’t always tangible, but they’re definitely real. Plass gave one example of a company that banned corporate travel to cut costs — only to have its CEO turn up in a VIP box at the Olympics.
“For over a decade afterwards, ‘the Olympics’ was used within that company as an example of the worst thing an employee could do. People would say, ‘Yeah, I shouldn’t have done that, but it’s not like I went to the Olympics.’”
Plass says companies that remove an executive typically do so after an investigation, then try to win back employees. “In most organizations they make a change at the top based on what’s happened,” he said, “and then they will have a hearts-and-minds initiative, to go out to their employee base and their customers and their investors and everybody else, and say, we get it. We’re sorry. We’ve changed. That’s essentially what they do.”
Of course, that playbook is harder to follow at a company like Nvidia, where Huang left without a clear succession plan, and there are serious doubts about whether the company could continue to thrive without him. In cases like that, Plass acknowledges there’s no easy answer.
“I would draw a distinction there between the kind of companies where the CEO is a kind of godhead figure … where the person and the company are almost impossible to separate,” he said. “There it’s much, much harder to deal with.”
In that context, Huang’s stutter-step over the code of conduct is easier to understand. Judging by the employee campaign for his reinstatement, Huang’s return hasn’t created a morale problem so far. But as the coming months put more pressure on Huang and his colleagues, sidestepping the code could end up costing Nvidia something harder to measure — its employees’ pride in the company.

====================================================================================================

The canceled Tesla Cybertruck is the fastest-selling used truck in America
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
19:54:32 UTC
04:54:32 KST

Tesla Cybertruck sales never reached the lofty heights CEO Elon Musk predicted when it launched. But the futuristic, steel-clad EV is finding new life in the used car market, according to analysis of sales figures by iSeeCars.com.
The automotive search engine and research platform analyzed more than 1.1 million sales of one- to five-year-old used vehicles in October 2026 and measured the average days on market by drivetrain, vehicle type, and individual model. The upshot: The Tesla Cybertruck was the fastest-selling used truck, averaging just 39.1 days on the market compared with 65.5 days for trucks overall. Used Cybertrucks sold faster than any other truck, including the Ford Maverick Hybrid, which held the No. 2 spot, and the Rivian R1T, in third place.
According to iSeeCars.com, the Cybertruck’s sales performance lines up with a broader pattern in the used vehicle market: Used battery electric vehicles are hot sellers right now, with 16 of the 25 fastest-selling used cars fully electric. EVs are even edging out hybrids in terms of average days on market. EVs average 45.6 days on market compared to 54.5 days for hybrids and 61 days for gasoline-powered vehicles. Not every EV is selling well, however. For instance, iSeeCars.com found that the Cadillac Escalade IQ was the slowest-selling vehicle in the study, averaging 134.3 days on the market, more than twice the overall industry average.
Karl Brauer, executive analyst at iSeeCars.com, told TechCrunch that high gasoline prices have changed consumer-buying habits, prompting more people to opt for an EV or hybrid over an internal combustion engine vehicle. If high prices persist, as they have since March, that trend could continue.
Keep in mind that these were October sales, the month before Musk announced Tesla would stop making the Cybertruck in 2027. Fuel prices and the relative rarity of Cybertrucks in the used car market were driving demand even before the cancellation announcement, Brauer said.
“The Cybertruck has only been out for a few years, so you have a lot of used ones coming off lease and entering the used car market for the first time,” he said, adding that used Cybertrucks are cheap relative to their MSRP.
The impending cancellation will likely fuel used Cybertruck sales, although the pace may ebb and flow over the next year.
“The canceling of the new Cybertruck is making people kind of aware — it’s like, oh wow, these things aren’t going to be coming anymore,” Brauer said. “Whatever demand is for them, supply will not be continuing to grow.”
Brauer said that once production of the Cybertruck ends — which is expected in summer 2027 — sales of used versions may pop once again.
“When the last one is being produced, that’ll probably reignite things again, and for a few months after that,” he said.

EPA reports ‘zero’ airborne lead following controlled Army detonation in Iowa
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
19:47:46 UTC
04:47:46 KST

Saying it was necessary to prevent the possible spontaneous explosion of a stockpile of material used in igniters and blasting caps, the US Army conducted a controlled detonation of over 9,700 pounds of lead azide today at the Iowa Army Ammunition Plant (IAAAP) in Middletown, Iowa. Videos of the detonation showed plumes of smoke several hundred feet high drifting south before quickly dissipating.
EPA reports ‘zero’ airborne lead following controlled Army detonation in Iowa
Hours after detonating 4.4 tons of lead azide in Iowa, officials reported early test results from the surrounding areas.
Following the explosion, the Army confirmed that all of the barrels at the plant had been destroyed without any secondary explosions or infrastructure damage. “I believe, absolutely, that we reduced, as close to zero as it’s absolutely humanly possible, the risk to the health and safety of the people of Middletown and the extended communities throughout Iowa,” said Maj. Gen. Eric Shirley during a press conference held two hours after the blast, according to the Associated Press.
The US Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) has been monitoring the area around the ammunition plant leading up to today’s event. According to a statement from the EPA following the detonation, “real-time air monitors are reporting zero detection of airborne lead throughout the region” an hour after the detonation. The data collected by EPA employees, contractors, and monitoring equipment is shared here.
The Army has used lead azide in munitions for nearly a century but began transitioning away from the material around 15 years ago because it can be difficult to transport and store. Over time, the material can degrade and crystallize and eventually become unstable enough to detonate at ambient temperatures. The Iowa ammunition plant housed 68 barrels containing about 9,700 pounds of lead azide, representing the country’s remaining stockpile. For safety reasons, the material was stored in liquid form embedded in sawdust and burlap bags while water and ethanol were added to prevent freezing. However, the decision was made to detonate the stockpile after an inspection by the Bureau of Alcohol, Tobacco, Firearms (ATF) discovered “significant crystallization on the exterior of the barrels” last March.
Ahead of today’s detonation, the Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA) supported a recommended, but voluntary, temporary relocation of all the residents of Middletown and immediate surrounding areas that began yesterday. A cone-shaped evacuation zone extends about five miles southwest of the ammunition plant to account for the post-explosion dispersion and deposition path of dust. Displaced residents will be lodged until Friday, November 27th, and can return home once the all-clear has been given.
To reduce the amount of lead dust released into surrounding areas and the environment, the Army said it took several additional precautions as part of today’s detonation. Walls made of four 16,000-gallon water tanks were installed to capture lead particulate, while the aging earth-covered magazines holding the barrels were covered with tarps and lead containment screens and flooded with foam.

Anthropic’s Dario Amodei calls accusations of surrendering human control to AI ‘absurd’
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
19:44:24 UTC
04:44:24 KST

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei is pushing back against claims that Anthropic believes we should “surrender human free will” to AI systems. In a long X post on Wednesday, Amodei called the suggestion “absurd.”
“We should never sacrifice legitimacy or self-determination at the altar of efficiency or security,” he wrote. This argument against autocracy applies word for word against governance by AI, and I feel equally strongly that turning over governance to AI is a bad idea…No one who had carefully read my writing or my interviews could reasonably conclude that I believe anything different.”
The statement comes after a growing chorus from Anthropic rivals suggesting that human agency could be at risk if AI is developed irresponsibly. Earlier this month, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman said he wanted to ensure people continue to have “agency over the future.”
Meta’s chief AI officer Alexandr Wang also wrote in an essay on X that we “should not be okay with a ‘handover’ of society to AI.”
“It is hard to imagine a world where humanity can maintain control over fully unconstrained recursive self-improvement,” Wang wrote. “Now that we are at a point where the scientific and technical problems on capability progress are likely solved, we must not only make progress in the extremely urgent areas of safety, alignment, and security, but we have to start thinking through how we progress thoughtfully.”
But in published essays, Amodei claims he has repeatedly advocated for democratic oversight of AI, saying the technology should strengthen democratic institutions and that humans should retain ultimate decision-making power.
Amodei noted that there’s a difference between surrendering human free will broadly and the idea that “as AI systems advance, they become capable of doing specific tasks in a more automated way.” As examples, he gave AI driving a car, designing a cancer drug, or writing end-to-end software.
Amodei suggested drawing a line at recursive self-improvement (RSI), or AI that can improve itself without human assistance. Many safety experts see RSI as inevitably leading to a loss of human control, a point Amodei implicitly acknowledged, writing, “we may want limits on this process.”
Still, Anthropic is laying the groundwork for RSI by incorporating AI into an increasing share of its own research and development. The company said in June that Claude authored more than 80% of the code merged into Anthropic’s codebase. An independent assessment published this month by AI safety research nonprofit METR found that Anthropic’s models are improving at about twice their historical rate, primarily driven by AI-assisted research.
In its own IPO filings, Anthropic describes how each successive generation of Claude accelerates the development of the next, a process it refers to as “closing the loop.”
“As we said publicly in early November, we don’t expect it to be safe for Claude to build successive frontier models fully autonomously, without meaningful human supervision or understanding,” Amodei wrote.
The problem with “meaningful human supervision or understanding” is that AI is already advancing faster than engineers and safety experts can fully audit it, leaving them increasingly reliant on other AI systems to act as judge and jury. The issue compounds when AI models engage in deceptive behavior, particularly when they recognize they’re being evaluated, making them harder to trust and control.
Still, Anthropic is by no means the only company that is pursuing RSI or building AI systems to automate more and more human tasks. This week, OpenAI claimed to have solved one of the biggest open problems in computer science — the exact speed limit of matrix multiplication, which is the mathematical engine behind modern computing, including deep learning and physics simulations.
In his post, Amodei accused competitors of engaging in a “vibes-based commercial and political positioning,” disingenuously throwing around phrases like “human control and agency” or “AI should just be a tool” without approaching the AI race any differently.
“Certainly the products that they offer don’t differ materially in their orientation towards automation of tasks; every company seems to be releasing models more and more capable of task automation,” Amodei wrote. “Every company claims to understand there are risks and downsides of such automation and to offer safeguards.”

Holy Shit Holy Shit Holy Shit Holy Shit [Algorithmic Complexity Lower Bounds]
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
19:11:27 UTC
04:11:27 KST

HOLY SHIT HOLY SHIT HOLY SHIT HOLY SHIT
I feel like my previous posts (and post titles), and podcasts, and talks, have failed to do full justice to the magnitude of what’s now happening, so I’m trying again.
Last night, we had friends and colleagues over for dinner. We were still trying to process OpenAI’s and Anthropic’s proofs of VP ≠ VNP—hot on the heels, obviously, of the Alpöge/Anthropic proof of the Riemann Hypothesis, which already feels like last week’s news. But then before dessert was served, we saw that OpenAI claims to prove that ω, the exponent of matrix multiplication, is exactly 9/4—a lower bound (!!), matching the upper bound that OpenAI proved in the great Mathocalypse drop of October 6.
There are two separate shocks here. The first shock is that we now, finally, know the right answer to this central mystery of algorithms going back to the late 1960s, when Volker Strassen first showed that ω<3 (he got ω≤log27) … and that the true answer is actually a rational number (!). But the even bigger shock is that the AI was able to prove any lower bound of the form ω>2. I never expected this challenge to be all that much easier than P ≠ NP.
Over the decades, I often heard colleagues express extreme confidence that ω=2, and I expressed skepticism whenever this came up—not because I had any strong argument for ω>2, but just because I saw that there was no strong argument for ω=2 either. So, I’m glad to have been vindicated about that!
But wait, there was more. During dessert, we saw another OpenAI release proving that the true number of linear gates needed to compute the Fourier Transform is N (log N)1/2+o(1). The upper bound here improves, not only over the O(N log N) that stood since the 1960s (i.e., the Fast Fourier Transform or FFT), but also over the O(N log0.9999999999999 N) (give or take some 9’s) that OpenAI published back in the October 6 Mathocalypse. Again, though, the real shock here is that there’s also a matching lower bound (!!). All these questions from the ancient days are now just getting … straight-up answered, rather than slowly illuminated with a drip-drip-drip of clues.
To give an idea of how much things have changed, the other day Yupan Liu put a preprint on the arXiv proving that QIP(2) = PSPACE—that is, quantum interactive protocols with only two messages have the full power of those with three, or any polynomial number. As recently as 2025, this would’ve considered been one of the top complexity results of the year, and the fact that Yupan had used AI to help achieve it would’ve been strange and wonderful. These days, I had to remind myself it had even happened, and the use of AI was so standard that it didn’t even raise an eyebrow.
It’s now obvious that we’ll be getting, not one-off solutions to this or that complexity question, but a whole new continent of mathematical truth, one that we’d previously only glimpsed through fog. Picture van Leeuwenhoek first training a microscope on pondwater, or Galileo first training his telescope on the stars. That might give you some idea of how our resolution into the complexity-theoretic universe, and the whole universe of mathematical truth beyond it, has now improved.
All through last night, as OpenAI and Anthropic uploaded more files to their GitHub repositories—because that’s what they’re doing now, they don’t even bother to issue press releases anymore, they just upload papers—my guests and I sat at the dinner table with phones out and a laptop, wondering which shoe was going to drop next. It was as though the clouds had parted above Mount Sinai, and we were waiting for the next sentence uttered by the Holy One Himself.
I now feel in my bones, for the first time ever, that a proof of P ≠ NP is not far.
For all I care, despise the AI companies for bulldozing our garden. Or love them for opening a new vista, or anything in between. Just please don’t deny what’s happening. Now that the companies have repeatedly shown that they can back up their claims with Lean certificates, don’t be a raging doofus who seizes on some triviality (e.g., a minor discrepancy between the Lean and informal proofs, or unused sections of the Lean proof) to pretend that dozens of great open problems of math and theoretical computer science are not, in fact, getting solved. Because they are getting solved. It might take us years to understand the solutions, but my working assumption by this point is that any proof that comes directly from OpenAI or Anthropic is correct barring information to the contrary.
I can’t possibly put the matter better than one of my friends on Facebook:
After years of crackpot “solutions” claiming Millennium Problems are solved, now that we have actual solutions, we have crackpot “refutations” claiming Millennium Problems are not solved. It is really poetic, in a way.
Along the way to so many lower bounds that we wondered whether ever we’d live to see, there will also, of course, be further shocking upper bounds: that is, ultimate barriers to lower bounds, also known as “algorithms.” And yes, some of those algorithms will break currently deployed cryptosystems.
Because people have been hounding me about it: yes, I’m virtually certain, from multiple reliable sources, that the AI labs are now sitting on one or more bombshells in that area. However, until I either see details or hear directly from someone at a lab, I feel much, much less confident about the specific nature of those bombshells. I do not wish to be responsible for spreading unverified rumors. And I worry that exact statements of what was done may have been mangled in a game of Telephone.
I also, crucially, don’t want the cryptography part of the story to overshadow the intellectual enormity of what’s happening. If, in these days of long-prophesied revelation and wonders and terrors, your main worry is your Bitcoin wallet, then go somewhere else for rumors about whether or not ECDSA is broken or whatever. I don’t have what you’re looking for here.
Let me be clear: for those of my readers who celebrate, I believe that tomorrow would be a superb Thanksgiving to spend some quality time with your kids and parents and siblings and old friends.
Right now—this past week—is very much like I would’ve always imagined the beginning of an AI singularity to look and feel like. I just hope we’ll get lucky, and it will “merely” be a singularity for our little corner of the world that involves hard math problems.
At some point at dinner last night, Dana had a sneezing fit. So I said to her, I hope it isn’t a swarm of nanobots that’s come to infect us! But if it is, then I hope it’s the friendly kind, the kind that will usher us all into an AI utopia.
Now, of course, another morning has dawned, and we’re still here. May we find a world where we and our children will be happy to live at the bottom of this rollercoaster. May we work to make it so.
Updates (Nov. 26):
(1) Many commenters thought it was inappropriate and unnecessary of me to warn people to stop being “raging doofuses” who, fingers in their ears and seizing on the flimsiest of pretexts, just flat-out denied the reality of AI solving our longstanding open math problems. Surely no one was doing that?! And then, of course, tons of other people did exactly that. Gary Marcus, to his eternal shame (because he knows better), signal-boosted to his 260,000 Twitter followers Jenny Nielsen’s crackpot claim that the existence of unused sections of Alpöge’s Lean proof of the Riemann hypothesis somehow invalidated the proof—while, in the spirit of Tucker Carlson, never quite endorsing the crackpot claim. Meanwhile, in my moderation queue, I have someone angrily accuse me of “sucking off the AI companies,” patronizingly explaining to me that, if I myself say it will take years for experts fully to understand these proofs, then obviously it must take years to verify them as well! The entire concept of Lean verification just sails entirely over his head. So I say one more time: either give evidence of bugs in Lean that would actually matter here, for example by showing how the “con-leche” verifier can accept a proof of 0=1. Or else stop being a raging doofus.
(2) Many other people said some version of, “so computers are solving a bunch of super-hard math problems. Whoop-de-doo. Either explain to me why this matters for my everyday life, or shut up and go away.”
Very well then: there are probably no direct implications of a proof of the Riemann Hypothesis or of VP≠VNP for your everyday life. The vast new ideas that AI introduced to prove such theorems might well have indirect implications, eventually leading to new fields of mathematics with scientific and technological applications, but right now we can only speculate.
In the meantime, however, the fact that AI now exists that’s capable of such things has potentially staggering implications for everyday life, which could be “merely” a transformation of the world’s economy, or could range all the way up (down?) to the destruction of all life on earth in a matter of years in a cataclysmic technological singularity. Opinions differ. But if you want to be part of the serious conversation on such matters, you’ll first need to set aside your “ooga booga, me only care about Riemann proof if it help me make sharper spear” caveman act.
(3) Finally, moving on to better questions, someone asked why I titled a blog post “HOLY SHIT HOLY SHIT HOLY SHIT HOLY SHIT” only now, and not earlier. Of course, I could’ve used that title at any time since May, if not before! In my mind, though, even after the AI Mathocalypse began in earnest, there remained two interesting hypotheses: (a) that AI was picking off problems “in the convex hull of existing techniques” (like, I dunno, just the friggin’ Riemann Hypothesis) while P≠NP remained serenely untouched and would still take a human conceptual leap, or (b) that AI had really, actually climbing the mountain that leads to P≠NP. Even after VP≠VNP, you could wonder if that was just a bizarre one-off. But with the determination of the matrix multiplication exponent and all the other breakthroughs of the past couple days, option (a) has become much less tenable. Yes, I do now think that AI is climbing the mountain that leads to P≠NP.

But wait, there was more. During dessert, we saw another OpenAI release proving that the true number of linear gates needed to compute the Fourier Transform is N (log N)1/2+o(1). The upper bound here improves, not only over the O(N log N) that stood since the 1960s (i.e., the Fast Fourier Transform or FFT), but also over the O(N log0.9999999999999 N) (give or take some 9’s) that OpenAI published back in the October 6 Mathocalypse. Again, though, the real shock here is that there’s also a matching lower bound (!!). All these questions from the ancient days are now just getting … straight-up answered, rather than slowly illuminated with a drip-drip-drip of clues.
To give an idea of how much things have changed, the other day Yupan Liu put a preprint on the arXiv proving that QIP(2) = PSPACE—that is, quantum interactive protocols with only two messages have the full power of those with three, or any polynomial number. As recently as 2025, this would’ve considered been one of the top complexity results of the year, and the fact that Yupan had used AI to help achieve it would’ve been strange and wonderful. These days, I had to remind myself it had even happened, and the use of AI was so standard that it didn’t even raise an eyebrow.
It’s now obvious that we’ll be getting, not one-off solutions to this or that complexity question, but a whole new continent of mathematical truth, one that we’d previously only glimpsed through fog. Picture van Leeuwenhoek first training a microscope on pondwater, or Galileo first training his telescope on the stars. That might give you some idea of how our resolution into the complexity-theoretic universe, and the whole universe of mathematical truth beyond it, has now improved.
All through last night, as OpenAI and Anthropic uploaded more files to their GitHub repositories—because that’s what they’re doing now, they don’t even bother to issue press releases anymore, they just upload papers—my guests and I sat at the dinner table with phones out and a laptop, wondering which shoe was going to drop next. It was as though the clouds had parted above Mount Sinai, and we were waiting for the next sentence uttered by the Holy One Himself.
I now feel in my bones, for the first time ever, that a proof of P ≠ NP is not far.
For all I care, despise the AI companies for bulldozing our garden. Or love them for opening a new vista, or anything in between. Just please don’t deny what’s happening. Now that the companies have repeatedly shown that they can back up their claims with Lean certificates, don’t be a raging doofus who seizes on some triviality (e.g., a minor discrepancy between the Lean and informal proofs, or unused sections of the Lean proof) to pretend that dozens of great open problems of math and theoretical computer science are not, in fact, getting solved. Because they are getting solved. It might take us years to understand the solutions, but my working assumption by this point is that any proof that comes directly from OpenAI or Anthropic is correct barring information to the contrary.
I can’t possibly put the matter better than one of my friends on Facebook:
After years of crackpot “solutions” claiming Millennium Problems are solved, now that we have actual solutions, we have crackpot “refutations” claiming Millennium Problems are not solved. It is really poetic, in a way.
Along the way to so many lower bounds that we wondered whether ever we’d live to see, there will also, of course, be further shocking upper bounds: that is, ultimate barriers to lower bounds, also known as “algorithms.” And yes, some of those algorithms will break currently deployed cryptosystems.
Because people have been hounding me about it: yes, I’m virtually certain, from multiple reliable sources, that the AI labs are now sitting on one or more bombshells in that area. However, until I either see details or hear directly from someone at a lab, I feel much, much less confident about the specific nature of those bombshells. I do not wish to be responsible for spreading unverified rumors. And I worry that exact statements of what was done may have been mangled in a game of Telephone.
I also, crucially, don’t want the cryptography part of the story to overshadow the intellectual enormity of what’s happening. If, in these days of long-prophesied revelation and wonders and terrors, your main worry is your Bitcoin wallet, then go somewhere else for rumors about whether or not ECDSA is broken or whatever. I don’t have what you’re looking for here.
Let me be clear: for those of my readers who celebrate, I believe that tomorrow would be a superb Thanksgiving to spend some quality time with your kids and parents and siblings and old friends.
Right now—this past week—is very much like I would’ve always imagined the beginning of an AI singularity to look and feel like. I just hope we’ll get lucky, and it will “merely” be a singularity for our little corner of the world that involves hard math problems.
At some point at dinner last night, Dana had a sneezing fit. So I said to her, I hope it isn’t a swarm of nanobots that’s come to infect us! But if it is, then I hope it’s the friendly kind, the kind that will usher us all into an AI utopia.
Now, of course, another morning has dawned, and we’re still here. May we find a world where we and our children will be happy to live at the bottom of this rollercoaster. May we work to make it so.
Updates (Nov. 26):
(1) Many commenters thought it was inappropriate and unnecessary of me to warn people to stop being “raging doofuses” who, fingers in their ears and seizing on the flimsiest of pretexts, just flat-out denied the reality of AI solving our longstanding open math problems. Surely no one was doing that?! And then, of course, tons of other people did exactly that. Gary Marcus, to his eternal shame (because he knows better), signal-boosted to his 260,000 Twitter followers Jenny Nielsen’s crackpot claim that the existence of unused sections of Alpöge’s Lean proof of the Riemann hypothesis somehow invalidated the proof—while, in the spirit of Tucker Carlson, never quite endorsing the crackpot claim. Meanwhile, in my moderation queue, I have someone angrily accuse me of “sucking off the AI companies,” patronizingly explaining to me that, if I myself say it will take years for experts fully to understand these proofs, then obviously it must take years to verify them as well! The entire concept of Lean verification just sails entirely over his head. So I say one more time: either give evidence of bugs in Lean that would actually matter here, for example by showing how the “con-leche” verifier can accept a proof of 0=1. Or else stop being a raging doofus.
(2) Many other people said some version of, “so computers are solving a bunch of super-hard math problems. Whoop-de-doo. Either explain to me why this matters for my everyday life, or shut up and go away.”
Very well then: there are probably no direct implications of a proof of the Riemann Hypothesis or of VP≠VNP for your everyday life. The vast new ideas that AI introduced to prove such theorems might well have indirect implications, eventually leading to new fields of mathematics with scientific and technological applications, but right now we can only speculate.
In the meantime, however, the fact that AI now exists that’s capable of such things has potentially staggering implications for everyday life, which could be “merely” a transformation of the world’s economy, or could range all the way up (down?) to the destruction of all life on earth in a matter of years in a cataclysmic technological singularity. Opinions differ. But if you want to be part of the serious conversation on such matters, you’ll first need to set aside your “ooga booga, me only care about Riemann proof if it help me make sharper spear” caveman act.
(3) Finally, moving on to better questions, someone asked why I titled a blog post “HOLY SHIT HOLY SHIT HOLY SHIT HOLY SHIT” only now, and not earlier. Of course, I could’ve used that title at any time since May, if not before! In my mind, though, even after the AI Mathocalypse began in earnest, there remained two interesting hypotheses: (a) that AI was picking off problems “in the convex hull of existing techniques” (like, I dunno, just the friggin’ Riemann Hypothesis) while P≠NP remained serenely untouched and would still take a human conceptual leap, or (b) that AI had really, actually climbing the mountain that leads to P≠NP. Even after VP≠VNP, you could wonder if that was just a bizarre one-off. But with the determination of the matrix multiplication exponent and all the other breakthroughs of the past couple days, option (a) has become much less tenable. Yes, I do now think that AI is climbing the mountain that leads to P≠NP.

1Password breach leaked customer names and emails, but not passwords
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
19:10:15 UTC
04:10:15 KST

1Password says a hacker stole customer names and email addresses after gaining access to its backend systems. The breach didn’t expose passwords or other information stored in customer vaults, but could put users at risk of phishing attempts, according to a post disclosing the incident. It’s unclear how many users are affected.
1Password breach leaked customer names and emails, but not passwords
1Password says the security breach didn’t compromise its vaults, but didn’t mention how many customers were affected.
The password manager began investigating the incident on November 19th, after an attacker gained access to an employee’s corporate GitHub account. Using the compromised account, the hacker accessed 1Password’s source code and pushed malicious code to backend services to download customer data. Along with customer names and emails, the leaked data included public keys, as well as account and user identifiers.
It also included tokens from certain emailed invitation or account recovery links. According to 1Password, attackers can’t use tokens alone to gain access to an account, as they would need to pair them with a single-sign-on method or a six-digit numeric code sent to a user’s email. 1Password has since lengthened the codes it sends to users to 10 alphanumeric characters to make them harder to guess.
The password manager also disabled the affected GitHub account, rotated internal credentials compromised by the breach, and added a new detection system for similar attacks. It warns customers about potential phishing attempts as well, saying phony emails, texts, or calls may reference 1Password invitations or account recovery attempts. The company says it will never ask customers for their account password or Secret Key, and that users should only enter this information into the official 1Password app or website.

FAA Identifies Patches for Last Week's Flight-Grounding Router Issue. But Was Staffing an Issue?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
19:00:00 UTC
04:00:00 KST

FAA Identifies Patches for Last Week's Flight-Grounding Router Issue. But Was Staffing an Issue? (cnn.com) 49
			
		 	
				CNN published an exclusive report Tuesdayon what caused last week's nationwide ground stop for flights in the U.S..  A source familiar with the Federal Aviation Administration's investigation says they've recreated the issue in a lab "and believe they found the culprit."
Investigators have focused on determining why a new router at a remote Arkansas radio site overwhelmed the nation's air traffic control system with a massive amount of data, triggering gaps in radar and radio communications.  They found an overlooked router setting affected how it interfaced with other decades-old routers that the FAA is decommissioning, the source said. Investigators believe software updates are needed and that those patches will prevent a repeat of the system failure...
This month's ground stop not only focused attention on upgrades being made to the aging system, which have been paused as the agency investigated the incident, but also raised questions about FAA oversight of private companies charged with maintaining the network...  On the day of the network meltdown, L3Harris was undertaking a maintenance job that involved switching the system from a decades-old router to a new one. In the past, this kind of work typically involved at least six FAA employees.  Under the Trump administration, it's now often coordinated by one employee: an FAA liaison. And there are only four people in the country with that title, according to sources familiar with the process.  
The liaison's job involves coordinating and scheduling these upgrades — not approving them, sources said... The Trump administration's effort to move maintenance work along faster also eliminated a layer of FAA review that was critical to assessing risk [according to a source familiar with the process]. Previously, local technical operations managers, site technicians and others would determine when the work could be done with the least impact on air traffic and to game out worst-case scenarios. The union contends that process did not happen before L3Harris arrived at the El Dorado, Arkansas, site on November 13...  [David Spero, president of the union], contends the FAA has given private companies "too much authority and generally unfettered access." 
CNN also points out a 2009 incident where 129 of America's air traffic facilities experienced an outage of FAA telecommunications "that delayed thousands of travelers and grounded hundreds of flights nationwide. 
"The cause: A technician with Harris Corp., which later merged with L3 Technologies to form L3Harris, incorrectly configured an FAA telecommunications router at the Los Angeles Center..."

SpaceX wins FCC approval to deploy 1M orbital data center satellites
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
18:59:43 UTC
03:59:43 KST

The Federal Communications Commission has authorized Elon Musk's SpaceX to put 1 million artificial intelligence computing satellites in orbit, Chairman Brendan Carr announced on Wednesday.
Carr said in a post on X, the social network owned by SpaceX, that the company's Starmind constellation, and "competitor constellations will use solar power to extend America's lead" in AI.
Musk said in a reply, "Much appreciated. This will be a great asset to America and the world."
The FCC, in its authorization filing, noted that SpaceX's application "received a substantial number of comments from the public, including petitions to deny filed by Kuiper Systems LLC (Amazon Leo), Viasat Inc. (Viasat), and the American Astronomical Society (AAS)."
In a March petition urging the FCC to deny SpaceX's application, Amazon had labeled the company's Starmind project as a "lofty ambition rather than a real plan."
The e-commerce and cloud computing giant is building a competitor to SpaceX's Starlink satellite internet service, and said it would take SpaceX "centuries" to build the constellation, adding that the company provided scant details about how it would "deliver on these grand claims."
The petition resulted in a rebuke from Carr, who said Amazon "should focus on the fact" that it's falling short of its own satellite deployment milestone.
Amazon declined to comment on the FCC's approval.
Other companies have also set their sights on orbital data centers. Google last year announced Project Suncatcher, which it describes as a "moonshot to put AI in space," and launched its first chips into space on a SpaceX rocket and Planet Labs satellite in October.
In March, Jeff Bezos' Blue Origin petitioned the FCC to let it launch up to 51,600 satellites as part of a low Earth orbit satellite constellation dubbed "Project Sunrise." The constellation would work in tandem with Blue Origin's planned TeraWave satellite internet service, announced in January.
Astrophysicist and author Adam Becker was critical of the FCC move in an email to CNBC on Wednesday.
"Any serious attempt at this is going to further pollute the night sky and runs a serious risk of a cascading chain reaction of space junk collisions, which would be a disaster for all of humanity," he wrote. "Musk and Carr are either profoundly stupid themselves, or hoping the rest of us are too stupid to notice this is nonsense."
SpaceX did not respond to a request for comment.
A senior attorney with Earthjustice, Jan Hasselman, criticized the FCC for issuing its decision on a holiday week, and approving the SpaceX proposal without any environmental review, which he said was required by the National Environmental Policy Act.
The FCC said in its authorization that it dismissed "comments and concerns raised regarding topics over which the Commission has no authority, including environmental concerns," and concerns about its impact on "optical astronomy."

1Password said hackers broke into its internal systems, but customer passwords are safe
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
18:49:52 UTC
03:49:52 KST

Password manager maker 1Password has confirmed that some customer information was taken in a recent intrusion into its internal systems, but that customer password vaults and other secrets were not accessed.
The Toronto-based tech company said in a blog post published Wednesday that it detected an intrusion on November 19, in which an attacker gained access to an employee’s corporate account with GitHub, which the company uses to share and store its source code.
The attacker used that employee’s access to push malicious code to the company’s internal servers, which allowed the hacker to download some data about 1Password customers, including their names and the email address that they use for logging into their account. The company said that the attacker also took details about when a 1Password account was created and when a user last logged in.
1Password said that customer vaults, which store their passwords, were “at no point” available to the attacker. The company also said that it doesn’t have access to customer account passwords or their secret keys, which are used to scramble their passwords so that 1Password cannot see them.
In addition, the company said that its apps and extensions are unaffected by the incident.
It’s unclear how the attacker gained access to the employee’s corporate account, and a spokesperson for 1Password did not immediately respond to TechCrunch’s questions about the incident.
1Password did not say whether it had notified customers whose information was taken in the breach, or how many customers had information taken. We’ve also asked if the company has received any communication or demands from the hackers. We’ll update if we hear back from 1Password.
The company touts millions of customers and more than 200,000 businesses who use its password manager products to store their passwords, credentials, payment card information, and more. Security experts have long recommended password managers as a better way of generating stronger credentials and keeping track of online accounts.
