Huawei Is Guilty of Conspiracy To Steal Trade Secrets, a US Jury Finds
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
18:00:00 UTC
03:00:00 KST

Huawei Is Guilty of Conspiracy To Steal Trade Secrets, a US Jury Finds (apnews.com) 16
			
		 	
				The Associated Press reports:
A U.S. jury convicted Chinese tech giant Huawei Technologies on Tuesday of conspiring to steal trade secrets and defrauding banks as it grew into one of the world's biggest suppliers of gear for wireless telecommunications networks.  But jurors acquitted Huawei of the most serious charge: engaging in a criminal racketeering conspiracy, a result that the company's lawyer said was a "profound rejection" of the government's narrative that it was corrupt from top to bottom and had a corporate culture of cheating, stealing and lying.  Prosecutors had used the charge, commonly found in organized crime and drug cases, to accuse Huawei of operating illicitly to poach intellectual property and technological secrets from competitors and partners. 
The convictions covered allegations that the company did business in countries subject to U.S. sanctions — including Iran and North Korea — and made false statements to U.S. government officials and banks as part of an effort to hide its misdeeds.  In all, Huawei was convicted on 10 of 12 counts. The case in Brooklyn federal court involved no human defendants, but the company could be made to pay billions of dollars in penalties when it is sentenced next September...
Chinese officials said the U.S. has engaged in "economic bullying" and improperly used national security as a pretext for oppressing Chinese companies.

December 2026 Opportunities: Open Calls, Residencies, and Grants for Artists
Every month, we share opportunities for artists and designers, including open calls, grants, fellowships, and residencies. Make sure you never miss out by joining our monthly Opportunities Newsletter.
Fall $1,800 Innovate Grants for Art + PhotoFeatured
Innovate Grant awards two $1,800 grants each quarter to one visual artist and one photographer. Nine honorable mentions in each category are also featured on the Innovate Grant platform and join a growing creative community of esteemed artists. Applicants must be 18 years and older, and all media and genres are accepted. All applicants retain the right to the work they submit. 
Deadline: 11:59 p.m. PST on December 15, 2026.
Bryson County Retreat Featured
Practicing artists of any background and at any stage of their career are encouraged to apply for a 2027 residency at Bryson County Retreat. Residency periods can be flexible, but month-long stays are suggested. Bryson County is designed to provide ample living and working space and inspirational settings where an artist may work free from the distractions and responsibilities of day-to-day life. The program includes free lodging near tannic dark water lakes, sprawling live oaks, and drooping Spanish moss at the headwaters of the Santa Fe River in north central Florida.
Deadline: Rolling.
Open Calls
Wildlife Photographer of the Year(International)
Alongside its conservation message, this prestigious global competition is hosted by London’s Natural History Museum to champion the work of photographers, whether professional or amateur, young or older, or using top-of-the-range equipment or smartphones. Photographers are eligible for myriad awards, with a Grand Title prize of £15,000. The entry fee is £35 (free for Young WPY contest).
Deadline: 11:30 p.m. GMT on December 3, 2026.
Responsa Foundation Open Call for Textile Art(International)
The Responsa Foundation champions projects that create meaningful social impact and foster public engagement. The organization is seeking textile artists to submit work for This Memory, a group exhibition taking place from September 22 to October 3, 2027, at Burgh House in Hampstead. 
Deadline: December 4, 2026.
Open Call for Public Art Proposals in Moab(Utah, National)
Moab Arts is accepting proposals for 2027 public art projects. Proposals are welcome for sculpture, murals, three crosswalks, and sticker designs but may include other ideas for different media or locations around the city. Preference is given to local and regional artists.
Deadline: 11:59 p.m. MST on December 6, 2026.
Material Works Art Award 2026(International)
Material Works supports artists through one-time $2,500 awards in exchange for original pieces added to its growing contemporary art collection. The award recognizes artistic excellence and aims to promote career advancement through increasing visibility and exposure. There is a €40 application fee.
Deadline: December 6, 2026, or when the first 300 artworks submitted, whichever comes first.
Call for Mail Art(International)
Hive Center for the Book Arts in Evanston, Illinois, welcomes mail art for the exhibition love|mark, which runs from January 1 to February 27. All submissions must arrive at at the center by December 15 for inclusion in the show.
Deadline: December 15, 2026.
Sony Future Filmmaker Awards(International)
The Sony Future Filmmaker Awards is a major program providing opportunities for creators and filmmakers. The shortlisted filmmakers are flown to Sony Pictures Studios in Culver City, California, to attend an awards ceremony and an industry immersion program. Categories include fiction, nonfiction, animation, immersive, student, and vertical format.
Deadline: 7 a.m. CST on December 15, 2026.
GLEAM 2027 Request for Proposals(International)
Olbrich Botanical Gardens in Madison, Wisconsin, invites proposals for temporary, large-scale, light-based artwork for GLEAM, a public art exhibition. The show will be open to the public from August 28 to October 23, 2027.
Deadline: 11:59 p.m. CST on January 6, 2027.
Visual Arts Prize IDEA – Vila d’Amer(International)
Apply for this inaugural prize open to artists working in all visual arts media. Two winning artworks, with prizes of €3,500 and €1,500, will become part of the permanent International Visual Arts Collection IDEA – Vila d’Amer.
Deadline: January 10, 2027.
360 CHICAGO: CloudWalk Call for Artists(Chicago)
One artist will be selected for this major installation in the CloudWalk gallery atop 875 N. Michigan Ave. The selected artist receives a $10,000 commission, and the winning installation will go on view starting April 2027.
Deadline: January 18, 2027.
World of WearableArt 2027 Competition(International)
Entries are now open for the 2027 WOW Competition in Wellington, New Zealand. Awards consist of NZ$200,000 in prizes, including the Supreme WOW Award of NZ$30,000.
Deadline: February 11, 2027 (international), March 31, 2027 (New Zealand).
Grants
The Filmed in Nevada County Grant(International)
To support filmmakers in the production of work in Nevada County, California, Nevada City Film Festival offers grants between $1,000 and $20,000.
Deadline: December 1, 2026.
Southern Prize and State Fellowships for Visual Arts(U.S. South)
The South Arts Southern Prize and State Fellowships acknowledge the highest quality artistic work being created in the American South. The program is open to individual artists living in Alabama, Florida, Georgia, Kentucky, Louisiana, Mississippi, North Carolina, South Carolina, and Tennessee. State Fellowships are $5,000, with one awarded per state. These then compete for two Southern Prize tiers of $25,000 and $10,000.
Deadline: January 15, 2027 (fee waiver request by December 18, 2026).
The Virigina A. Groot Foundation Grants(International)
Each year, the Virginia A. Groot Foundation offers three grants to artists who have exceptional talent and a demonstrated ability in ceramic sculpture or sculpture of diverse mediums. The grants are organized into tiers, with the top award providing $60,000, the second award offering $30,000, and the third awarding $20,000.
Deadline: January 9, 2027.
Foundation for Contemporary Arts Emergency Grants(U.S.)
Emergency Grants provide urgent funding for visual and performing artists and poets who have sudden, unanticipated opportunities to present their work to the public, but there is insufficient time to seek other sources of funding or unexpected expenses are incurred for projects close to completion. Each month, the organization makes approximately 15 to 20 grants in the range of $500 to $3,000 each.
Deadline: Rolling.

Both individuals and organizations can apply to Arts Council National Lottery Project Grants. These awards are designed to focus on specific projects with starting and ending dates including festivals and carnivals, dance, libraries, literature, museums, music, theater, or visual arts programs. There is no maximum threshold for the fund.
Deadline: Rolling.

The Black Artists Grant offered by Creative Debuts is no-strings-attached financial support to help Black artists. Selected applicants receive £500, and applications are reviewed monthly.
Deadline: Rolling.
The Adolf and Esther Gottlieb Emergency Grant (International)
This program provides one-time financial assistance to qualified painters, printmakers, and sculptors whose needs resulted from an unforeseen catastrophic incident and who lack the resources to meet that situation. Awardees typically receive $5,000 and up to $15,000.
Deadline: Rolling.
Pollock-Krasner Foundation Grant (International)
The foundation welcomes applications from painters, sculptors, and artists working on paper, including printmakers. Grants are intended for one year and range up to $50,000. The artist’s circumstances determine the size of the grant, and professional exhibition history will be considered.
Deadline: Rolling.

How Can Anything Beat n log n?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
17:54:29 UTC
02:54:29 KST

How Can Anything Beat n log n?
Section: Home / Post
Categories: Computer Science,
Tags: Algorithms, Entropy, Information Theory,
Recently, headlines have kept making the rounds about sub-$n \log n$ algorithms—first Duan et al. breaking Dijkstra’s $O(m + n \log n)$ sorting barrier for directed shortest paths, and then a preprint claiming sub-$n \log n$ integer multiplication.
My first reaction was skepticism. If a problem cares about ordering $n$ items, don’t you need $\log n$ steps to uniquely place each item, repeated $n$ times? That gives $n \log n$. How could you possibly do better?
The short answer is that $n \log n$ is not a universal speed limit. It is the lower bound of a specific model of computation. To see how algorithms beat it, we need to look at what $n \log n$ actually measures—and which assumptions those algorithms drop.
What $n \log n$ Actually Looks Like
Think of sorting $n$ items as routing each item down a decision tree into its final slot.
If you are only allowed to take 2-way comparison steps (< or >), every fork in the tree has 2 branches. Each step cuts the remaining slots in half. To route one item to its unique leaf among $n$ slots, it has to travel down roughly $\log_2 n$ levels.
Send all $n$ items down a tree of depth $\log_2 n$, and you pay $n \log_2 n$ total steps.
The Hidden Assumptions
That intuition feels airtight: if characterizing or placing a single item among $n$ slots takes $\log_2 n$ steps, and there are $n$ items to process, then any task requiring ordering or classification across $n$ items seems forced to pay $n \log_2 n$. Under two specific conditions, that reasoning is airtight:
- Black-box items: You cannot peek inside an item. Your only tool is a 2-way yes/no step (like is a < b?) that yields at most 1 bit of information per step.
- Factorial-scale outcomes: The number of possible final answers grows like $n!$ (or $n^n$)—meaning each of the $n$ items could end up in one of roughly $n$ distinct roles or positions. Not every task on $n$ items has that many outcomes: finding the maximum has only $n$ possible answers and assigning yes/no labels has $2^n$, compared to $n!$ orderings for sorting.
How Existing Algorithms Break $n \log n$
Counterexamples already exist in every algorithms textbook. Two classic ones—Bucket Sort and Radix Sort—sort $n$ numbers in $O(n)$ time when the numbers come from a bounded range or fixed digit width:
- Bucket Sort computes a bucket address directly from each number’s value, drops the item straight into that bucket in a single step (when numbers map cleanly into $n$ slots, that is a direct $n$-way lookup), and then reads the buckets back in order.
- Radix Sort processes numbers one digit at a time. In Most-Significant-Digit (MSD) Radix Sort, 3-digit numbers are first routed into 10 buckets (0–9) by their hundreds digit; only buckets holding more than one item are split again by their tens digit, and so on—without ever comparing two numbers against each other.
Neither algorithm breaks mathematics. Both drop the black-box assumption—and flatten the tree.
Imagine lining up 30 kids by birth month. If you treat them as black boxes and only compare pairs of kids (“who was born earlier?”), you are stuck with a 2-way tree of depth $\log_2 n$. Instead, put 12 labeled boxes on the floor (Jan through Dec). Each kid looks at their own birth month and walks straight to their box in one step.
Computers can do the same thing because numbers aren’t opaque black boxes—they are digits and bit patterns, and hardware can jump directly to a memory address in a single step. Direct indexing widens the branching factor all the way to $n$, collapsing the tree to depth 1. MSD Radix Sort makes the intermediate case just as clear: it routes the exact same items down a tree, except each node has 10 branches (0–9) instead of 2, flattening the depth from $\log_2 n$ down to the number of digits—with zero comparisons. (As we will see next, when numbers are allowed to grow arbitrarily large with $n$, escaping $n \log n$ asymptotically requires choosing the branching factor as a function of $n$ rather than keeping it fixed at 10.)
The Information-Theoretic View
Why does widening the branching factor break $n \log n$, and when is a lower bound truly unavoidable? Information theory puts both assumptions into a single equation.
In an earlier post on entropy, I framed entropy $H$ as the minimum number of binary choices (bits) needed to describe categories of events:
$$ H(\text{outcomes}) = -\sum_i p_i \log_2 p_i $$
That same lens gives us a hard floor for any algorithm.
Let $N$ be the total number of possible final answers to a problem—a function of the input size $n$, not $n$ itself. When every answer is equally likely (the worst case, $p_i = 1/N$), the entropy of the answer space simplifies to:
$$ H(N) = -\sum_{i=1}^{N} \frac{1}{N} \log_2 \frac{1}{N} = \log_2 N $$
Why is $H(N) = \log_2 N$ a hard floor for 2-way steps? Let $q$ be the number of 2-way (yes/no) steps an algorithm takes. Those $q$ steps can produce at most $2^q$ distinct transcripts of answers. If $2^q < N$, at least two different final answers must share the exact same transcript, and no algorithm can tell them apart. Therefore:
$$ 2^q \ge N \implies q \ge \log_2 N = H(N) $$
Where does the $\log_2 n$ in $n \log_2 n$ come from? Logarithms turn multiplication into addition:
- When each item has 2 outcomes ($N = 2^n$), taking the log yields $H(2^n) = \log_2(2^n) = n$—linear, with no $\log n$.
- When all $n$ items can be ordered in $N = n!$ ways, you are multiplying $n$ factors that grow with $n$. Taking $H(n!) = \log_2(n!)$ adds up $\log_2 1 + \log_2 2 + \dots + \log_2 n$, which lands at roughly $n \log_2 n$.
Now look at what happens when you widen each step’s branching factor to $k$. Remember from the entropy post that the base of the logarithm corresponds to how many outcomes a single step can distinguish, so $q$ $k$-way steps yield $k^q$ transcripts:
$$ k^q \ge N \implies q \ge \frac{H(N)}{\log_2 k} = \log_k N $$
Notice a crucial distinction here. Any constant $k$-way step only differs from a 2-way step by a constant factor ($1/\log_2 k$): a fixed 10-way decimal lookup trims the work by $\log_2 10 \approx 3.32\times$, and a fixed 256-way byte lookup trims it by $8\times$, yet as $n$ grows without bound (and distinct numbers need at least $\log_k n$ digits), both stay locked to $O(n \log n)$ forever.
To actually escape $n \log n$, $k$ must be a function of $n$ (for example, $k = \sqrt{n}$ or $k = n$). Once $k$ scales with $n$ as $k = n^\alpha$, $\log_2 k = \alpha \log_2 n$ cancels the $\log_2 n$ in $n \log_2 n$, so the total number of steps is only off from a one-shot $n$-way lookup by a constant factor ($1/\alpha$)—that is, linear $O(n)$. Radix Sort achieves this when its base $k$ is chosen relative to the size and range of the numbers (e.g., $k = n$ or $k = \sqrt{n}$): we are using the numerical value of the input itself to inform the algorithm’s addressing, piercing the black box.
Entropy Is a Floor, Not a Guarantee
One important nuance: having $H(N) = \log_2 N$ bits of entropy does not guarantee you can solve a problem in $\log_2 N$ steps (or $\log_k N$ steps with a $k$-way step).
Compare searching a sorted list against finding the maximum of an unsorted list. Both have $N = n$ possible answers, so both have the exact same entropy: $H(n) = \log_2 n$ bits. Yet finding the max takes $n - 1$ comparisons, because each step only knocks out one loser—it cannot cut the remaining candidates in half.
More generally, to reach $q \approx \log_k N = H(N) / \log_2 k$ steps with a $k$-way step over $N$ possible outcomes, each step must partition the remaining candidates symmetrically (evenly) into $k$ disjoint buckets of roughly $N/k$ outcomes. That is what lets every step shrink the answer space by division ($N \to N/k$) and extract the full $\log_2 k$ bits toward the answer.
When a step is asymmetric—say, checking “is item $i$ the target?” on an unsorted list, where one branch isolates $1$ outcome and the other leaves $N - 1$—the worst-case branch shrinks by subtraction ($N \to N - 1$) instead of division, so you never reach $\log_k N$. Finding the maximum runs into a related failure: a > b has two equally likely outcomes, yet on the answer space of $N$ candidate maxima it only discards a single loser, leaving $N - 1$ candidates alive either way because the remaining information is spent on relative order we do not need. You only hit the entropy floor when the problem’s structure lets each step carve the remaining answers into roughly equal, disjoint fractions.
Bringing It All Together
Every complexity claim sits on two axes: how fast the answer space $N$ grows, and how much information a single $k$-way step can extract.
So whenever you see a headline announcing a sub-$n \log n$ algorithm, there are only two ways it happened: either the problem never actually had a factorial-scale answer space in the first place (computing shortest-path distances doesn’t require sorting every vertex, and multiplying two $n$-bit integers only has $2^{2n}$ possible outputs—an entropy floor of $2n$, never $n!$), or the algorithm found a way to look inside the box and widen the branching factor $k$ with $n$.

====================================================================================================

FCC signs off on SpaceX’s plan to put up to 1 million data center satellites into space
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
17:44:57 UTC
02:44:57 KST

In an announcement on Wednesday, the FCC says it authorized SpaceX to deploy up to 1 million AI chip-equipped satellites as part of the Elon Musk-owned company’s “Starmind” initiative. With several others also pursuing the idea of putting AI data centers in space, including Google and Blue Origin, this is the first approval the agency has granted for a full “constellation” of AI satellites.
FCC signs off on SpaceX’s plan to put up to 1 million data center satellites into space
SpaceX aims to power AI capabilities here on Earth with its ‘Starmind’ constellation.
The FCC’s approval requires SpaceX to deploy at least one satellite by 2033, and meet the 1 million satellite milestone by 2040. The FCC also deferred SpaceX’s request to dispose of satellites located above 600km in space “graveyard” orbits, rather than having them burn up in the Earth’s atmosphere, saying the company hasn’t provided “sufficient information” about its disposal process plans.
SpaceX has described its Starmind project as a way to “capture solar energy in space to power” AI capabilities on Earth. The company says Starmind will transmit data to Earth using high-speed lasers that connect to Starlink. It plans to produce chips for its satellites at an upcoming Terafab facility.
Google has already taken its first steps toward putting compute into space, with the launch of a “Project Suncatcher” AI server satellite last month. Jeff Bezos’ Blue Origin will carry out the first dedicated heavy-lift launch for Project Suncatcher in 2029.

Britain's ghost stations
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
17:43:46 UTC
02:43:46 KST

When I stepped off a train at Achanalt station into the frosty Highlands one morning last March, I did notice that it wasn’t quite Waterloo in the rush hour, but I didn’t realise what an exclusive club I had just joined. According to a list of Britain’s most and least-used stations published this week, I was one of only 352 people to board or get off a train there in the entire 12 months from April 2025 to March 2026.
Flanders and Swann wrote ‘The Slow Train’ about the Beeching closures, lamenting the rural stations which were to close. They needn’t have worried: over half a century on, such places still exist.
My instinct is to attack the waste of public money in keeping a station open when hardly anyone uses it, but the absurdity of Britain’s ‘ghost’ stations fascinates me more than angers me. In any case, having paid £11 to travel 20 miles down the line from Strathcarron, I did rather feel that I had single-handedly covered the annual cost of maintaining what is really little more than a platform and Perspex shelter.
Achanalt, it turns out, is only the 15th least-used station in Britain. The honour of being the least-used this year is taken by Reddish South, on an obscure line from Stockport to Stalybridge in Greater Manchester, which was used by only 94 passengers. It is an achievement it will be hard-pressed to repeat next year, as the country’s least-used station invariably receives a leap in custom as railway fanatics descend on it just for the sake of being there: station-visiting has apparently taken over from train-spotting now that few trains have proper locomotives any more. Last year’s winner of the title for least-used station, Elton and Orston, in Nottinghamshire, saw its annual passenger numbers jump from 68 to 102, demoting it to third place. I wouldn’t be surprised if every single one of the extra passengers wore an anorak and carried a bag of Marmite sandwiches.
There are two types of ghost station. Firstly, there are those such as Reddish South which are the product of public opposition to the Beeching closures. Some time in the 1970s, British Rail stopped closing stations, reckoning that the bureaucracy and expense of going through the consultation procedure required to remove them from the railway map altogether was greater than simply running down the service to a minimal level. As a result, Reddish South receives one train a week in each direction, on a Saturday morning. You can’t even get a day out in Stockport: catch the 8.46 from Reddish South and you only get five minutes before the train home.
The second type of ghost station is those like Achanalt, which offer a full service of several trains a day; it is just that there aren’t many people who want to travel there. In Achanalt’s case, it isn’t hard to wonder why. The ‘village’, if it can be called that, consists of four houses. Nor is the station all that much good for doing what I was doing: accessing the nearby hills, as a river cuts them off to the south and a thick forestry plantation lies in the way to the north. If you want to go for a walk, it is almost certainly going to involve a few miles’ romp along the A832.
It is pretty absurd that Dr Beeching left a station like this open while depriving Dudley (population 335,000) and Mansfield (114,000) of a rail service – although the latter has subsequently reopened. But at least Achanalt serves four properties. Even more bizarre is Altnabreac station on the Far North Line between Inverness and Wick.
Maybe Dr Beeching couldn’t even find it on the map. It lies up a track seven miles from the nearest public road and there is precisely one house within five miles: the former station house. Trouble is, owners, who presumably could have bought a remote property anywhere in the empty vastness of Caithness, don’t seem to like trains very much. In 2023, a gang of workers from Network Rail turned up to repair the platform and found the gate locked. After a stand-off lasting many months, involving many lawyers and a court case, and a threat by Network Rail to compulsorily purchase the property so that access could be restored, an injunction finally gave the work gang access.
In the meantime, no trains ran at all: the official passenger numbers for the year 2024/25 were zero. Happily, services have now been restored and the walkers and bird-spotters have returned to the platform. Er, all 172 of them last year.

Why aren't we working 2-days a week?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
17:37:17 UTC
02:37:17 KST

Why aren't we working 2-days a week?
Okay, AI is here to replace us. At this point the dream is that corporations save billions through layoffs, productivity gains and increasing the precarity of the existing workers, while a few people at AI companies profit from the technology and the hype. There are AI tools being baked into every process whether they work or not and the hocus-pocus words of the hours automation, knowledge work, singularity and efficiency.
So why is everyone in white collar jobs still working 40+ hour days as the norm? Why aren't these productivity gains being passed on to the employees who can in turn use it for leisure or whatever it is that they fancy? Rather with the advent of AI, the focus seems to be working more. Jobs are slashed and the existing workers take the work of more people for the same pay and even more precarious conditions.
At some point there needs to be a bigger talk about the societal impact of AI and how it should lead to more free time if not less. The times of factory work and the mandated 5 day work weeks feel archaic in this new paradigm, less commutes, lesser pollution offsetting for the greater energy consumption from the datacenters. Let the rich get rich and the poor have fun, let laziness roost and 21st century be one of rest and relaxation.

DOJ accuses former cyber executive of defrauding ransomware victims by secretly paying hackers
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
17:36:51 UTC
02:36:51 KST

The U.S. Department of Justice has indicted a former executive of a ransomware recovery firm after he allegedly defrauded victims of cyberattacks by claiming the company could get their encrypted files back.
According to federal prosecutors, Juan Ricardo Palacio Escobar, 52, was the chief technology officer at RansomwareHelp, which purported to offer data recovery services to victims of ransomware. The company’s website, which currently reads as “under maintenance,” claimed it could decrypt hacked data by using its proprietary technology, according to an archived copy of the site from earlier this year. RansomwareHelp touted a success rate of “over 99%.”
But the company allegedly had no technical ability to unscramble data and instead “negotiated with and paid a ransom to the ransomware actors to decrypt the victim data,” while charging the ransomware victim more and keeping the difference,” per the Justice Department’s statement.
Palacio Escobar then allegedly used the funds to buy several properties in Florida.
The DOJ said the FBI carried out an undercover operation in June, in part posing as a company that claimed it had been hit by ransomware. The company claimed to the undercover agents that it never negotiated with hackers or paid a ransom. But then Palacio Escobar paid the apparent hackers some $6,000 in cryptocurrency, without the knowledge that FBI agents were also posing as the ransomware hackers.
404 Media first reported the indictment last week against Palacio Escobar. He faces three charges of wire fraud, and could receive 20 years in prison on each count.
Palacio Escobar is the latest, albeit rare, example of cybersecurity professionals going rogue. Earlier this year, two ransomware negotiators working for cybersecurity companies were jailed on hacking and extortion charges, after being caught moonlighting with the ransomware gangs that they were ostensibly investigating. A third ransomware negotiator was also convicted of helping hackers deploy ransomware against several companies.
Last month, independent cybersecurity journalist Brian Krebs reported that FBI agents arrested a Canadian cybersecurity executive in Philadelphia on allegations that he was involved with the theft of FBI personnel records by the hacking and extortion gang ShinyHunters. The hacking group told TechCrunch that they took credit for the theft of FBI data in September, which they said they would not publicly release, a departure from its earlier extortion-based cyberattacks.
The federal charges against the Canadian cybersecurity executive relate to conspiracy to commit extortion, though much of the court docket — and his alleged links to the hacking group — remains under seal.

====================================================================================================

State AGs fight Character.AI’s plan to auction off users’ private chats
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
17:35:58 UTC
02:35:58 KST

What happens to your private conversations when an AI company goes bankrupt? If it’s Character.ai, apparently, the plan is to auction them off to the highest bidder.
Now, a coalition of 42 state attorneys general is intervening in Character.ai’s bankruptcy proceedings in an effort to stop the chatbot maker from selling off sensitive user data, which includes the private conversations of millions of users.
The attorneys general involved in the matter have put out individual press releases, claiming that Character.ai’s dataset includes chat histories from more than 21 million U.S. accounts, more than 2.1 million of which belong to minors. In addition, the sale would involve other personal data, like IP addresses, the statements said.
Character.ai, which filed for Chapter 11 bankruptcy in October, previously told consumers that their personal information would not be sold, the statements also noted. (In a blog post, Character.ai assured its users that their information would still be protected under its privacy policy.)
Only one anonymous bidder for the chat data has been disclosed so far: Smirks and Pangs Acquisition LLC (Smirks), which was set up as a Delaware company on November 13, 2026. The following day, Character.ai picked it as the base bid for the auction, scheduled for December 1, California Attorney General Rob Bonta noted. It is unclear what the buyer’s plans are for the chats.
The AGs are opposing this sale on the grounds that there are no protections for users — whoever buys the data can do as they like, including reselling the data without disclosure, they said. Buyers also won’t have to honor Character.ai’s privacy policy. Plus, Character.ai hasn’t provided the court with as much information as the bidders have been given about any potential safety or legal issues following the sale, the AGs claim.
In addition, some states, including California, Colorado, New York, and Connecticut, have enacted chatbot safety legislation that will go into effect on January 1, 2027, which could trigger further violations if the sale goes through.
“Character.AI promised consumers that their data would not be sold. This kind of behavior is illegal under federal and California law and bankruptcy should not be used to sidestep these consumer protections,” Bonta said in a statement.
Connecticut Attorney General William Tong also criticized Character.ai’s business model.
“Their chatbots drove vulnerable youth down a rabbit hole of emotionally volatile and sexually inappropriate chats,” Tong said. “Their reckless misconduct and broken business model drove their company into the ground. Now they want to abuse the bankruptcy process to do exactly what they said they wouldn’t do — auction off those private chat records and personal details to a shady buyer.”
Tong added: “We objected to the Disney sale, and we’re calling on the bankruptcy court to reject this sale too as yet another guise to eviscerate legal liability and consumer protection.”
The opposition to the Disney sale was led by a similar but not identical coalition of state AGs. They fought against a proposed $20 million licensing deal that would give Disney access to Character.ai’s intellectual property, but not to users’ chats. The deal has not yet received final court approval. (Disney also agreed to hire most of Character.ai’s remaining staff.)
Character.ai suffered after its co-founders, Noam Shazeer and Daniel De Freitas, returned to Google, when the tech giant signed a non-exclusive license to use the AI startup’s tech. The company also faced lawsuits related to child safety — one involved the suicide of a 14-year-old Florida boy whose mother claimed he had become obsessed with a chatbot on the platform.
California AG Bonta is joined in the effort to oppose the sale of Character.ai user data by attorneys general from Alaska, Arkansas, Colorado, Connecticut, the District of Columbia, Delaware, Florida, Georgia, Hawaii, Idaho, Illinois, Indiana, Kansas, Kentucky, Louisiana, Maine, Maryland, Michigan, Minnesota, Mississippi, Missouri, Nevada, New Hampshire, New Jersey, New York, North Carolina, North Dakota, Ohio, Oklahoma, Oregon, Pennsylvania, Rhode Island, South Carolina, South Dakota, Tennessee, Utah, Virginia, Vermont, Washington, Wisconsin, and West Virginia.

====================================================================================================

X’s latest update for creators focuses on vertical video
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-11-25
17:28:29 UTC
02:28:29 KST

X announced new creation tools Wednesday, many of them focused on short-form vertical video. The updates are part of the platform’s effort to compete with social networks such as YouTube and Instagram as the format continues to grow in popularity.
The tools are designed to help creators make professional-quality vertical videos, share original content more easily, and earn more. They also give creators more ways to record, edit, and publish content directly on the platform without relying on third-party apps.
A key addition is an upgraded media editor that lets creators produce polished vertical videos without leaving X. Recording features include the ability to capture video in segments and use green-screen effects, the company said.
The editor also offers a range of postproduction tools. Creators can convert horizontal videos to vertical format, automatically generate captions, trim footage, add text overlays, choose custom thumbnails, and adjust photos before publishing.
Thanks to a recently announced update, users can now add up to 20 photos and videos to a single post, which audiences can swipe through horizontally. X says the feature is intended for sharing event recaps, behind-the-scenes moments, and product launches.
Availability of the new features varies by region, X said.
Earlier this month, the company introduced a clipping feature that lets users take snippets from longer videos, add their own commentary, and post the clips without leaving X. Clips credit and link to the original video, plus the person who creates a clip and the original poster can earn from its views.
Another feature, “React with Video,” launched earlier this year. Similar to TikTok’s “stitch” feature, this lets creators respond to posts with video commentary while displaying the original post in the background.
X has also introduced new earning opportunities and updates to its creator monetization programs in recent months. These include the “Video Kickstart Bonus,” an invitation-only pilot that rewards selected creators for publishing original vertical videos under three minutes long and without watermarks.
The platform has also launched “Subscriber-only Group Chats,” which let creators host private, real-time conversations with paying subscribers. The feature is currently open to creators with 800 or fewer subscribers, and each group chat can accommodate up to 1,000 members.
